Hochschild (Co)-Homology of D-modules on Rigid Analytic Spaces II
Fernando Peña Vázquez

Abstract
. Let X X be a smooth p p -adic Stein space with free tangent sheaf. We use the notion of Hochschild cohomology for sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras developed in our previous work to study the Hochschild cohomology of the algebra of infinite order differential operators 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) \wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X) . In particular, we show that HH ∙ ⁡ ( 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) ) \operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)) is a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces which is quasi-isomorphic to the de Rham complex of X X . We then use this to compare HH ∙ ⁡ ( 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) ) \operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)) with a wide array of Ext functors. Finally, we investigate the relation of HH ∙ ⁡ ( 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) ) \operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)) with the deformation theory of 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) \wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X) . Assuming some finiteness conditions on the de Rham cohomology of X X , we define explicit isomorphisms between HH 1 ⁡ ( 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) ) \operatorname{HH}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)) and the space of bounded outer derivations of 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) \wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X) , and between HH 2 ⁡ ( 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) ) \operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)) and the space of infinitesimal deformations of 𝒟 wideparen X ​ ( X ) \wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X) .



1.
Introduction

Let
K
be a complete non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be the ring of integers of
K
, and let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic
K
-variety. In
[
34
]
we developed a formalism of Hochschild (co)-homology for sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
, putting special emphasis on the sheaf of infinite order differential operators
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. The goal of the present paper is showcasing the relevance of the Hochschild cohomology complex
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
as a tool in
p
-adic geometry. We will do so by showing some applications of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
to the study of smooth rigid analytic spaces, and to the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules attached to them. By construction, the complex

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a geometric invariant of
X
. Hence, as with any geometric invariant, a good starting point is finding a class of spaces for which the invariant is well-behaved. In analogy with the algebraic setting, one could think that the best candidate for such a class would be that of affinoid spaces. However, this is not the correct choice. Indeed,
let
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
be the quasi-abelian category of Ind-Banach spaces over
K
, and let
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
be the category of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces on
X
. Recall that the main result of
loc. cit.
is the existence of the following isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}):=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
be the quasi-abelian category of complete bornological
K
-vector spaces. As a consequence of the previous isomorphism, we obtain the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

(1)

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}):=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}),

which shows that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is intimately related with the de Rham cohomology of
X
. It is precisely for this reason that affinoid spaces are not the class of spaces we are looking for. Namely, for an arbitrary smooth affinoid space, the de Rham cohomology groups can showcase pathological behavior and, in general, they do not give the expected results. For example, let
\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K}=\operatorname{Sp}(K\langle t\rangle)
be the closed unit ball. In analogy with the complex analytic setting, one would expect that
\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K}=\operatorname{Sp}(K\langle t\rangle)
has trivial de Rham cohomology. However, it can be shown that
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K})
is an infinite dimensional
K
-vector space. Even worse, if we regard
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K})
as a topological vector space via the surjection
\Omega_{\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K}/K}(\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K})
, then
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K})
has the trivial topology. This stems from the fact that the de Rham complex
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}(X)
is not strict for an arbitrary smooth affinoid space
X
. Thus, even if affinoid spaces have the properties we are looking for with respect to sheaf cohomology, their analytic properties are not good enough to suit our needs.

Hence, we need to find a class of spaces in which co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules are well-behaved, and such that their de Rham complex has good analytic properties. This role will be played by the smooth Stein spaces. A Stein space is a rigid analytic space
X
admitting an admissible cover:

U_{0}\subset U_{1}\subset\cdots\subset X,

satisfying that each
U_{i}
is an affinoid space, and each map
U_{i}\rightarrow U_{i+1}
is the immersion of a relatively compact Weierstrass subdomain (
cf.
Definition
3.1.1
). The existence of this particular cover has powerful implications in the theory of Stein spaces, both in the analytic and algebraic aspects. As a first example of this, E. Grosse-Klönne showed in
[
20
, Corollary 3.2]
that if
X
is a smooth Stein space, then the de Rham complex:

(2)

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\Omega_{X/K}^{1}(X)\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Omega_{X/K}^{\operatorname{dim}(X)}(X)\rightarrow 0,

is a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces whose cohomology computes the de Rham cohomology of
X
. Hence, we can regard
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)
as a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. Let
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
be the category of bornological
K
-vector spaces, and
LCS_{K}
be the category of locally convex
K
-vector spaces. As shown in
[
8
, Section 5.1]
, there is an adjunction:

(-)^{t}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\leftrightarrows LCS_{K}:(-)^{b},

which sends strict complexes of nuclear Fréchet spaces to strict complexes of complete bornological spaces. Hence, we let
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}
be the associated strict complex of complete bornological spaces.

Thus, smooth Stein spaces satisfy the first of the properties we are looking for. Next, we need to show that the category of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on a smooth Stein space is well-behaved. Namely, let
X
be a smooth Stein space. We need to show that
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules, behaves like the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on an affinoid space. We may condense our main results into the following theorem:

Theorem A
.

Assume
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, and let
X
be a smooth Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. The following hold:

(i)

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is a Fréchet-Stein algebra and a nuclear Fréchet space.

(ii)

Any co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module is a nuclear Fréchet space.

(iii)

There is an equivalence of abelian categories:

\Gamma(X,-):\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

(iv)

Co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules are acyclic for the functor:

\Gamma(X,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

Statements
(i)
and
(ii)
are a part of Theorem
3.2.3
. Statement
(iii)
is shown Proposition
3.2.7
, and
(iv)
is Proposition
3.2.10
.
∎

We point out that our assumptions on
K
are a byproduct of the methods we use to show Theorem
A
, and that it is unknown to us if the theorem holds in greater generality. Combining this theorem with equations (
1
), and (
2
), we can deduce the following:

Theorem B
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein space. We have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})=\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}.

Thus,
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a strict complex, and
\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X)^{b}
is a nuclear Fréchet space for
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

This is Theorem
4.1.2
.
∎

Notice that Theorem
B
holds without our previous assumptions on
K
. As an upshot, if
K
is discretely valued or algebraically closed, we obtain a Künneth formula for the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
. Namely, if
X
and
Y
are smooth Stein spaces, then the following identity holds in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\times Y})=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}).

In particular, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have the following Künneth formula in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\times Y})=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{HH}^{r}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{HH}^{s}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}).

Using the identification from Theorem
B
, we obtain a Künneth formula for the de Rham cohomology of the product of two smooth Stein spaces. See
Corollary
4.1.5
for details.

By now, the reader should be fairly convinced that the category of smooth Stein spaces is the correct one. Indeed, the behavior of the de Rham complex is optimal, and the category of co-admissible modules is completely determined by their global sections. Furthermore, Stein spaces include many interesting spaces, such as analytifications of affine varieties, and Drinfeld’s
p
-adic symmetric spaces.

In the algebraic setting, the fact that quasi-coherent modules on an affine variety have trivial sheaf cohomology can be used to show that the Hochschild cohomology of the sheaf of differential operators agrees with the Hochschild cohomology of its algebra of global sections. That is, if
X
is a smooth affine
K
-variety, then there are quasi-isomorphisms:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{D}_{X}):=R\Gamma(X,R\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}_{X}^{e}}(\mathcal{D}_{X},\mathcal{D}_{X}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X),\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))=:\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)).

This is based upon the fact that, in the algebraic setting, all the relevant algebras are noetherian, so it is possible to construct resolutions by finite-projective modules. We would like to show that this phenomena is also present in the setting of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on
p
-adic Stein spaces.

Let
X
be a smooth
p
-adic Stein space equipped with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. In order to show that the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
can be computed at the global sections, we will make extensive use of the sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras
\wideparen{E}_{X}
. This auxiliary sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X^{2}
was introduced in
[
34
]
, and is
defined via the following formula:

\wideparen{E}_{X}:=p_{1}^{-1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{op}).

The sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras
\wideparen{E}_{X}
is a key ingredient in most of the calculations in
[
34
]
. Its relevance stems from the two following facts: First, the following formula holds in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X^{2},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})),

which allows to calculate Hochschild cohomology via
\wideparen{E}_{X}
. Second, there is a side-switching equivalence for bimodules. That is, there are mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian categories:

\operatorname{S}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}_{X}):\operatorname{S}^{-1}.

Even better, our assumption that the tangent sheaf of
X
is free implies that this adjunction corresponds to the extension-restriction of scalars adjunction of an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras
\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}_{X}
. In particular,
\wideparen{E}_{X}
is locally a Fréchet-Stein algebra, and the side-switching formalism allows us to define a category of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules. Furthermore, as
X^{2}
is a smooth Stein space, the statements of Theorem
A
also hold for co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules.

Even if
\wideparen{E}_{X}
is locally Fréchet-Stein, it is still not a sheaf of noetherian algebras. Hence, it is not clear if
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
admits a strict resolution by finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules. However, we can see that a slightly weaker statement does hold:

Theorem C
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. There is a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that there is a strict exact complex:

0\rightarrow P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0,

where each
P^{i}
is a finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module and
P^{0}=\wideparen{E}_{X}
. Furthermore, if
X
is affinoid or
X
is Stein and
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, then this complex can be used to show that for any co-admissible module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\Gamma(X^{2},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}))=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M})).

Thus, setting
\mathcal{M}=\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, we obtain the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=:\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

This is shown in Proposition
4.3.3
and Theorem
4.3.6
.
∎

We remark that the existence of the étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
is an essential part of the proof, and that it is unknown to us if the result holds without this assumption. This theorem has deep implications for the theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. For instance, it allows us to show the following structural result:

Theorem D
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be an affinoid space and
\mathcal{A}\subset A
be an affine formal model of
A
. Assume
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
admits a free
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{L}
satisfying the following two properties:

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\subset\pi\mathcal{A},\quad[\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}]\subset\pi^{2}\mathcal{L}.

In this situation, the following inequality holds for each
n\geq 0
:

\operatorname{gl.dim.}(\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K})\leq 2\operatorname{dim}(X).

Furthermore, if
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K})
satisfies that it is projective as an Ind-Banach space, then
M
admits a resolution by projective
\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K}
-modules of length
2\operatorname{dim}(X)
.

Proof.

This is Corollary
4.3.4
.
∎

We point that a similar result has been recently shown by A. Bode in
[
9
, Theorem 1.1]
. However, our methods are completely different. Combining Theorem
C
with our previous results, we arrive at the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}.

This identity shows that there is an intimate relation between the geometry of
X
and the algebraic structure of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. Following this line of thought, the next step is showing how the Hochschild cohomology groups can be used to study certain algebraic invariants of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
.

From a historical perspective, one of the most important features of the theory of Hochschild cohomology of associative algebras is its applications in deformation theory. Indeed, let
A
be an associative
K
-algebra, and consider its Hochschild cohomology groups:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A):=R\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}(A,A).

As shown by M. Gerstenhaber in
[
16
]
, the Hochschild cohomology groups of
A
have explicit interpretations in low degrees. For instance, we have the following identities of
K
-vector spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{0}(A)=\operatorname{Z}(A),\quad\operatorname{HH}^{1}(A)=\operatorname{Out}(A),

where
\operatorname{Z}(A)
and
\operatorname{Out}(A)
are the center of
A
, and the space of
K
-linear outer derivations of
A
respectively. Working a bit more, it is possible to obtain interpretations of the Hochschild cohomology groups of
A
in degrees
2
, and
3
. Namely, let us make the following definition:

Definition
.

We define the following concepts:

(i)

An infinitesimal deformation of
A
is a
K[t]/t^{2}
-algebra structure on
A^{\prime}:=A\otimes_{K}K[t]/t^{2}
which is isomorphic to
A
after reducing modulo
t
.

(ii)

Two infinitesimal deformations
B,C
or
A
are isomorphic if there is an isomorphism of
K[t]/t^{2}
-algebras
\varphi:B\rightarrow C
which is the identity modulo
t
.

(iii)

We let
\mathscr{E}xt(A)
be the set of isomorphism classes of infinitesimal extensions of
A
.

Remark
.

Notice that these concepts also make sense for bornological algebras making the obvious modifications, and we will use them in this generality in this introduction.

As shown by Gerstenhaber, the space
\operatorname{HH}^{2}(A)
parametrizes the isomorphism classes of infinitesimal deformations of
A
. Furthermore, the obstructions to lifting an infinitesimal deformation of
A
to a second order deformation (
i.e.

K[t]/t^{3}
-algebra structure on
A\otimes_{K}K[t]/t^{3}
which is isomorphic to
A
after reducing modulo
t
) are parameterized by
\operatorname{HH}^{3}(A)
. In the last part of the paper, we will see how some aspects of this theory can be generalized to the setting of complete bornological algebras.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra. The strategy is establishing a new cohomology theory based upon the following complex of complete bornological spaces:

(3)

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{\delta_{0}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A})\xrightarrow{\delta_{1}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{2}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A})\xrightarrow{\delta_{2}}\cdots\right),

obtained by applying the functor
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(-,\mathscr{A})
to the bar resolution of
\mathscr{A}
(
cf
. Section
6.1
). We remark that this approach was first pursued by J.L Taylor in
[
47
]
(although he works in the category of locally convex spaces). Notice that the complex in (
3
) is just a bornological version of the classical Hochschild cohomology complex of an associative
K
-algebra.

Our main motivation for studying the complex
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}

is that, unlike our previous definition of Hochschild cohomology,
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
has an explicit description as a chain complex, and we know the formulas of the differentials. Hence, we can perform explicit calculations with
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
. In order to preserve this explicit nature, we will make use of the canonical inclusion functor:

J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}.

This is due to the fact that the structure of cokernels in
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
is simpler. See Section
2.3
for a more complete treatment of the functor
J
, and the discussion below Definition
6.1.1

for a description of the cokernels in
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. Let us now define the TH cohomology groups of
\mathscr{A}
:

Theorem E
.

The
TH
cohomology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
are the following bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{A})=\operatorname{Coker}\left(J(\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{n-1})\rightarrow J(\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}))\right),

The
TH
cohomology spaces satisfy the following properties:

(i)

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\mathscr{A})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A})
.

(ii)

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\mathscr{A})=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A}):=\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\mathscr{A})/\underline{\operatorname{Inn}}(\mathscr{A})
.

(iii)

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\mathscr{A})=\mathscr{E}xt(\mathscr{A})
.

Proof.

Statements
(i)
and
(ii)
are shown in Proposition
6.1.4
. Statement
(iii)
is Theorem
6.1.8
.
∎

If
\mathscr{A}
is a Fréchet
K
-algebra, then it is possible to construct a first quadrant spectral sequence in the left heart
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
connecting these two cohomology theories (
cf.
Theorem
6.2.1
). Evaluating this sequence in the special case
\mathscr{A}=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, we obtain the following theorem:

Theorem F
.

Assume
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, and let
X
be a smooth Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. Then the following hold:

(i)

There are canonical isomorphisms of complete bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{0}(X)^{b}=\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

(ii)

There are isomorphisms of complete bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Furthermore, we can construct an explicit isomorphism:

c_{1}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

(iii)

Assume
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)^{b}
is finite-dimensional. Then there is an explicit isomorphism:

c_{2}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}xt(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

Statement
(i)
is Proposition
6.3.1
. The first part of
(ii)
is shown in Proposition
6.3.2
, and the construction of
c_{1}
is done in Proposition
6.3.14
. Statement
(iii)
is Theorem
6.3.11
.
∎

We remark that the results we obtain in the body of the text are slightly more general than the version presented here. In particular, the map
c_{2}
can be defined even if the finiteness condition on de Rham cohomology fails. One can check Chapter
6
for the specific statements.

Hence, at least in some situations, we have completely clarified the structure of the infinitesimal deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. We expect that the methods of this paper can be generalized to hold even when the de Rham cohomology is not finite (
cf.
the discussion below Corollary
6.3.13
), and that these techniques open the way for new advances in deformation theory of rigid analytic spaces. For instance, a theory of formal deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
could be within reach. On the other hand we also expect that similar results hold in the
G
-equivariant setting, where
G
is a finite group acting on our smooth Stein space
X
. In analogy with the algebraic situation, we hope that the
p
-adic Cherednik algebras defined in
[
35
]
can be used to classify all infinitesimal deformations of
G\ltimes\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, in the same way that algebras of twisted differential operators classify the deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
(
cf
. Definition
6.3.6
and the discussion above). Although similar in nature, the
G
-equivariant case presents some technical challenges, so we will postpone this study to a future paper.

Although the focus of most of this paper is centered around the Hochschild cohomology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
and their deformation-theoretic applications, we will also see that many of the calculations above can also be performed for the Hochschild homology groups. In fact, the results for Hochschild homology can be deduced from those of Hochschild cohomology. This is due to the fact that the Hochschild (co)-homology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
satisfies
Van den Bergh duality:

Theorem G
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. For any
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X^{2},\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathscr{M})=\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Furthermore, if
X
is affinoid or
X
is Stein and
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, then for any
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})
there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{H}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Proof.

This is Theorem
5.2.2
.
∎

This can be seen as a version of Poincaré duality for Hochschild (co)-homology. Notice in particular that Theorem

G
does not impose any finiteness assumptions on
\mathscr{M}
. In particular, we do not require that
\mathscr{M}
is a
\mathcal{C}
-complex. As a corollary of this theorem, it follows that the Theorem
C

holds for Hochschild homology
mutatis mutandis
.

Although the Hochschild homology groups do not have a direct deformation-theoretic meaning, they offer the advantage of being explicitly computable. In particular, we will see in the body of the text that
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
can be represented by a complex:

\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\xrightarrow{\delta^{-2}}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{2}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\xrightarrow{\delta^{-1}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\xrightarrow{\delta^{0}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\right),

with differentials given by explicit formulas. In fact,
\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
is an analytic version of the usual Hochschild homology complex of an associative algebra. An upshot of this is that it allows us to define analytic versions of some of the well-known operations in Hochschild homology, and then we can use the Van den Bergh isomorphism to obtain the corresponding operations on Hochschild cohomology. For instance, we will construct analytic versions of the Connes differential, the Batalin-Vilkovisky operator, the cup product, and the Gerstenhaber bracket.

Related work

This paper is a part of a series of papers
[
34
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
36
]
, in which we start a study of the deformation theory of the algebras
G\ltimes\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, where
G
is a finite group acting on a smooth Stein space
X
. In particular, in
[
34
]
we develop a formalism of Hochschild cohomology and homology for
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules on smooth and separated rigid analytic spaces, and give explicit calculations of the Hochschild cohomology groups
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
in terms of the de Rham complex of
X
. This is done in the setting of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces and quasi-abelian categories, as showcased in
[
8
]
.

In
[
35
]
we develop a theory of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras on smooth rigid analytic spaces with a finite group action. These algebras can be regarded as a
p
-adic analytic version of the sheaves of Cherednik algebras constructed by P. Etingof in
[
14
]
. In particular, let
X
be a smooth rigid analytic space with an action of a finite group
G
satisfying some mild technical conditions. The family of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras associated to the action of
G
on
X
is a family of sheaves of complete bornological algebras on the quotient
X/G
which generalizes the skew group algebra
G\ltimes\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. In particular, given a
p
-adic Cherednik algebra
\mathcal{H}_{t,c,\omega}
, its restriction to the smooth locus of
X/G
is canonically isomorphic to
G\ltimes\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Furthermore, all
p
-adic Cherednik algebras are locally Fréchet-Stein, and their isomorphism classes are parameterized by certain geometric invariants associated to the action of
G
on
X
.

In the algebraic setting, this parameter space has a deep deformation-theoretic meaning. Namely, if
X
is a smooth affine
K
-variety with an action of a finite group
G
, then it can be shown that the family of Cherednik algebras associated to the action of
G
on
X
parametrizes all the infinitesimal deformations of the skew-group algebra
G\ltimes\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
. We hope that our methods can be extended to show that this phenomena is also present in the rigid analytic setting. The paper
[
35
]
is devoted to laying the foundations of the theory of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras. In particular, we provide a construction of these algebras, study their algebraic properties, and obtain a classification theorem.

The study of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras is continued in
[
36
]
,
where we study the representation theory of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras, focusing on the case where
X
is the analytification of an affine space, and
G
acts linearly on
X
. The algebras arising from such an action are called
p
-adic rational Cherednik algebras, and their representation theory shares many features with that of the
p
-adic Arens-Michael envelope
\wideparen{U}(\mathfrak{g})
of a finite dimensional reductive Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
studied in
[
42
]
. In particular, to any
p
-adic rational Cherednik algebra one can attach a category
\wideparen{\mathcal{O}}_{c}
, which is a highest weight category whose simple objects are in bijection with the irreducible representations of
G
.

Structure of the paper

We will now give an overview of the contents of the paper:

In Chapter
2
we provide the reader with some basic material needed for the rest of the paper. Namely, we devote Sections
2.1
and
2.2
to give a brief remainder on the closed symmetric monoidal structures on
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, Section
2.3
to give a brief outline of the main results of
[
34
]
, and Section
2.4
to define the sheaves of external homomorphisms.

In Chapter
3
we introduce the notion of Stein space, and show some of their most important properties. In particular, basic results on the geometry of Stein spaces are explained in Section
3.1
, and used in Section
3.2
to study the properties of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules on smooth Stein spaces.

In Chapter
4
we start giving some applications of Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on a smooth Stein space. In Section
4.1
we establish a comparison theorem with de Rham cohomology, and use it to show that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. This is then used to show a Künneth formula for Hochschild cohomology. In Sections
4.2
and
4.3
, we compare the complex
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
with different
Ext
functors, allowing us to show that the Hochschild cohomology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
parameterize certain Yoneda extensions. We also show the important formula:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

showing that the Hochschild cohomology can be calculated at the level of global sections. Finally, we show in Section
4.4
that Hochschild cohomology computes Lie-Rinehart cohomology.

In Chapter
5
we will show Van den Bergh duality. We start with Section
5.1
, where we review the notion of a weakly holonomic
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module and show some properties regarding direct image and extraordinary direct image along a closed immersion. We finish with Section
5.2
where we show Theorem
G
, and Section
5.3
, where we show an analog of Theorem
C
for Hochschild homology.

We finish the paper with Chapter
6
, where we study the deformation theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. In particular, we use Section
6.1
to give an alternative definition of the Hochschild (co)-homology of a complete bornological algebra in terms of the bar resolution, and give explicit interpretations of the associated cohomology groups in low degrees. In Section
6.2
we establish a spectral sequence connecting this new definition with the one we have been using so far. Next, we devote Sections
6.3
and
6.4
and to study the particular case of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, and use our previous results to obtain explicit calculations. Finally, we will dedicate Section
6.5
to defining some operations on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
and
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
, and Sections
6.6
and
6.7
to define the module of non-commutative differential forms and use it to obtain some upgrades of the computations from Section
6.3
.

Notation and conventions

For the rest of this text,
K
denotes a complete non-archimedean
field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. We let
\mathcal{R}
be its valuation ring,
k
be its residue field, and fix
a pseudo-uniformizer
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
with
0<|\pi|<1
. If
A
is a Banach
K
-algebra, we let
A^{\circ}
be the
\mathcal{R}
-algebra of power-bounded elements in
A
, and let
A^{\circ\circ}
be the ideal of
A^{\circ}
given by the topologically nilpotent elements in
A
. Notice that, if
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, every affinoid
K
-algebra
A
satisfies that
A^{\circ}

is an affine formal model of
A
(
cf.

[
10
, Corollary 6.4.1.5/6]
). We will use this fact in the sequel without further mention. All rigid analytic
K
-varieties are assumed to be quasi-separated and quasi-paracompact. For a
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we will often times write
\mathcal{M}_{K}=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}K
. If
A=\varprojlim_{n}A_{n}
is a Fréchet-Stein algebra, we let
\mathcal{C}(A)
denote its category of co-admissible modules.

Throughout the text, we will make extensive use of the theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on smooth rigid analytic spaces, as developed in
[
3
]
, and of the homological machinery of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras from
[
8
]
. In order to keep the preliminaries to a minimum, we will not give an introduction to the theory of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules here. All the necessary material is available in the background sections of our companion paper
[
34
]
, as well as in the original reference
[
3
]
. In order to avoid set-theoretic issues, we will assume that we are working within a fixed Grothendieck universe.

2.
Background

We will devote this chapter to laying some of the foundational material needed for the rest of the paper. In particular, we will give a brief summary of the contents of
[
34
]
, and introduce the sheaf of external
Hom
functors associated to a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on a rigid analytic space.

2.1.
Generalities on
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}

For the benefit of the reader, and in order to fix some notation, we will now give a brief outline of the properties of the categories of Ind-Banach and (complete) bornological spaces. In order to keep the preliminaries to a minimum, we will not include any proofs. A complete discussion on these topics can be found in Chapters 2 and 3 of our companion paper
[
34
]
, and the references given therein. Let us start by giving some definitions:

Definition 2.1.1
.

A bornology on a
K
-vector space
V
is a family
\mathcal{B}
of subsets
of
V
such that:

(i)

If
B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}
and
B_{2}\subset B_{1}
, then
B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}
.

(ii)

\{v\}\in\mathcal{B}
for all
v\in V
.

(iii)

\mathcal{B}
is closed under taking finite unions.

(iv)

If
B\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\lambda\cdot B\in\mathcal{B}
for all
\lambda\in K
.

(v)

Let
B\in\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{R}\cdot B
be the
\mathcal{R}
-module spawned by
B
. Then
\mathcal{R}\cdot B\in\mathcal{B}
.

We call the pair
(V,\mathcal{B})
a bornological space, and the elements of
\mathcal{B}
bounded subsets. We refer to the pair
(V,\mathcal{B})
as
V
. A
K
-linear map of bornological spaces
f:V\rightarrow W
is bounded if it maps bounded subsets to bounded subsets. We let
\mathcal{B}c_{K}

be the category of bornological spaces and bounded maps.

Before giving further details on the properties of
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
, let us motivate the definition. First, let us point out that, when dealing with problems in non-archimedean functional analysis, the most commonly used category is the category of locally convex spaces over
K
, which we denote
LCS_{K}
. This category has been thoroughly studied in previous years, and there are plenty of texts detailing its properties. See, for example, P. Schneider’s monograph
[
44
]
. The main problem that arises in
LCS_{K}
is that the (projective) tensor product of locally convex spaces and the space of continuous maps with the convergence on bounded subsets topology do not endow
LCS_{K}
with a closed symmetric monoidal structure. This pathological behavior makes it very hard to study categories of modules in
LCS_{K}
. It is for this reason that bornological spaces need to be brought into the picture. Nonetheless, we would like to point out that there is an adjunction:

(-)^{t}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\leftrightarrows LCS_{K}:(-)^{b}.

The functor
(-)^{b}
is called the bornologification functor, and it is fully faithful on metrizable locally convex spaces. See
[
24
, pp. 93, Proposition 3]
for a complete discussion on this matter.

Back to our setting, let
V\in\mathcal{B}c_{K}
be a bornological space. It can be shown that
V
can be expressed as a filtered colimit in
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
:

V=\varinjlim_{i\in I}V_{i},

satisfying that each
V_{i}
is a normed space. We say that
V
is a complete bornological space if all the
V_{i}
in the above filtered colimit can be taken to be Banach spaces.

Definition 2.1.2
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
given by the complete bornological spaces.

We point out that there is an adjunction:

J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{B}c_{K}:\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}},

where
J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
is the inclusion, and
\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is the completion functor. This adjunction will be further explored in Section
2.3
. Let us now describe the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
: Consider a pair of bornological spaces
V,W\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. We may regard the
K
-vector space
V\otimes_{K}W
as a bornological space with respect to the bornology induced by the family of subsets:

\{B_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}B_{2}\textnormal{ }|\textnormal{ }B_{1}\subset V,B_{2}\subset W\textnormal{ are bounded }\mathcal{R}\textnormal{-modules}\}.

This is called the (projective) bornological tensor product of
V
and
W
. We define
V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W
as the completion of
V\otimes_{K}W
with respect to this bornology. On the other hand, the
K
-vector space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
has a natural bornology with bounded subsets:

\{U\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)\textnormal{ }|\textnormal{ for all bounded }B\subset V,\textnormal{ }\cup_{f\in U}f(B)\textnormal{ is bounded in }W\}.

We let
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
denote
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
equipped with this bornology. We remark that the space
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
is complete with respect to this bornology whenever
W
is complete.

Proposition 2.1.3
.

The functors
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}
make
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
a closed symmetric monoidal category. In particular, for
U,V,W\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, we have an adjunction:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(U\widehat{\otimes}_{K}V,W)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(U,\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)).

The unit of the closed symmetric monoidal structure of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is
K
.

Proof.

This is
[
8
, Theorem 4.10]
.
∎

Similarly,
\otimes_{K}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathcal{B}c_{K}}
give a closed symmetric monoidal structure to
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
. The next category of interest is the category of Ind-Banach spaces. Let us start by giving the definition:

Definition 2.1.4
.

We define the category
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
of Ind-Banach spaces as the category with objects given by functors
V:I\rightarrow\operatorname{Ban}_{K}
, where
I
is a small filtered category. We call these objects the Ind-Banach spaces. For simplicity, we will write Ind-Banach spaces as formal direct limits:

``\varinjlim"V_{i}.

Morphisms in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
are defined as follows: Given two objects
``\varinjlim"V_{i}
,
``\varinjlim"W_{j}
, we set:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(``\varinjlim"V_{i},``\varinjlim"W_{j})=\varprojlim_{i}\varinjlim_{j}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ban}_{K}}(V_{i},W_{j}).

As before,
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
has a canonical closed symmetric monoidal structure. Let us describe the corresponding functors: Let
``\varinjlim"V_{i}
, and
``\varinjlim"W_{j}
be two Ind-Banach spaces. We define the tensor product of Ind-Banach spaces as follows:

``\varinjlim"V_{i}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}``\varinjlim"W_{j}=``\varinjlim"V_{i}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W_{j},

where
V_{i}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W_{j}
denotes the complete projective tensor product
of Banach spaces (
cf
. Similarly, we define an inner hom functor via the following formula:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(``\varinjlim"V_{i},``\varinjlim"W_{j})=\varprojlim_{i}``\varinjlim_{j}"\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ban}_{K}}(V_{i},W_{j}),

where
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ban}_{K}}(V_{i},W_{j})
represents the Banach space of continuous linear operators with the operator norm (
cf
.
[
44
, Section 6]
). Similar to the case of complete bornological spaces, we have the following:

Proposition 2.1.5
.

The functors
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}
make
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
into a closed symmetric monoidal category. For
U,V,W\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, we have an adjunction:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(U\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V,W)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(U,\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V,W)).

We point out that the unit of the closed symmetric monoidal structure of
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is
K
.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
8
, Theorem 4.10]
.
∎

As usual, we can use these closed symmetric monoidal structures to define monoids. In particular, a monoid in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is a complete bornological space
\mathscr{A}\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, together with a pair of morphisms:

K\rightarrow\mathscr{A},\,\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A},

satisfying the usual axioms of an algebra. We will call monoids in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
complete bornological algebras. Given a complete bornological algebra
\mathscr{A}
, an
\mathscr{A}
-module is a complete bornological space
\mathcal{M}
, together with a bounded map:

\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M},

satisfying the usual axioms of a module. We define
\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A})
as the category of all
\mathscr{A}
-modules with
\mathscr{A}
-linear maps. There are also
\mathscr{A}
-module versions of the tensor product and inner homomorphism functor, which satisfy the usual adjunctions (
cf.

[
34
, Sections 2.3/2.5]
). We will denote the tensor product of
\mathscr{A}
-modules by:

-\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K},

and the inner homomorphism functor of
\mathscr{A}
-modules by:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A})\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}.

We will give an explicit description of these operations in the following section.
Let us remark that the constructions so far make sense in any closed symmetric monoidal category. In particular, there are also monoids in
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, which we call the bornological algebras and Ind-Banach algebras respectively. Given an Ind-Banach algebra
\mathscr{B}
, we denote its category of modules by
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B})
, the tensor product of
\mathscr{B}
-modules by
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{B}}
, and the inner homomorphism functor by
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{B}}(-,-)
. We follow the analogous convention for bornological algebras.

The most attractive feature of these categories is that they have a canonical quasi-abelian category structure (
cf
.
[
34
, Section 2.1]
). In practice, this means that, although they are not abelian, they are still good enough from the perspective of homological algebra. Indeed, let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with kernels and cokernels. We say a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{C}
is strict if the induced map:

\operatorname{CoIm}(f)\rightarrow\operatorname{Im}(f),

is an isomorphism. A quasi-abelian category
\mathcal{C}
is an additive category with kernels and
cokernels such that strict epimorphisms are stable under pullback, and strict monomorphisms are stable under pushout. In these conditions, we can define the derived category
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
as the localization of the homotopy category
\operatorname{K}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
at the full triangulated subcategory given by the strict exact complexes.
Furthermore, if
\mathcal{C}
is a quasi-abelian category, then there is an abelian category
LH(\mathcal{C})
, which we call the left heart of
\mathcal{C}
, equipped with a fully faithful functor
I:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{C})
satisfying that
I
admits a right adjoint
C:LH(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, and induces an equivalence of triangulated categories:

I:\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(LH(\mathcal{C})),

where
\operatorname{D}(LH(\mathcal{C}))
denotes the classical (unbounded) derived category of an abelian category.

Proposition 2.1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
or
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, and let
\mathscr{A}
be a monoid in
\mathcal{C}
. Then
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{A})
is a quasi-abelian category. Furthermore,
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category.

Proof.

See
[
34
, Section 2.4]
.
∎

The idea now is extending the closed symmetric monoidal structures to the left hearts. We will focus on the cases of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, which are the most relevant to our setting:

Proposition 2.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
or
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. The closed symmetric monoidal structure in
\mathcal{C}
extends uniquely to a closed symmetric monoidal structure on
LH(\mathcal{C})
. We denote the corresponding functors by
\widetilde{\otimes}_{LH(\mathcal{C})}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\mathcal{C})}
. Furthermore, the functor
I:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{C})
is lax symmetric monoidal, and we have the following identification of functors:

I(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,-))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\mathcal{C})}(I(-),I(-)).

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, then the functor
I:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
is strong symmetric monoidal. That is, there is an identification of functors:

I(-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-)=I(-)\widetilde{\otimes}_{LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))}I(-).

Proof.

This is shown in
[
8
, Section 3.3]
.
∎

The categories
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
are related via the following proposition:

Proposition 2.1.8
.

There is an adjunction of quasi-abelian categories:

\widehat{L}:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\leftrightarrows\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}:\operatorname{diss}(-).

We call
\operatorname{diss}(-)
, the dissection functor. This adjunction satisfies the identity:
\widehat{L}\circ\operatorname{diss}(-)=\operatorname{Id}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}
, and induces mutually inverse equivalences of abelian categories:

\widetilde{L}:LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\leftrightarrows LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):\widetilde{\operatorname{diss}}(-).

Furthermore,
\widetilde{L}
and
\widetilde{\operatorname{diss}}(-)
are strong symmetric monoidal functors.

Proof.

See
[
37
, Proposition 5.15]
, and
[
37
, Proposition 5.16.]
.
∎

In order to simplify the notation, we will denote the functors of the closed symmetric monoidal structure of
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
obtained in Proposition
2.1.7
by
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-)
.

For some technical reasons regarding exactness of filtered colimits, it is often times better to work in the category
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. For instance, given a rigid space
X
, the category of sheaves
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is not necessarily a quasi-abelian category, but
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
is always a quasi-abelian category. However,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is a much more explicit category. Furthermore, objects in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
have an underlying
K
-vector space, and this makes some of the constructions simpler. Thus, we will use this proposition to use whichever category is better for our purposes. Furthermore, as we are interested in algebras of infinite order differential operators, most of the algebras we will deal with are (bornologifications of) Fréchet algebras. In this setting, the results above are even stronger:

Proposition 2.1.9
.

Let
V,W
be (bornologifications of) metrizable locally convex spaces. Then there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
:

\operatorname{diss}(V)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{diss}(W)\rightarrow\operatorname{diss}(V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W).

In particular, if
\mathscr{A}
is the bornologification of a Fréchet K-algebra, then the dissection functor induces an exact and fully faithful functor:

\operatorname{diss}_{A}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})).

Furthermore, this yields an equivalence of
abelian categories:

LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}))\cong LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})))\cong\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})).

Proof.

This is
[
8
, Proposition 4.25]
and
[
8
,
Proposition 4.3]
.
∎

2.2.
Sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces

Let
X
be a rigid space, and let
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
be the category of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces on
X
. As mentioned above, this is a quasi-abelian category. Furthermore, we have an identification of abelian categories:

LH(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))=\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))=\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})).

The functor
I:\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is defined for every sheaf of Ind-Banach spaces
\mathcal{F}
and every open subspace
U\subset X
by the rule:

I(\mathcal{F})(U)=I(\mathcal{F}(U)).

As shown in Proposition
2.1.6
,
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is a Grothendieck category. Hence,
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is also a Grothendieck category. In particular, it has enough injectives and exact filtered colimits.

The closed symmetric monoidal structure of
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
extends naturally to
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. Namely, for
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, we let
\mathcal{F}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{G}
be the sheafification of the following presheaf:

U\mapsto\mathcal{F}(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{G}(U).

In this way, we obtain a tensor product of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces:

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\times\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})),

which can be shown to be exact. Analogously, there is a tensor product:

-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\times\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})),

and it follows by Proposition
2.1.7
that
I(-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-)=I(-)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(-)
.

We now construct the inner homomorphism functor. For
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, the sheaf of inner homomorphisms
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
is defined by setting for every admissible open
U\subset X
:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})(U)=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\prod_{V\subset U}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(V),\mathcal{G}(V))\rightrightarrows\prod_{W\subset V}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(V),\mathcal{G}(W))\right).

The maps in the equalizer are the following: For admissible open
subspaces
W\subset V\subset U
, a homomorphism
f\in\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(V),\mathcal{G}(V))
is mapped to the composition:

\mathcal{F}(V)\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{G}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(W).

Similarly, every
g\in\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(W),\mathcal{G}(W))
is mapped to:

\mathcal{F}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(W)\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{G}(W).

We may define
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-)
analogously, and the fact that
I
commutes with equalizers, together with Proposition
2.1.7
show that
I(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-))=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(-),I(-))
.

As above, the fact that
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
is a closed symmetric monoidal category allows us to define monoids in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, which we call sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
. Given a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras
\mathscr{A}
, we let
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
be the sheaf of Ind-Banach
\mathscr{A}
-modules. Analogously, if
\mathscr{B}
is a monoid in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, we let
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\mathscr{B})
be its category of modules. We may condense the main properties of these categories into the following proposition:

Proposition 2.2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
. The following hold:

(i)

\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
is a quasi-abelian category.

(ii)

There is a canonical isomorphism of abelian categories:

LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))\cong\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})).

(iii)

\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category.

Proof.

See
[
8
, Proposition 2.6]
and
[
8
, Lemma 3.7]
for details.
∎

Fix a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras
\mathscr{A}
on
X
. There are relative versions of the tensor product and sheaf of inner homomorphisms for
\mathscr{A}
-modules. The tensor product of sheaves of Ind-Banach
\mathscr{A}
-modules, which we will denote by:

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})),

is defined for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
by the formula:

\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N}:=\operatorname{Coeq}\left(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\right),

where the maps in the coequalizer are given by the maps:

T_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{M},\quad T_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{N},

induced by the action of
\mathscr{A}
on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
respectively. The inner homomorphism functor for
\mathscr{A}
-modules is denoted by the following expression:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})),

and is
defined for
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
by the following equalizer:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\rightrightarrows\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\right).

The first map in the equalizer is obtained by applying
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,\mathcal{N})
to the action:

\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M},

and the second is obtained by applying
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},-)
to the canonical morphism:

\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{N}).

Again, these constructions make sense in any additive closed symmetric monoidal category. Hence, there are relative tensor products and sheaves of inner homomorphisms for modules over monoids in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, and we will use analogous naming conventions. Furthermore, we have the following identification of functors:

(4)

I(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-))=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(-),I(-)).

As mentioned above, this paper is devoted to the study of Hochschild cohomology. Thus, we will be interested in the derived functors of
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
. Now, the theory of derived functors in quasi-abelian categories is rather involved. In particular, the category
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
will almost never have enough injective objects, so it is difficult to formulate a good theory of derived functors. However, as seen in Proposition
2.2.1
, its left heart
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category. Thus, it has enough injective objects, and left exact functors admit (unbounded) derived functors. Hence, we will use equation (
4
), together with the identification of triangulated categories:

\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}):=\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})))\xrightarrow{I}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})))=:\operatorname{D}(I(\mathscr{A}))

to make the following definition:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(-),I(-)):\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\times\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))).

We remark that if
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
has a derived functor, then the identification of triangulated categories
I:D(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(I(\mathscr{A}))
and equation (
4
) show that it must agree with
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(-),I(-))
. Hence, there really is no loss in making this identification, and we will follow the same convention for the global sections functor
\Gamma(X,-)
. A more detailed discussion of these topics can be found in
[
8
, Section 3.6]
, as well as in our companion paper
[
34
, Section 3.3]
.

2.3.
Hochschild (co)-homology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules

We will now provide an overview of the contents of
[
34
]
. Let
X
be a rigid space, and
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
. The sheaf of enveloping algebras of
\mathscr{A}
is the following sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras:

\mathscr{A}^{e}=\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}.

Let us now introduce the definition of the Hochschild cohomology of
\mathscr{A}
:

Definition 2.3.1

(
[
34
, Definition 5.4.4]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
and consider a complex
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
. We define the following objects:

(i)

The inner Hochschild cohomology complex of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))):

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}).

The
n
-th Hochschild cohomology sheaf of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following sheaf:

\mathcal{HH}^{n}(\mathscr{A}):=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\right).

(ii)

The Hochschild cohomology complex of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following object in the derived category
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})).

The
n
-th Hochschild cohomology group of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following object:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\right).

If the choice of
\mathscr{A}
is clear, we will sometimes drop it from the notation and simply write
\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{M})
, and
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{M})
. If
\mathscr{\mathscr{M}}=\mathscr{A}
, we will call
\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A})
the inner Hochschild cohomology complex of
\mathscr{A}
, and
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A})
the Hochschild cohomology complex of
\mathscr{A}
. Notice that if
X=\operatorname{Sp}(K)
then we have the following canonical identifications:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{\mathscr{M}})=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{\mathscr{M}})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{\mathscr{M}}),

and we call this complex the Hochschild cohomology complex of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{\mathscr{M}}
.

Similarly, we can make the following definition:

Definition 2.3.2

(
[
34
, Definition 5.4.7]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
, and consider a complex
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
. We define the following objects:

(i)

The inner Hochschild cohomology complex of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))):

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}\mathscr{M}.

The
n
-th Hochschild homology sheaf of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following sheaf:

\mathcal{HH}_{n}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}):=\operatorname{H}^{-n}\left(\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\right).

(ii)

The Hochschild homology complex of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following complex in the derived category
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})).

The
n
-th Hochschild homology group of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following object:

\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{H}^{-n}\left(\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\right).

We fill follow the analogous notational conventions as with Hochschild cohomology.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space. Most of the contents of
[
34
]
are aimed at calculating the inner Hochschild cohomology complex of the sheaf of infinite order differential operators
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. These efforts can be summarized into the following theorem:

Theorem 2.3.3

(
[
34
, Theorem 8.1.6]
)
.

There is an isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))):

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}.

In particular, we have
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})
as objects in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
.

Notice that the above expression does not mean that

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
agrees with the de Rham cohomology of
X
. Indeed, the previous theorem shows that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
agrees with the hypercohomology of
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
viewed as an object in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
. In particular, the cohomology groups of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
are objects in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
, and it is not even clear that they are contained in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
.

On the other hand, the de Rham cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
is defined as the hypercohomology of
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
viewed as a complex of sheaves of
K
-vector spaces. Hence, the de Rham cohomology groups of
X
are
K
-vector spaces. This discussion shows that these two notions are different. However, the similarity in the formulas suggest that both cohomologies should be related. In order to understand this relation, we will need some technical results from
[
34
, Section 8.1]
.

First, recall that there is an adjunction:

J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{B}c_{K}:\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}},

where
J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
denotes the inclusion, and
\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
denotes the completion of bornological spaces. This extends to an adjunction between the associated left hearts:

J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\leftrightarrows LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}):\operatorname{H}^{0}(L\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}).

Furthermore, the functor
J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})

is exact, and the previous adjunction showcases that
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is a reflexive abelian subcategory of
LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
. Similarly, we have a forgetful functor:

\operatorname{Forget}(-):\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

By construction of the quasi-abelian structure on
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
, this functor is strongly exact. In particular, it preserves arbitrary kernels and cokernels. As
\operatorname{Vect}_{K}
is an abelian category, it is canonically isomorphic to its left heart. Thus, we get a unique extension:

\widetilde{\operatorname{Forget}}(-):LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})=\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

The fact that
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
is strongly exact implies that
\widetilde{\operatorname{Forget}}(-)
is exact. Hence, in order to simplify notation, we will denote
\widetilde{\operatorname{Forget}}(-)
simply by
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
. In
[
34
]
, we discussed an extension of these functors to the corresponding categories of sheaves. In particular, we have the following definition:

Definition 2.3.4

(
[
34
, Definition 8.1.8]
)
.

We define the following functor:

J:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})),\quad\mathcal{F}\mapsto J(\mathcal{F}),

where
J(\mathcal{F})
satisfies
J(\mathcal{F})(U)=J(\mathcal{F}(U))
for every admissible open
U\subset X
. Analogously, we define:

\operatorname{Forget}(-):\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}),\quad\mathcal{G}\mapsto\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G}),

where
\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G})
satisfies
\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G})(U)=\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G}(U))
for every admissible open
U\subset X
.

Notice that these functors are well defined because both
J
and
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
are exact. Furthermore, by
[
34
, Lemma 8.1.10]
, these functors are in fact exact. This can be used to show the following:

Proposition 2.3.5

(
[
34
, Corollary 8.1.12]
)
.

We have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)=\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})).

Besides this result, the functors
J
and
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
will also be used in Chapter
4
, as a tool to compare Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
with different types of Hom functors.

Before moving on, let us recall one of the most important technical tools of
[
34
]
. Namely, the sheaf of complete bi-enveloping algebras of
X
. This sheaf will be of the utmost importance in Section
4.3
. Let
X^{2}=X\times_{\operatorname{Sp}(K)}X
, and denote the projections by
p_{i}:X^{2}\rightarrow X
, for
i=1,2
. We define the sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras of
X
as the following sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X^{2}
:

\wideparen{E}_{X}:=p_{1}^{-1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}).

Our interest in
\wideparen{E}_{X}
stems from the following properties:

Theorem 2.3.6

(
[
34
, Theorem A]
)
.

The sheaf
\wideparen{E}_{X}
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{E}_{X}=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
is free, there is an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras:

\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}_{X}.

(iii)

There are mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian categories:

\operatorname{S}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}_{X}):\operatorname{S}^{-1},

which we call the side-switching equivalence for bimodules.

If the conditions of
(iii)
hold, then
\operatorname{S}
agrees with the extension of scalars along
\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}
.

Statement
(i)
in the preceding theorem allows us to regard the derived category
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
as a full subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
, which is in turn equivalent to
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})
by
(iii)
. Roughly speaking, this implies that we can reduce problems about
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-modules to problems about
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules, and then use the side-switching equivalence for bimodules to treat the problem in the category of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-modules. Finally, let us also point out that statement
(ii)
can be used to define a category of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules, which we denote
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
, and that the side-switching equivalence for bimodules restricts to an equivalence:

\operatorname{S}:\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X}):\operatorname{S}^{-1}.

In particular, the contents of Section
3.2
will also hold in the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules.

2.4.
The external sheaf of homomorphisms

In most of our previous developments, we have worked with the inner
Hom
functors,
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(-,-)
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(-,-)
. These are obtained from the closed symmetric monoidal structures on
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
respectively. However, we can use the fact that
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
and
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
are additive categories to obtain a different notion of
Hom
functors, which only take into account the additive (in fact
K
-linear) structures on the aforementioned categories. Let us start with the following definition:

Definition 2.4.1
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is said to be
K
-linear, if there is a factorization of its
Hom
functor:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,-):\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

Let
``\varinjlim"V_{i},"\varinjlim"W_{j}\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
be a pair of Ind-Banach spaces. By definition, morphisms in Ind-Ban are given by the following expression:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(``\varinjlim"V_{i},``\varinjlim"W_{j})=\varprojlim_{i}\varinjlim_{j}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ban}_{K}}(V_{i},W_{j}),

which is clearly a
K
-vector space. In particular, we have a well-defined
K
-linear structure:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-):\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

Similarly, let
\mathscr{A}
be an Ind-Banach algebra, and
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
be a pair of Ind-Banach
\mathscr{A}
-modules. By definition, we have the following identity:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}):=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\rightrightarrows\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\right),

which shows that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
is canonically a
K
-vector space. Again, this allows us to obtain a canonical
K
-linear structure on
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

The idea is extending this structure to the left heart
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))
:

Lemma 2.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a
K
-linear quasi-abelian category with enough projectives. Then
LH(\mathcal{E})
admits a unique
K
-linear structure extending the one on
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

Denote the inclusion of
\mathcal{E}
into its left heart by
I:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{E})
. By construction,
LH(\mathcal{E})
is an abelian category. In particular, it is additive. Hence, its
Hom
functor satisfies:

\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(-,-):LH(\mathcal{E})^{\operatorname{op}}\times LH(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ab}.

Furthermore,
\mathcal{E}
is also additive, and by construction of
LH(\mathcal{E})
we have:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(-,-)=\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(I(-),I(-)),

as functors with values on abelian groups. Let
V,W\in LH(\mathcal{E})
. As
\mathcal{E}
has enough projective objects, it follows by
[
46
, Proposition 1.3.24]
that there
are projective objects
P_{0},P_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
such that we have the following right exact sequence in
LH(\mathcal{E})
:

I(P_{1})\rightarrow I(P_{0})\rightarrow V.

Furthermore, all the
I(P_{i})
is projective in
LH(\mathcal{E})
for
i=0,1
. In particular, we obtain the following left exact complex of abelian groups:

(5)

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(V,W)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(I(P_{0}),W)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(I(P_{1}),W).

By construction of
LH(\mathcal{E})
, there are objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{E}
which fit in a short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow I(X)\rightarrow I(Y)\rightarrow W\rightarrow 0.

As the
I(P_{i})
are projective in
LH(\mathcal{E})
, we obtain the following commutative diagram :

(6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(P_{0},X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(P_{0},Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(I(P_{0}),W)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(P_{1},X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(P_{1},Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(I(P_{1}),W)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where the rows are short exact sequences of abelian groups. As
\mathcal{E}
is
K
-linear, the first two terms in each
row are
K
-vector spaces, the first map in each row is
K
-linear, and the first two vertical maps are also
K
-linear. Thus, there are unique
K
-vector space structures on
\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(I(P_{i}),W)
for
i=0,1
making the above commutative diagram a commutative diagram of
K
-vector spaces. Using this structure, it follows that there is a unique
K
-linear structure on
\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\mathcal{E})}(V,W)
making
(\ref{equation K-linear structure first res})
a left exact sequence of
K
-vector spaces. Thus, we obtain a unique
K
-linear structure on
