LH(\mathcal{E})
.
∎

By
[
8
, Lemma 3.7]
, the categories
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
are elementary. Hence, we obtain a canonical
K
-linear structure on
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))=\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
, which we denote by:

\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(-,-):LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))\times LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

As usual, this
K
-linear structure extends to the categories of Ind-Banach modules over a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on a rigid space
X
. We may use this structure to define the next
Hom
functor:

Definition 2.4.3
.

Let
X
be a rigid space and
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
. We define the (external) sheaf of homomorphisms functor of
\mathscr{A}
as follows:

\mathcal{H}om_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}),

for
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
, and every admissible open
U\subset X
we have:

\Gamma(U,\mathcal{H}om_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}(\mathcal{M}_{|U},\mathcal{N}_{|U})),

and restriction maps given by restriction of morphisms of sheaves. More generally, if
\mathscr{B}
is a sheaf of algebras in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, we may repeat this construction to define a functor:

\mathcal{H}om_{\mathscr{B}}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\mathscr{B})\times\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\mathscr{B})\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}).

If
X=\operatorname{Sp}(K)
, we obtain
\mathcal{H}om_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
, so we recover the functor defined above. Furthermore, given a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras
\mathscr{A}
, we have the following identity of functors:

(7)

\mathcal{H}om_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)=\mathcal{H}om_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(-),I(-)).

As usual, the fact that
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
does not have enough injectives makes is unclear if
\mathcal{H}om_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
admits a derived functor. However, we can use the fact that
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, together with identity (
7
) to define:

R\mathcal{H}om_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):=R\mathcal{H}om_{I(\mathscr{A})}(-,-):\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K})).

Similarly, we define the following derived functor:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):=R\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(-,-):\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K}).

The interest of these
Hom
functors stems from the following observation:

Choose
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
. As
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, it follows by
[
52
, Tag 06XP]
that for every
n\geq 0
the
Ext
group:

\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathscr{A}}^{n}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}):=R^{n}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}),

parametrizes the isomorphism classes of
n
-th degree Yoneda’s extensions of
I(\mathcal{N})
by
I(\mathcal{M})
as
I(\mathscr{A})
-modules.
This perspective will be further explored in Section
4.2
.

3.
Stein Spaces

As mentioned in the introduction, the goal of this paper is giving explicit applications of our theory of Hochschild (co)-homology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules, and justifying its usefulness as a tool in the study of rigid analytic spaces and
p
-adic geometric representation theory. Let
X
be a smooth rigid analytic space. By construction, the Hochschild cohomology spaces
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
are geometric invariants of
X
. As with any geometric invariant, it is of the utmost importance to determine a class of spaces for which it is best behaved, and show that this class is wide enough to contain interesting examples.

In our case, this role will be played by the smooth Stein spaces. These spaces were first introduced into the rigid analytic setting by R. Kiehl in
[
27
]
. Roughly speaking, a Stein space is a rigid analytic space
X
equipped with a strictly increasing affinoid cover:

X_{0}\subset\cdots\subset X_{n}\subset X_{n+1}\subset\cdots\subset X,

satisfying that the identity
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n})
makes
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
a nuclear Fréchet space. From the algebraic point of view, these spaces are specially well-behaved. Namely, the category of coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
behaves very much like the category of coherent modules on an affinoid space. In particular, coherent modules have trivial sheaf cohomology, and each module is completely determined by its global sections. If
X
is smooth and satisfies some mild technical conditions, we will show that these properties also hold for the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Furthermore, an even stronger statement holds. Namely, we will show below that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is a Fréchet-Stein algebra, and that the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules is equivalent to the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-modules. From the analytic point of view, Stein spaces are also specially well-behaved. To wit, we will show that the global sections of objects in
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
are canonically nuclear Fréchet spaces. Furthermore, as will be explored in Chapter
4
, the de Rham complex of
X
is canonically a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. This fact will be used in later sections to obtain explicit interpretations of the cohomology groups of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
.

For use in later sections, we also include an account of the properties of finite group actions on Stein spaces. Namely, if
G
is a finite group acting on a Stein space
X
, we will show that the fixed point locus of the action
X^{G}
is always a Stein space. Similarly, we will show that the categorical quotient always
X/G
exists as a rigid space, and it is a Stein space.

Finally, let us mention that the class of smooth Stein spaces includes many interesting examples. For instance, the rigid analytification of a smooth affine
K
-variety is a smooth Stein space. Thus, every projective smooth rigid space is covered by finitely many smooth Stein spaces. Furthermore, the
p
-adic symmetric spaces covered by P. Schneider and U. Stuhler in
[
43
]
are smooth Stein spaces.

3.1.
Definition and basic properties

We start the chapter by giving an introduction to the theory of
p
-adic Stein spaces. In particular, we will recall some of their geometric properties and study the category of coherent modules on a Stein space. Let us start by recalling the definition:

Definition 3.1.1
.

We define the following objects:

(i)

Let
V\subset U
be an open immersion of affinoid spaces. We say
V
is relatively compact in
U
, and write
V\Subset U
, if there is a set of affinoid generators
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{U}(U)
such that:

V\subset\{x\in U\textnormal{ }|\textnormal{ }|f_{i}(x)|<1,\textnormal{ for }1\leq i\leq n\}.

(ii)

A rigid space
X
is called a quasi-Stein space if it admits an admissible affinoid cover
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
U_{i}\subset U_{i+1}
is a Weierstrass subdomain.

(iii)

We say
X
is a Stein space if, in addition, we have
U_{i}\Subset U_{i+1}
for all
i\geq 0
. We call
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a Stein covering of
X
, and write
X=\varinjlim_{i}U_{i}
.

Notice that any Stein space is partially proper. In particular, it is separated.

Remark 3.1.2
.

We point out that there are more general notions of Stein space available in the literature (cf.
[
32
]
). These notions are centered around cohomological properties of coherent sheaves on
X
. Our focus, however, is on the analytical properties of the global sections, and of the transition maps of the Stein covering. Hence, we will stick to the classical definition stated above.

Let
X
be a Stein space. It follows from the definition that given a Stein covering
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
then for each
n\geq 1
there are affinoid generators
f_{1},\cdots,f_{r_{n}}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n})
, and some real number
\epsilon\in\sqrt{|K^{*}|}
, satisfying that
0<\epsilon<1
and such that we have:

(8)

X_{n-1}\subseteq X_{n}\left(\epsilon^{-1}f_{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{-1}f_{r_{n}}\right).

If the identity holds, we recover the definition of a Stein space given by R. Kiehl in
[
27
, Definition 2.3]
.

Our interest in Stein spaces stems from the fact that they satisfy algebraic properties which are very similar to those of affinoid spaces, but have much better analytic properties. For instance, if
X
is a smooth Stein space, then
the complex
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}(X):=\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})
is a strict complex of Fréchet
K
-vector spaces, and its cohomology calculates the de Rham cohomology of
X
. This particular property will be exploited in the next sections. We will now give an overview of some of the most relevant features of Stein spaces. Recall the following definitions:

Definition 3.1.3

(
[
8
, Lemma 5.2]
[
28
, Definition 1.1]
)
.

A bounded map of Banach spaces
f:V\rightarrow W
is called strictly completely continuous morphism (
scc
morphism) if there is a sequence of morphisms:

f_{n}:V\rightarrow W,

such that
f=\varinjlim_{n\geq 0}f_{n}
in operator norm and such that
f_{n}(V)
is finite-dimensional for all
n\geq 0
.

Definition 3.1.4

(
[
8
, Definition 5.3]
)
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space. We say
V
is a nuclear Fréchet space if there is a Fréchet space
\varprojlim_{n}V_{n}
satisfying the following:

(i)

V=\varprojlim_{n}V_{n}
as Fréchet spaces.

(ii)

The image of
V
in
V_{n}
is dense for each
n\geq 0
.

(iii)

For each
n\geq 1
there is a Banach
K
-vector space
F_{n}
and a strict surjection:

F_{n}\rightarrow V_{n},

such that the composition
F_{n}\rightarrow V_{n}\rightarrow V_{n-1}
is
\emph{scc}
.

Let us start our study of Stein spaces with the following lemma:

Lemma 3.1.5
.

Let
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
be a Stein space. The following hold:

(i)

\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=\varprojlim_{n}\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a Fréchet-Stein presentation.

(ii)

\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a nuclear Fréchet
K
-space.

(iii)

Any closed analytic subspace
Y\rightarrow X
is a Stein space.

(iv)

If
Y
is contained in an affinoid space then
\operatorname{dim}(Y)=0
.

Proof.

Statement
(i)
follows directly from the definitions. For statement
(ii)
, we may assume for simplicity that the Stein covering
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
satisfies the conditions in equation
(\ref{equation special Stein covering})
. Fix some
n\geq 1
, and consider the surjection
\mathbb{B}^{r_{n}}_{K}\rightarrow X_{n}
given by
t_{i}\mapsto f_{i}
. We have a pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{r_{n}}_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{B}^{r_{n}}_{K}\left(\epsilon^{-1}t_{1},\cdots\epsilon^{-1}t_{r_{n}}\right)}}

such that the horizontal maps are closed immersions, and the vertical maps correspond to immersions of Weierstrass subdomains. It suffices to show that the associated map:

K\langle t_{1},\cdots,t_{r_{n}}\rangle\rightarrow K\langle\epsilon^{-1}t_{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{-1}t_{r_{n}}\rangle,

is
scc
. If
\epsilon\in|K^{*}|
, then this holds by
[
8
, pp. 63]
. In the general case, we may choose a finite extension
L
of
K
such that
\epsilon\in|L^{*}|
. In this case, we have the following pushout diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\langle t_{1},\cdots,t_{r_{n}}\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\langle t_{1},\cdots,t_{r_{n}}\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\langle\epsilon^{-1}t_{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{-1}t_{r_{n}}\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\langle\epsilon^{-1}t_{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{-1}t_{r_{n}}\rangle}}

such that the horizontal maps are finite, and the rightmost vertical map is
scc
. Again, the arguments in
[
8
, pp. 63]
show that

K\langle t_{1},\cdots,t_{r_{n}}\rangle\rightarrow K\langle\epsilon^{-1}t_{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{-1}t_{r_{n}}\rangle
is
scc
. Therefore,
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a nuclear Fréchet
K
-space, as we wanted to show.

Let
Y\rightarrow X
be a closed immersion, and let
\mathcal{I}_{Y}
be the associated sheaf of ideals. As
\mathcal{I}_{Y}
is a coherent sheaf on
X
, it follows that

\mathcal{I}_{Y}(X)
is a co-admissible
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-module. Thus, we have:

\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)/\mathcal{I}_{Y}(X)=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n})/\mathcal{I}_{Y}(X_{n}).

Letting
Y_{n}=\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n})/\mathcal{I}_{Y}(X_{n}))
, it follows that
Y=\varinjlim_{n}Y_{n}
is a Stein covering of
Y
. Assume that
Y
is contained in an affinoid subdomain. Then
Y
itself is an affinoid space. Hence,
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)

is a Banach
K
-algebra, and a nuclear Fréchet space. Notice that nuclear Fréchet spaces are reflexive by
[
44
, Corollary 19.3]
. However, by
[
44
, Proposition 11.1]
a Banach
K
-vector space is reflexive if and only if it is finite-dimensional. Hence,
\operatorname{dim}(Y)=0
, as we wanted to show.
∎

In particular, if
X
is a smooth Stein space with an action of a finite group
G
, this implies that the fixed point locus
X^{G}
is also a Stein space. Furthermore, by
[
35
, Proposition 2.1.9]
it follows that
X^{G}
is also smooth. We will now analyze the category of coherent sheaves on a Stein space:

Proposition 3.1.6
.

Let
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
be a Stein space, and let
\operatorname{Coh}(X)
be the category of coherent sheaves on
X
. The following hold:

(i)

Every
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
satisfies
\operatorname{H}^{i}(X,\mathcal{M})=0
for
i\geq 1
.

(ii)

There is an equivalence of abelian categories
\Gamma(X,-):\operatorname{Coh}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
.

(iii)

For each
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
, let
\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}
. The presentation
\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}
makes
\mathcal{M}
a nuclear Fréchet
K
-vector space.

Proof.

The first two statements are shown in
[
32
]
. The last follows by
(ii)
in Lemma
3.1.5
.
∎

Corollary 3.1.7
.

Let
X
be a Stein space and
\mathscr{V}
be a vector bundle on
X
. Then
\mathscr{V}(X)
is a locally finite-free
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-module.

Proof.

As
\mathscr{V}
is a vector bundle, the internal
Hom
functor
\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{V},-)
is exact. In particular, its derived functor satisfies
R\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{V},-)=\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{V},-)
. On the other hand, by Proposition
3.1.6
, the global sections functor
\Gamma(X,-)
is also exact. Thus, we have:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathscr{V}(X),-)=R\Gamma(X,R\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{V},-))=\Gamma(X,\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{V},-))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathscr{V}(X),-).

In particular,
R^{1}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathscr{V}(X),\mathscr{V}(X))=0
. Consider a short exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-modules:

0\rightarrow I\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{J}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}(X)\rightarrow 0.

Applying
R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathscr{V}(X),-)
to this sequence yields a short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathscr{V}(X),I)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathscr{V}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{J})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathscr{V}(X),\mathscr{V}(X))\rightarrow 0.

Thus, the surjection
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{J}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}(X)
has a split, and it follows that
\mathscr{V}(X)
is a projective
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-module. As the rank of
\mathscr{V}(X)
can be calculated at the stalks, and
\mathscr{V}
is a vector bundle, it follows that
\mathscr{V}(X)
is locally finite-free, as wanted.
∎

For some of the developments below, it will be convenient to show that smooth Stein spaces admit covers by Stein spaces in which certain vector bundles are trivial. Our next goal is showing that such covers always exist. Let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 3.1.8
.

Let
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
, and
Y=\varinjlim_{n}Y_{n}
be two Stein spaces. Then
X\times Y=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}\times Y_{n}
is also a Stein space. Furthermore, there is a canonical isomorphism of Fréchet spaces:

\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}(X\times Y)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)=\varprojlim\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n})\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y_{n}),

where
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
denotes the complete projective tensor product of locally convex spaces.

Proof.

It is clear that
X\times Y=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}\times Y_{n}
is an admissible cover of
X\times Y
by affinoid subdomains. We just need to show that it is a Stein cover. Clearly, for every
n\geq 0
we have that:

X_{n}\times Y_{n}\subset X_{n+1}\times Y_{n+1},

is a Weierstrass immersion. Furthermore, by
[
11
, Lemma 6.3.7]
,

X_{n}\Subset X_{n+1}
, and
Y_{n}\Subset Y_{n+1}
imply
X_{n}\times Y_{n}\Subset X_{n+1}\times Y_{n+1}
. Thus,
X\times Y=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}\times Y_{n}
is a Stein covering. The second part of the lemma is a classical statement in
p
-adic functional analysis. It can be found in
[
44
, pp. 107]
.
∎

Proposition 3.1.9
.

Let
X
be a Stein space and choose a holomorphic function
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. In this situation, the Zariski open subspace:

X(f^{-1})=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}X\left(\frac{\pi^{n}}{f}\right)=\{x\in X\textnormal{ }|\textnormal{ }|f(x)|>0\},

is a Stein space.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1.8
, the product
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}
is a Stein space. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the sheaf of ideals generated by
tf-1\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K})
, and
Y\subset X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}/\mathcal{I}
be the closed subvariety associated to
\mathcal{I}
. There is a canonical isomorphism
X(f^{-1})\rightarrow Y
, which shows that
X(f^{-1})
is a closed subvariety of
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}
. Thus, it follows by statement
(iii)
in Lemma
3.1.5
that
X(f^{-1})
is a Stein space.
∎

Corollary 3.1.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein space. There is a finite covering
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
X
such that each
U_{i}
is a smooth Stein space with an étale map
U_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
.

Proof.

As
X
is smooth,
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
is a locally free sheaf of rank
r=\dim(X)
. By Corollary
3.1.7
, there is a family of global sections
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
such that
the spaces
\{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)[f_{i}^{-1}])\}_{i\in I}
form a Zariski cover of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
, and such that the module:

(9)

\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)[f_{i}^{-1}]\cong\bigoplus^{r}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)[f_{i}^{-1}]

is a finite-free
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)[f_{i}^{-1}]
-module for each
i\in I
. As
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
is quasi-compact, we can assume that
I
is a finite index set. Thus, we have that
\{X(f_{i}^{-1})\}_{i\in I}
is a finite Zariski cover of
X
, and by Proposition
3.1.9
, each of the
X(f_{i}^{-1})
is a smooth Stein space. On the other hand, for each
i\in I
there is a canonical factorization:

\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)[f_{i}^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X(f_{i}^{-1})).

Thus, equation
(\ref{equation finite free locally on Stein})
shows that
\mathcal{T}_{X(f_{i}^{-1})/K}
is globally free of rank
r
, and this implies that there are étale maps
X(f_{i}^{-1})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, as we wanted to show.
∎

3.2.
Co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on smooth Stein spaces

Let
X=\varinjlim X_{n}
be a smooth Stein space. The goal of this section is studying the properties of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules on
X
. Following the path laid down in previous sections, we will show that the aforementioned category presents the same algebraic behavior as the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on an affinoid space, but satisfies better analytic properties. Namely, the global sections of co-admissible modules are canonically nuclear Fréchet spaces. We start by showing the corresponding results for coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. First, notice that by
[
8
, Theorem 6.4]
, we may regard the abelian category
\operatorname{Coh}(X)
as a full abelian subcategory of the quasi-abelian category
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
, and we will do so without further notice. Let us start with the following proposition:

Proposition 3.2.1
.

Let
X
be a Stein space and
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Then

\mathcal{M}
is acyclic for the functor:

\Gamma(X,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
. We need to show that
\mathcal{M}
is Čech-acyclic. Every admissible cover of
X
admits a refinement
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{n,i}\}_{n\geq 0}^{1\leq i\leq i_{n}}
, satisfying that for every
n\geq 0
, the family:

\mathcal{V}_{n}=\{V_{m,i},\textnormal{ }m\leq n,\,1\leq i\leq i_{m}\},

is a finite admissible cover of
X_{n}
. Hence, it suffices to show that the Čech complex
C(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}
is a strict and exact complex of Banach spaces for any cover satisfying said properties.

Fix a cover
\mathcal{V}
as above. Notice that
\mathcal{V}_{n}\subset\mathcal{V}_{n+1}
for each
n
, and that the Čech complexes:

C(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\prod_{m\leq n,\textnormal{ }1\leq j\leq i_{m}}\Gamma(V_{m,j},\mathcal{M})\rightrightarrows\cdots\right),

are exact complexes of Banach spaces for every
n\geq 0
. Hence, they are strict and exact complexes in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
.
Furthermore, the inclusions
\mathcal{V}_{n}\subset\mathcal{V}_{n+1}
induce maps:

C(\mathcal{V}_{n+1},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n+1}})^{\bullet}\rightarrow C(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{\bullet},

and we have
C(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}=\varprojlim_{n}C(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{\bullet}
.

As each of the
C(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{\bullet}
is exact, it follows by
[
8
, Corollary 5.2]
that it suffices to show that for each
m\geq 0
the inverse systems:

C(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{m}=\varprojlim_{n}C(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{m},

are pre-nuclear inverse systems of Banach spaces with dense image. By construction, the maps
C(\mathcal{V}_{n+1},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n+1}})^{m}\rightarrow C(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{m}
are split epimorphisms for
m>0
. Hence, they clearly form a pre-nuclear system of Banach spaces. For
m=0
, the inverse system becomes:

\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim_{n}\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{M}),

which is nuclear by statement
(iii)
in Proposition
3.1.6
. Hence,
C(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}
is strict and exact.
∎

In particular, the sheaves
\Omega_{X/K}^{n}
are
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic for every
n\geq 0
. This will be used in the following chapter to compare
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
with
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
.

Remark 3.2.2
.

For the rest of the section, we assume that
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, and that
X
admits an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
.

Our goal now is extending this result to co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. It will be convenient to start by showcasing some of the algebraic properties of
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. Let us start with the following theorem:

Theorem 3.2.3
.

There is a family of
\mathcal{R}
-modules:

\{\mathcal{T}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0},

and a strictly increasing sequence of non-negative integers
\{m(n)\}_{n\geq 0}
satisfying the following properties for each
n\geq 0
:

(i)

\mathcal{T}_{n}\subset\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X_{n})
.

(ii)

We have an identity of
A^{\circ}_{n}
modules:
\mathcal{T}_{n}=A^{\circ}_{n}\otimes_{A^{\circ}_{n+1}}\mathcal{T}_{n+1}
.

(iii)

\pi^{m(n)}\mathcal{T}_{n}\subset
is a free
(\mathcal{R},A^{\circ}_{n})
-lie lattice of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X_{n})
.

In this situation, let
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m}=\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T}_{n})_{K}
. Then the following hold:

(i)

For each
n\geq 0
, the inverse limit
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})=\varprojlim_{m\geq m(n)}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m}
is a Fréchet-Stein presentation.

(ii)

The inverse limit
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{i}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{i})=\varprojlim_{n}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}
is a Fréchet-Stein presentation.

Furthermore, every co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module is a nuclear Fréchet space.

Proof.

If
K
is discretely valued, the theorem is a special instance of
[
36
, Proposition 4.3.2]
, and
[
36
, Corollary 4.3.4]
. The general case follows by the same argument together with the flatness results in
[
2
, Theorem 4.1.11]
and
[
2
, Theorem 4.2.8]
.
∎

Remark 3.2.4
.

It is precisely at this point where we need that
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed. Indeed, for arbitrary complete non-archimedean extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
the algebras of power bounded elements
A^{\circ}_{n}
as above are not necessarily topologically finitely presented.

For the rest of this section, we fix a family
\{\mathcal{T}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, and a strictly increasing sequence of non-negative integers
\{m(n)\}_{n\geq 0}
as above. As a consequence of this theorem, we obtain the following:

Corollary 3.2.5
.

For each
n\geq 0
the canonical map
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})
is
c
-flat.

Proof.

In light of the previous theorem, it suffices to show that for each
n\geq 0
and each
i\geq n
the map
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{i,m(i)}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(i)}
is flat. However, by construction, we have:

\pi^{m(i)}\mathcal{T}_{n}=A_{n}^{\circ}\otimes_{A_{i}^{\circ}}\pi^{m(i)}\mathcal{T}_{i}.

Hence, the result is a direct consequence of
[
2
, Theorem 4.2.8]
.
∎

We point out that the existence of such presentations depends on our assumption that
X
admits an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. However, by Corollary
3.1.10
, every smooth Stein space can be covered by finitely many smooth Stein spaces satisfying this assumption. Thus, imposing the existence of an étale map to
\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
is not a very restrictive condition. With this theorem at hand, we are ready to start characterizing the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules on
X
. Let us start with the following:

Lemma 3.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. We have an identification:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}),\Gamma(X,\mathcal{N})).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. First, as each
X_{n}
is an affinoid space, it follows by a slight modification of
[
34
, Proposition 6.2.12]
that we have an identification:

(10)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X_{n}}}(\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}},\mathcal{N}_{|X_{n}})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})}(\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{M}),\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{N})).

For each
n\geq 0
, and each
i\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{M}_{n,i}:=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,i}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X_{n})
. As
\mathcal{M}
is a sheaf of Ind-Banach spaces, we have the following identities:

\mathcal{M}(X)=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}(X_{n})=\varprojlim_{n}\varprojlim_{i\geq m(n)}\mathcal{M}_{n,i}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n,n(m)}.

Thus,
\mathcal{M}(X)
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module. Chose a morphism of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-modules
f:\mathcal{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{N}(X)
. By
[
34
, Proposition 6.2.9]
, for each
n\geq 0
there is a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(X_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that the lower map is uniquely determined by
f:\mathcal{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{N}(X)
. In virtue of identity (
10
), it suffices to show that we have an identification of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})
-modules:

\mathcal{M}(X_{n})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X).

By
[
8
, Proposition 5.33]
, and Corollary
3.2.5
, we have the following identity in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X))=I((\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\wideparen{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X))^{b}),

where
\wideparen{\otimes}
denotes the co-admissible tensor product from
[
3
, Section 7]
. Hence, it suffices to show that we have an identification of Fréchet spaces:

\mathcal{M}(X_{n})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\wideparen{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X).

As
\mathcal{M}(X)
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module, we have the following identities of Ind-Banach spaces:

\mathcal{M}(X)=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}(X_{n})=\varprojlim_{n}\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{M}_{n,i}.

On the other hand, by definition of the co-admissible tensor product, we have the following identities:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\wideparen{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X)=\varprojlim_{i\geq m(n)}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,i}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{i,i}}\mathcal{M}_{i,i}=\varprojlim_{i\geq n}\mathcal{M}_{n,i}=\mathcal{M}(X_{n}),

which is precisely the identity we wanted to show.
∎

We may use this lemma to show the following:

Proposition 3.2.7
.

There is an equivalence of abelian categories:

\Gamma(X,-):\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)):\operatorname{Loc}(-),

where
\operatorname{Loc}(-)
is defined by sending a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module
\mathcal{M}
to the unique co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module satisfying the following identity:

\operatorname{Loc}(\mathcal{M})(X_{n})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}.

Proof.

The fact that
\Gamma(X,-)
is fully faithful was shown in the previous lemma. Thus, we only need to show that it is essentially surjective. On the other hand, we also saw in the previous lemma that for every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
, and every
n\geq 0
the Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})
-module
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}
is co-admissible. Hence, by
[
8
, Theorem 6.4]
, it extends to a co-admissible

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X_{n}}
-module. Furthermore, as co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on affinoid spaces are completely determined by their global sections, it is clear that
\operatorname{Loc}(\mathcal{M})
is a sheaf of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. We just need to show that:

\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{n}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}.

However, using the notation from the proof of Lemma
3.2.6
we have:

\varprojlim_{n}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{n}\varprojlim_{m\geq m(n)}\mathcal{M}_{n,m}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n,m(n)}=\mathcal{M},

as we wanted to show.
∎

This allows us to obtain a version of
[
8
, Corollary 5.15]
for Stein spaces:

Corollary 3.2.8
.

We have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{diss}\circ(-)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{diss}\circ(-)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X,-)}

where the upper horizontal map is an equivalence of abelian categories and the vertical maps are exact and fully faithful.

Thus, from now on, we will identify
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
with its essential image inside
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
.

Now that we have an accurate description of the category of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules on
X
, we can start analyzing its behavior with respect to the global sections functor. As usual, the first step is understanding the situation in the affinoid case:

Lemma 3.2.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth affinoid space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
and
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. Then

\mathcal{M}
is acyclic with respect to the functor
\Gamma(X,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
.

Proof.

As
X
is quasi-compact, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{M}
has trivial higher Čech cohomology with respect to finite affinoid covers. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be such a cover. We need to show that the augmented Čech complex:

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}=\left(0\rightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Gamma(\cap_{i=1}^{m}V_{i},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow 0\right),

is a strict exact complex of Ind-Banach spaces. However, each object in
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}
is the image of a complete bornological space under the dissection functor. Hence, it suffices to show that the complex is strict exact in the category of complete bornological spaces.

For each
V_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
, let
A_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{X}(V_{i})
, and set
A=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. As
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
is free as an
A
-module and
\mathcal{V}
is a finite cover of
X
, we may apply
[
3
, Proposition 5.1]
and
[
3
, Proposition 7.6]
to choose adequate smooth affine formal models of
A
, and of the

A_{i}
, so that we have Fréchet-Stein presentations:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(V_{i})=\varprojlim_{r}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{r,i},\,\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{r}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{r},

satisfying that the complex:

(11)

\mathcal{C}{r}^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{r}\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{r,i}\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(V_{i})}\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\cdots\right),

is a strict and exact complex of complete bornological spaces for every
r\geq 0
. Notice that the aforementioned results only show that
(\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\bullet})^{t}
is a strict exact complex in
LCS_{K}
. However, as this is a complex of Banach spaces, strict exactness is preserved under
(-)^{b}
by
[
8
, Lemma 4.4]
. Furthermore, by definition of co-admissible module, we have
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}=\varprojlim_{r}\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\bullet}
.

As each of the
\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\bullet}
is strict and exact, we may apply
[
8
, Corollary 5.2]
to conclude that showing strict exactness of
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}
is reduced to showing that for each
m\geq 0
, the inverse limit:

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{m}=\varprojlim_{r}\mathcal{C}_{r}^{m},

is a pre-nuclear inverse system of Banach spaces. By construction, for every affinoid subdomain
V_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
, we have an isomorphism:

\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim_{r}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{r,i}\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(V_{i})}\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{M}).

By
[
8
, Proposition 5.5]
such a presentation makes
\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{M})
a nuclear
A_{i}
-module. In particular, the associated inverse system is a pre-nuclear system of Banach spaces. As this argument holds for
X
and finite intersections of elements in
\mathcal{V}
, we conclude that
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}
is a strict and exact complex of complete bornological spaces, as wanted.
∎

We are finally ready to show the main theorem of the section:

Proposition 3.2.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, then

\mathcal{M}
is acyclic with respect to the functor:

\Gamma(X,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

Choose a family of covers
\cup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{V}_{n}
as in the proof of Proposition
3.2.1
. By Lemma
3.2.9
, it follows that the Čech complex
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{\bullet}
is a strict and exact complex of complete bornological spaces for every
n\geq 0
. Furthermore, the inclusions
\mathcal{V}_{n}\subset\mathcal{V}_{n+1}
induce an isomorphism:

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{M})^{\bullet}=\varprojlim\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{\bullet}.

Hence, we need to show that for each
m\geq 0
, we have:

R^{1}\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{m}=0.

As in the proof of Proposition
3.2.1
, for
m\geq 1
the maps in the inverse system

\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{m}
are split surjections, hence
R^{1}\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{|X_{n}})^{m}=0
for all
m\geq 1
. Thus, we only need to show:

R^{1}\varprojlim\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{M})=0.

However, applying the notation from Lemma
3.2.6
to
\mathcal{M}(X)
, we have:

\varprojlim\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n,m(n)}.

By Theorem
3.2.3
this presentation makes
\mathcal{M}(X)
a nuclear Fréchet space. Thus, we have:

R^{1}\varprojlim\Gamma(X_{n},\mathcal{M})=0,

by
[
8
, Theorem 5.26]
. As we wanted to show.
∎

Remark 3.2.11
.

Notice that if
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
is a Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, then
X^{2}:=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}\times X_{n}
is also a Stein space with an étale map
X^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}
. In particular, the results above hold for co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-modules. Therefore, the analogous results hold for co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules as well, via the equivalences of quasi-abelian categories:

\operatorname{S}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}_{X}):\operatorname{S}^{-1}.

4.
Applications of Hochschild cohomology

After introducing the class of spaces we are interested in, the next step is studying their behavior with respect to Hochschild cohomology.
Let
X
be a smooth Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. In this chapter, we will compare the Hochschild cohomology complex
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
with a bunch of other complexes arising from different cohomology theories. As an upshot, we obtain numerous explicit interpretations for the Hochschild cohomology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Let us now give an overview of the contents of the chapter: We start by recalling a result by E. Grosse-Klönne in
[
20
]
, which states that we have a canonical identification of complexes:

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)=\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}),

which makes
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. This result, together with our previous calculations, shows that there is a strict quasi-isomorphism
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}
. In particular,
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a strict complex of (bornologifications of) nuclear Fréchet spaces. We then use this fact to show a Künneth formula for Hochschild cohomology:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\times Y})=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}),

where
Y
is another smooth Stein space. Next, we use the Spencer resolution:

0\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow 0,

to express
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
as a derived internal Hom functor. In particular, we show that the following identity holds in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)).

Following this line of thought, we then use the discussion on external sheaves of homomorphisms from Section
2.4
to deduce the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)).

Notice that this shows that the vector spaces underlying the cohomology groups of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
parameterize the Yoneda’s extensions of
I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
by
I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
in
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))
. Similarly, this also shows that the isomorphism classes of Yoneda extensions of
I(\mathcal{O}_{X})
by
I(\mathcal{O}_{X})

in
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
are completely determined by their global sections, and that every Yoneda extension of the global sections is induced by an extension at the level of sheaves. We remark that this type of phenomena resembles the behavior of quasi-coherent
\mathcal{D}
-modules on smooth affine
K
-varieties.

One of the most interesting features of smooth Stein spaces is that many algebraic properties of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules can already be detected at the level of global sections. This stems from the equivalence of abelian categories obtained in Proposition
3.2.7
:

\Gamma(X,-):\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)):\operatorname{Loc}(-).

Unfortunately, these abelian categories do not contain injective objects, so it is not possible to extend this equivalence to the derived level. Nonetheless, in analogy with the algebraic setting, we would like to show that the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
can be calculated at the global sections. Namely, we want to show that there is an identification in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

(12)

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

In order to do so, we resort to the sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras
\wideparen{E}_{X}
on
X^{2}
, and show that there is a co-admissible

\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that there is a strict exact complex:

E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}:=\left(0\rightarrow P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0\right),

where each
P^{i}
is a finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module and
P^{0}=\wideparen{E}_{X}
. The identification in (
12
) then follows naturally from the existence of the resolution
E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}
. As a consequence, we obtain an isomorphism
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}
. This isomorphism will be instrumental to our approach to the deformation theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
in Chapter
6
.

4.1.
Comparison with de Rham cohomology

In this section, we will use the results from
[
20
]
to study the Hochschild cohomology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
for a smooth Stein space
X
. Until the end of the section, we fix a smooth Stein space
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
admitting an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. Recall that, as we saw above, this is always the case locally.

As shown in Proposition
3.2.1
, the sheaves of differentials
\Omega_{X/K}^{n}
are acyclic for the functor:

\Gamma(X,-):\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}).

Thus, we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})=\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}).

Notice that this is not enough to conclude that
\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})
is a strict complex of bornological spaces. In particular, the Hochschild cohomology spaces need not be bornological spaces. The reason behind working with smooth Stein spaces stems from the following proposition:

Proposition 4.1.1

(
[
20
, Corollary 3.2]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein space. Then the complex:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)=\left(0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\Omega_{X/K}^{1}(X)\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Omega_{X/K}^{\operatorname{dim}(X)}(X)\right),

is a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. Thus,
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X)
is a nuclear Fréchet space for
n\geq 0
.

Notice that the proposition above does not imply that the complex
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
is a strict complex in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, only that
\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})
is a strict complex in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. Furthermore,
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
will in general not be a strict complex of sheaves. For instance,
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}
is a smooth Stein space, but
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}/K}
is not a strict complex of sheaves. Indeed, if
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}/K}
was strict, then the complex:

\Gamma(\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K},\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}/K})=\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K}/K}(\mathbb{B}^{1}_{K}),

would be a strict complex in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. However, as seen in the introduction, this is false.

Recall from
[
24
, pp. 93, Proposition 3]
that there is an adjunction:

(-)^{t}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\leftrightarrows LCS_{K}:(-)^{b}.

By the previous proposition, we may regard
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)
as a complex of Fréchet spaces, and we let
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}
be the associated complex of complete bornological spaces. We can use these tools to show an improved version of
[
34
, Corollary 8.1.12]
:

Theorem 4.1.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein space. We have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})=\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}.

Thus,
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a strict complex, and
\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X)^{b}
is a nuclear Fréchet space for
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

The first three identities have already been shown. For the last identity, regard
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)

as a complex in
LCS_{K}
with the topology constructed in Proposition
4.1.1
. By that proposition, all objects in
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)
are nuclear Fréchet spaces. As
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)
is a strict complex of Fréchet spaces, all images are closed. Thus, by
[
44
, Proposition 19.4]

all kernels, images and cohomology groups of
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)
are nuclear Fréchet spaces. Hence, by
[
8
, Proposition 5.12]
the complex:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}=\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}),

is a strict exact complex of complete bornological spaces.
∎

As a first application of this theorem, we will calculate the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\times Y}
, where
X
and
Y
are smooth Stein spaces. In order to do this, we will now show that the Künneth formula holds for the de Rham cohomology of products of smooth Stein spaces. Notice that this result was already claimed in

[
20
, Proposition 3.3]
. However, the arguments used there only work if one of the spaces has finite de Rham cohomology. Let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth Stein spaces. Then the following complex is strict:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}.

In particular, for each
n\geq 0
we have the following identity in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

\operatorname{H}^{n}(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b})=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b}.

Proof.

By the above,
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}
and
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}
are strict complexes of Fréchet spaces. Thus, it follows by
[
8
, Proposition 4.22]
and
[
8
, Corollary 4.24]
that
I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b})
and
I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b})
are complexes of
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
-flat modules such that the image of any differential is
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
-flat. Furthermore, we have:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}=I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}).

Therefore, we may use the fact that
[
50
, Theorem 3.63]
holds in any closed symmetric monoidal Grothendieck category to get:

\operatorname{H}^{n}(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b})=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b}).

Furthermore, as all de Rham cohomology groups are Fréchet spaces, they are metrizable. Thus, by
[
8
, Proposition 4.25]

we have:

\bigoplus_{r+s=n}I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b})=I(\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b}).

Hence, all cohomology groups of
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}
are objects of the essential image of the functor
I:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Therefore,
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}
is a strict complex, as we wanted.
∎

Theorem 4.1.4
.

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth Stein spaces. We have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X\times Y)^{b}=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}.

In particular, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get the following Künneth formula in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X\times Y)^{b}=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b}.

Proof.

As
X
and
Y
are smooth Stein spaces,
X\times Y
is also a smooth Stein space by Lemma
3.1.8
. We need to show that the canonical morphism of chain complexes of complete bornological spaces:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}\rightarrow\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X\times Y)^{b},

is an isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. By Lemma
4.1.3
, this amounts to showing that for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X\times Y)^{b}=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b}.

Fix some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. By the proof of
[
20
, Corollary 3.2]
, we have an admissible cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
of
X
satisfying that
U_{i}\subset U_{i+1}
for all
i\geq 0
, and
U_{i}
is a smooth Stein space with finite dimensional de Rham cohomology for every
i\geq 0
. We claim that such a cover satisfies the following identity
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X)^{b}=\varprojlim_{i}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i})^{b},

so that we have a strict short exact sequence:

(13)

0\rightarrow\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\prod_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i})^{b}\rightarrow\prod_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i})^{b}\rightarrow 0.

Indeed, as
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i})
is finite dimensional for all
i\geq 0
, it follows by
[
43
, Chapter 2 Corollary 5]
that:

R^{1}\varprojlim\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i})=0.

Hence, we have the following short exact sequence in
LCS_{K}
:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X)\rightarrow\prod_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i})\rightarrow\prod_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i})\rightarrow 0.

The last two terms of the sequence are countable products of nuclear Fréchet spaces, hence nuclear Fréchet spaces by
[
44
, Proposition 19.7]
. Thus, it follows by
[
8
, Proposition 5.12]
that
(\ref{equation short exact sequence inverse limit de Rham cohomology})
is a strict short exact sequence of complete bornological spaces.

Let
\{V_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
be an admissible covering of
Y
satisfying the same properties as
\{U_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
. Then by the Künneth formula
[
20
, proposition 3.3]
, it follows that
\{U_{i}\times V_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
is an admissible covering of
X\times Y
satisfying the same properties. In particular, we have:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X\times Y)^{b}=\varprojlim_{i}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(U_{i}\times V_{i})^{b}=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\varprojlim_{i}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{i})^{b}\right).

Fix
r,s\geq 0
such that
r+s=n
. Then we have:

\varprojlim_{i}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{i})^{b}\right)=\varprojlim_{j}\varprojlim_{i}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}\right).

For each
j\geq 0
, the space
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}
is finite dimensional, and
\prod_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}
is the product of a countable family of finite dimensional spaces. Thus, by
[
8
, Corollary 5.2]
, we have:

\left(\prod_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}=\prod_{i\geq 0}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}\right).

As
-\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}
is strongly exact, the fact that
(\ref{equation short exact sequence inverse limit de Rham cohomology})
is strict exact implies that we have a strict short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}\rightarrow\prod_{i\geq 0}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}\right)\rightarrow\prod_{i\geq 0}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}\right)\rightarrow 0.

In particular, we arrive at the following identity in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

\varprojlim_{i}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}\right)=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}.

Applying the same argument above, and using the fact that
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)
is a Fréchet space, we get:

\varprojlim_{j}\varprojlim_{i}\left(\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(U_{i})^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}\right)=\varprojlim_{j}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(V_{j})^{b}=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b}.

Hence, we get
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{n}(X\times Y)^{b}=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{r}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{s}(Y)^{b}
, as we wanted to show.
∎

As a consequence, we get a Künneth formula for Hochschild cohomology:

Corollary 4.1.5
.

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth Stein spaces. The following identity holds in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\times Y})=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}).

In particular, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have the following Künneth formula in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\times Y})=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{HH}^{r}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{HH}^{s}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}).

Proof.

We have the following isomorphisms:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\times Y})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X\times Y)^{b}=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)^{b}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(Y)^{b}=\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}),

where the first and third identities follow from Theorem
4.1.2
, and the second one is Theorem
4.1.4
. The second part of the theorem follows from the corresponding Künneth formula for the de Rham cohomology of a product of smooth Stein spaces.
∎

4.2.
Hochschild cohomology and extensions I

As mentioned above, to final goal of this chapter is highlighting the relevance of Hochschild cohomology in the study of smooth rigid analytic spaces, and in particular in the study of smooth Stein spaces. In the previous section, we established a direct comparison between
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
and
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)
, which showcases the geometric significant of Hochschild cohomology. In this section, we follow a more algebraic approach, by showing how
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
can be related to the Ext functors arising as the derived functors of the different types of
Hom
functors.
As before, we fix a smooth Stein space
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
.

Remark 4.2.1
.

For the rest of the section, we assume
K
is discretely valued or algebraically closed.

Let us start by showing an
inner
version of Lemma
3.2.6
:

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. The following identity holds:

\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{N}(X)).

Proof.

By definition of
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(-,-)
, we have the following identity:

\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\prod_{U\subset X}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(U)}(\mathcal{M}(U),\mathcal{N}(U))\rightrightarrows\prod_{V\subset U}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(U)}(\mathcal{M}(U),\mathcal{N}(V))\right),

where
U
(resp.
V
) runs over all the affinoid subspaces of
X
(resp. all affinoid subdomains of
U
). The maps in the equalizer are constructed as follows: Let
U\subset X
be an affinoid open subspace, and
V\subset U
be an affinoid subdomain. The first of the maps in the equalizer is the product of the maps:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(U)}(\mathcal{M}(U),\mathcal{N}(U))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(U)}(\mathcal{M}(U),\mathcal{N}(V)),

induced by pushforward along the restriction map
\mathcal{N}(U)\rightarrow\mathcal{N}(V)
. The second map is obtained via products of maps of the form:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(V)}(\mathcal{M}(V),\mathcal{N}(V))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(U)}(\mathcal{M}(U),\mathcal{N}(V)),

induced by pullback along the map
\mathcal{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(V)
. As
X=\varinjlim X_{n}
is a Stein space, we may simplify this equalizer as follows:

(14)

\operatorname{Eq}\left(\prod_{n\geq 1}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})}(\mathcal{M}(X_{n}),\mathcal{N}(X_{n}))\rightrightarrows\prod_{n\geq 1}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})}(\mathcal{M}(X_{n}),\mathcal{N}(X_{n-1}))\right).

As
\mathcal{M}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module, it follows by Proposition
3.2.7
, that for every
n\geq 0
we have:

\mathcal{M}(X_{n})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}(X).

In particular, a quick Yoneda argument shows that for every
n\geq 0
we have the following identities:

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})}(\mathcal{M}(X_{n}),\mathcal{N}(X_{n}))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{N}(X_{n})),

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{n})}(\mathcal{M}(X_{n}),\mathcal{N}(X_{n-1}))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{N}(X_{n-1}))

Hence, the equalizer in
(\ref{equation 1 global sections of inner hom})
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Eq}\left(\prod_{n\geq 1}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{N}(X_{n}))\rightrightarrows\prod_{n\geq 1}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{N}(X_{n-1}))\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{N}(X_{n}))

\displaystyle=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{N}(X_{n}))

\displaystyle=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{N}(X)),

and this is precisely the identity we wanted to show.
∎

As
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module, we have the following identity of functors:

(15)

\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},-))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\Gamma(X,-)),

and we wish to study its derived functor. Recall from
[
8
, Section 6.3]
that the Spencer resolution
S^{\bullet}
gives a strict resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
by finite locally free
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Namely, we have a complex:

S^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0\right),

which is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{X}
in
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. Furthermore, as we have an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, the terms in
S^{\bullet}
are finite free
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
be a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module. In this situation, we have the following chain of identities:

R\left(\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M}))\right)=R\Gamma(X,R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M}))=R\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(S^{\bullet},\mathcal{M})),

where the first identity is
[
34
, Proposition 3.3.12]
, and the second identity follows from the fact that
S^{\bullet}
is a resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
by finite free
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Furthermore, the objects of the complex
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(S^{\bullet},\mathcal{M})
are finite direct sums of
\mathcal{M}
. In particular, they are co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Thus, the objects of
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(S^{\bullet},\mathcal{M})
are
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic by Proposition
3.2.10
. Hence, we have:

R\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(S^{\bullet},\mathcal{M}))=\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(S^{\bullet},\mathcal{M}))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\Gamma(X,S^{\bullet}),\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M})).

On the other hand, as the map
S^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
is an isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
between complexes of
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic modules, it follows that
\Gamma(X,S^{\bullet})
is a strict resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
by finite-free
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-modules. Therefore, we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\Gamma(X,S^{\bullet}),\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}))=R\Gamma(X,R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M})).

We may summarize the previous discussion into the following proposition:

Definition 4.2.3

(
[
34
, Definition 8.1.3.]
)
.

For any
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, the de Rham complex of
\mathcal{M}
is defined as the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\operatorname{dR}(\mathcal{M}):=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M}).

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
X
be a Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. For any co-admissible module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):

R\Gamma(X,\operatorname{dR}(\mathcal{M}))=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X)).

Next, we want to investigate the extent to which this discussion holds for the external
Hom
functors
\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(-,-)
, and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(-,-)
from Definition
2.4.3
. As we will see below, these functors behave exceptionally well when applied to co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules on a smooth Stein space. Furthermore, as we mentioned at the end of Section
2.4
, the derived functor
R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(-,-)
can be used to parameterize Yoneda’s extensions. Our next goal is using this interpretation to obtain a new application of the Hochschild cohomology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 4.2.5
.

Let
X
be a Stein space or an affinoid space, with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
. We have the following identity of
K
-vector spaces:

\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{M}),\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}(I(\mathcal{M}(X)),\mathcal{N}(X)).

Proof.

This is analogous to the proof of Lemma
3.2.6
.
∎

Many of the arguments leading up to Proposition
4.2.4
are a consequence of the fact that locally finite-free
I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
-modules are acyclic for
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(-,-)
, and this allows us to use the Spencer resolution to calculate all derived functors involved. In order to study the situation for the external sheaves of homomorphisms, we need to show that a similar result holds. Let us start with the following:

Lemma 4.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{V}\in LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Then for any
n\geq 0
we have:

\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}I(K),\mathcal{V})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Forget}(J(\mathcal{V})).

Proof.

For simplicity, we may assume
n=1
. By construction of the left heart, there are
V_{1},V_{2}\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
satisfying that we have the following short exact sequence in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

0\rightarrow I(V_{1})\rightarrow I(V_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}\rightarrow 0.

As
I(K)
is projective in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
, the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(K),-)
is exact. Thus, we have the following exact sequence of
K
-vector spaces:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(K),I(V_{1}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(K),I(V_{2}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(K),\mathcal{V})\rightarrow 0.

As
J
and
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
are exact and commute with finite direct sums, this short exact sequence shows that it suffices to show the claim for
\mathcal{V}=I(W)
for some
W\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. However, as the functor
I:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is fully faithful, we have:

\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(K),I(W))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(K,W)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}c_{K}}(J(K),J(W))=\operatorname{Forget}(J(W)),

which is the identity we wanted to show. Hence, the lemma holds.
∎

Next, we want to generalize this lemma to the level of sheaves. Recall the version for sheaves of
J
, and
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
given in Definition
2.3.4
. We can use the previous lemma to show the following:

Lemma 4.2.7
.

The following identity of functors holds:

\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),-)=\operatorname{Forget}(J(-)):\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{B}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}).

In particular,
\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),-)
is an exact functor.

Proof.

Choose a module
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{B}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
. By Lemma
4.2.5
,
\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),\mathcal{M})
is a sheaf of
K
-vector spaces given by the following expression for each affinoid open subspace
U\subset X
:

\Gamma(U,\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),\mathcal{M}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(U))}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(U)),\mathcal{M}(U))=\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(K),\mathcal{M}(U)).

Hence, by Lemma
4.2.6
we have
the following identity of functors:

\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),-)=\operatorname{Forget}(J\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),-)),

Furthermore, by
[
34
, Lemma 8.1.10]
the composition:

\operatorname{Forget}(-)\circ J:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}),

is exact. Thus,
\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),-)
is exact, as we wanted to show.
∎

As before, this result has important consequences for the derived external
Hom
functors:

Lemma 4.2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
be an injective object. Then
\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\mathcal{I})
is a
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic sheaf of
K
-vector spaces. In particular, the following identity holds:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},-)=R\Gamma(X,R\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},-)):\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})^{+}\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})^{+}.

Proof.

As
\mathcal{I}
is injective, we can use the Spencer resolution to obtain the following exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\mathcal{I})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),\mathcal{I})\rightarrow\\
\cdots\rightarrow\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}),\mathcal{I})\rightarrow 0.

An application of Lemma
4.2.7
shows that this sequence can be rewritten as follows:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\mathcal{I})\rightarrow\operatorname{Forget}(J\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),\mathcal{I}))\rightarrow\\
\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Forget}(J\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}),\mathcal{I}))\rightarrow 0.

Recall that by
[
34
, Lemma 8.1.10]
, the functors
J
and
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
are exact. Thus, it follows that we have an isomorphism of sheaves:

\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\mathcal{I})=\operatorname{Forget}(J\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\mathcal{I})).

As
\mathcal{I}
is injective, it follows by
[
34
, Lemma 3.3.13]
that
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\mathcal{I})
is injective. Thus, it is
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic. But then it follows by
[
34
, Proposition 8.1.11]
that
\mathcal{H}om_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(I(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\mathcal{I})
is also
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic, as we wanted to show. For the second part of the lemma, notice that we have restricted the derived functors to the bounded below derived category. Hence, the equation follows from the canonical identification:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(-,-)=\Gamma(X,\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(-,-)),

together with the first part of the lemma.
∎

These lemmas provide us with enough technical tools to show the following theorem:

Theorem 4.2.9
.

Let
X
be a Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. For any co-admissible module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}):

(16)

R\Gamma(X,\operatorname{dR}(\mathcal{M}))=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X)).

Furthermore, we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

(17)

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M})=R\Gamma(X,R\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X)).

In particular, the following holds:

(i)

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
as objects in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
.

(ii)

We have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

Equation (
16
) is a special case of Proposition
4.2.4
. Furthermore, our calculations of Hochschild cohomology show that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\operatorname{dR}(\mathcal{O}_{X}))
. Thus, statement
(i)
holds as well.

Next, we want to show (
17
). Notice that the first identity was already shown in Lemma
4.2.8
. The second identity is a direct calculation using the Spencer resolution. Namely, we have the following:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\Gamma(X,S^{\bullet}),\mathcal{M}(X))=\Gamma(X,\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(S^{\bullet},\mathcal{M}))\\
