=\Gamma(X,R\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M}))=R\Gamma(X,R\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M})).

In order to show
(ii)
, we proceed by a direct computation:

\displaystyle R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))=

\displaystyle\left(0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K},\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))\rightarrow 0\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\operatorname{Forget}(J\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Forget}(J\Omega_{X/K}^{n}(X))\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Forget}(J\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/K}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})),

where in the second identity we are using the fact that the functor:

\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{O}_{X}),

is fully faithful (
cf.

[
8
, Theorem 6.4]
), together with Lemma
4.2.6
. Hence, the theorem holds.
∎

We remark that this result was already known in the context of smooth affine
K
-varieties. See, for example,
[
14
, Proposition 2.1]
. However, our result adds an extra layer of depth, as the spaces involved are complete bornological spaces. As mentioned in the end of Section
2.4
, this theorem allows us to regard
\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
as the parameter space of isomorphism classes of the
n
-th Yoneda extensions of
I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
by
I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
in
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))
. Furthermore, Theorem
4.2.9
also shows that every such extension induces a unique
extension of
I(\mathcal{O}_{X})
by
I(\mathcal{O}_{X})
in
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
.

In the case of a first order Yoneda extension, this correspondence can be obtained explicitly. Namely,
by Theorem
3.2.3
there is a Fréchet-Stein presentation

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{m}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module, and set
\mathcal{M}_{m}=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{m}\otimes_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{M}
, so that we have
\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{m}\mathcal{M}_{m}
. Assume we have the following degree one Yoneda extension in
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
:

0\rightarrow I(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{N}\rightarrow I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))\rightarrow 0.

For every
m\geq 0
, we have the following short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow I(\mathcal{M}_{m})\rightarrow I(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{m})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))\rightarrow 0.

This sequence is split as a sequence of
I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
-modules. In particular, the
I(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{m})
-module:

\mathcal{N}_{m}:=I(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{m})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}\mathcal{N},

is the image under
I:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
of a Banach space. Furthermore, as
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
is noetherian, it follows that
\mathcal{N}_{m}
is a finitely generated
I(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{m})
-module. As a consequence of the snake Lemma, we have an isomorphism:

\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\varprojlim_{m}\mathcal{N}_{m},

and then it follows by
[
8
, Corollary 5.40]

that
\mathcal{N}
is the image under
I
of a co-admissible module, which we will also denote by
\mathcal{N}
for simplicity. Thus, we obtain a degree one Yoneda extension:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow 0,

and it follows by Proposition
3.2.7
that this process establishes a bijection between the two sets of isomorphism classes of first order Yoneda extensions.

In the general case, the situation is not so simple. In particular, a
n
-th degree Yoneda extension of
I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
by
I(\mathcal{M})
is a complex of the following form:

0\rightarrow I(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{n-1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{0}\rightarrow I(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))\rightarrow 0,

and it is not clear that the
\mathcal{N}_{i}
are co-admissible modules. In fact, it is not even clear that they are objects in

\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. Hence, the identification of the parameter spaces for isomorphism classes of
n
-th degree Yoneda extensions:

\operatorname{Ext}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}^{n}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\operatorname{Loc}(\mathcal{M}))=\operatorname{Ext}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}^{n}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}),

is only accessible via the means of homological algebra. This showcases an algebraic application of Hochschild cohomology to the study of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Furthermore, this type of behavior resembles the properties of coherent modules in the theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules on smooth algebraic
K
-varieties, and reinforces the idea that co-admissible modules are the right
p
-adic analog of coherent
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules.

4.3.
Hochschild cohomology and extensions II

Recall from Section
2.3
that given a rigid space
X
and a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras
\mathscr{A}
, we define the sheaf of enveloping algebras of
\mathscr{A}
as the following sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
:

\mathscr{A}^{e}:=\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}.

If
X=\operatorname{Sp}(K)
, we will call
\mathscr{A}^{e}
the enveloping algebra of
\mathscr{A}
. Let
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
be a smooth Stein space admitting an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, and let
\wideparen{E}_{X}
be the sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras of
X
. Recall that this is a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X^{2}:=X\times_{\operatorname{Sp}(K)}X
, and it follows by the contents of
[
34
, Section 5.4]
that we have the following identity of Ind-Banach algebras:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\operatorname{op}}=\wideparen{E}_{X}(X^{2}).

We remark that this notation is also used in a slightly different context. In particular, if
R
is a commutative algebra and
A
is a
R
-algebra, we define its enveloping algebra as:

A^{e}=A\otimes_{R}A^{\operatorname{op}}.

As Ind-Banach spaces do not have an underlying
K
-vector space, there is no confusion possible between these two concepts. Hence, we will use this terminology for both of them, and the type of object we are working with will make clear which one of the two we are referring to.

Notice that the key to the results of the previous section is the fact that we have a resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
by locally finite-free
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Indeed, the existence of the Spencer resolution allowed us to give explicit calculations of several derived functors, and we used this to show a bunch of identities in the corresponding derived categories. Consider the diagonal morphism:

\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}.

As shown in
[
34
]
, the pushforward
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is canonically a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module, and we have an identification of derived functors:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Delta^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}).

The goal of this section is obtaining an interpretation of the Hochschild cohomology groups
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
as isomorphism classes of Yoneda extensions of
I(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
by
I(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. The idea is trying to replicate the arguments we used in the proof of Theorem
4.2.9
. Ideally, we would want to show that there is a resolution of
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
by finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules. Unfortunately, we were not able to show that this holds in general. However, we will show a slightly weaker statement, which will be enough for our purposes. Notice that
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module. Hence, by Proposition
3.2.7
, it is enough to show that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
admits a resolution by finite-projective
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
-modules. Notice that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is a finitely generated
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
-module. However, as
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
is not noetherian, it is not immediate that the canonical epimorphism:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),

extends to a finite-projective resolution. In order to solve this issue, we will first construct the resolution in a setting where the relevant rings are noetherian, and then obtain our desired resolution via a series of pullbacks.

A distinguished class of smooth Stein spaces is given by the rigid-analytification of smooth affine varieties (cf.
[
11
, Section 5.4]
). Given a smooth algebraic
K
-variety
X
, we let
X^{\operatorname{an}}
be its rigid-analytification. The properties of the rigid analytic GAGA functor can be used to study the properties of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}
-modules using
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules, where some of the considerations are simpler. This approach was initiated in
[
36
, Section 4]
, and it is the strategy we will follow here. For simplicity in the notation, we will write
\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
to denote the algebraic variety and the associated rigid variety. The context will make clear which one of the two we are referring to.

Let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Assume
K
is discretely valued and let
X
be a smooth algebraic
K
-variety with free tangent sheaf. In this situation the canonical map:

\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{e}\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}})^{e},

is faithfully flat and has dense image.

Proof.

As
X
has free tangent sheaf, it follows that
X^{\operatorname{an}}
also has free tangent sheaf. Thus, we may use the isomorphisms:

\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\operatorname{op}},\quad\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}})\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}})^{\operatorname{op}},

from
[
34
, Proposition 6.2.1]
to reduce the problem to showing that the canonical map:

\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{2}:=\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\otimes_{K}\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2,\operatorname{an}}}(X^{2,\operatorname{an}}),

is faithfully flat and has dense image. This was shown in
[
36
, Corollary 4.4.10]
.
∎

We may use this lemma to take the first step towards our desired resolution:

Lemma 4.3.2
.

\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}
admits a resolution of length
2r
by finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}
-modules.

Proof.

In order to simplify notation and to avoid ambiguities, we will write
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})
to denote the filtered
K
-algebra
U(\operatorname{Der}_{K}(K[t_{1},\cdots,t_{r}]))
. That is,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})
is the algebra of differential operators associated to the scheme structure of
\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. By Proposition
3.2.7
, it is enough to show that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})
admits a resolution by finite-projective
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
-modules. Assume first that
K
is discretely valued. Notice that we have a canonical surjection:

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}).

In particular,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})
is finitely generated as a
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
-module. As mentioned above, the fact that
X
has free tangent sheaf implies that we have an isomorphism of
K
-algebras:

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}.

Hence, we may apply
[
23
, Theorem 2.6.11]
to conclude that there is a resolution:

P^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P^{0}\rightarrow 0\right),

of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})
such that each
P^{i}

is a finite-projective
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
-module. By Lemma
4.3.1
, the map
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
is faithfully flat with dense image. Hence, the complex:

\wideparen{P}^{\bullet}:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}}P^{\bullet},

is a resolution by finite-projective
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
-modules of length
2r
of the following
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
-module:

D:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}).

By the extension-restriction adjunction, we get a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
-linear map:

D\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}),

and we need to show it is an isomorphism. Consider the following family of closed disks indexed by non-negative integers
n\geq 0
:

\mathbb{B}^{r,n}_{K}=\operatorname{Sp}(K\langle\pi^{n}t_{1},\cdots,\pi^{n}t_{r}\rangle).

Then
\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}=\varinjlim_{n}\mathbb{B}^{r,n}_{K}
is a Stein covering of
\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
as a rigid variety. For each
n\geq 0
, let:

A_{n}:=K\langle\pi^{n}t_{1},\cdots,\pi^{n}t_{r}\rangle,

and consider the family of affine formal models:

\mathcal{A}_{n}:=\mathcal{R}\langle\pi^{n}t_{1},\cdots,\pi^{n}t_{r}\rangle\subset A_{n},

which satisfy that the
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module
\mathcal{T}_{n}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{n}\partial_{i}
is a finite-free
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-Lie lattice of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}/K}(\mathbb{B}^{r,n}_{K})
.

As
D
and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})
are co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}
-modules, it is enough to show that the map:

\varphi_{n}:\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{T}_{n})_{K}^{e}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})^{e}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})\rightarrow\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{T}_{n})_{K},

is an
\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{T}^{n})^{e}
-linear isomorphism for every
n\geq 0
. We will only show the case
n=0
, the rest is analogous. Consider the algebras
A=K[t_{1},\cdots,t_{r}]
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{R}[t_{1},\cdots,t_{r}]
. Notice that
\mathcal{A}
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{R}
-algebra satisfying that:

A=K\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{A},\quad\mathcal{A}_{0}=\varprojlim\mathcal{R}[t_{1},\cdots,t_{r}]/\pi^{m}.

Furthermore, the following identities hold:

\mathcal{T}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}\partial_{i}=\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{A}),\quad K\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{T}=\operatorname{Der}_{K}(A).

Thus, we have
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K})=K\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}U(\mathcal{T})
, and
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathcal{A}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{T}
. Therefore, there is a map:

\widetilde{\varphi}:\left(\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0})\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0})^{\operatorname{op}}\right)\otimes_{U(\mathcal{T})^{e}}U(\mathcal{T})\rightarrow\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0}),

satisfying
\varphi=\operatorname{Id}_{K}\otimes\widetilde{\varphi}
. Hence, it is enough to show that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is an isomorphism. As both sides are
\pi
-adically complete, it suffices to show that the map is an isomorphism after taking quotients by high enough powers of
\pi
. Chose some
m\geq 0
. Then we have the following identities of
\mathcal{R}
-modules:

\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0})/\pi^{m}=U(\mathcal{T}_{0})/\pi^{m}=\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}U(\mathcal{T})\right)\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}/\pi^{m}=U(\mathcal{T})/\pi^{m},

where the second identity follows from the PBW Theorem for Lie-Rinehart algebras. On the other hand, we have the following chain of identities of
\mathcal{R}
-modules:

\displaystyle\left(\left(\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0})\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0})^{\operatorname{op}}\right)\otimes_{U(\mathcal{T})^{e}}U(\mathcal{T})\right)\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}/\pi^{m}=\left(\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0})\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\widehat{U}(\mathcal{T}_{0})^{\operatorname{op}}\right)/\pi^{m}\otimes_{\left(U(\mathcal{T})/\pi^{m}\right)^{e}}U(\mathcal{T})/\pi^{m}

\displaystyle=\left(U(\mathcal{T}_{0})/\pi^{m}\right)^{e}\otimes_{\left(U(\mathcal{T})/\pi^{m}\right)^{e}}U(\mathcal{T})/\pi^{m}

\displaystyle=U(\mathcal{T})/\pi^{m}.

Thus,
\widetilde{\varphi}
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{R}
-algebras, as we wanted to show. For general
K
, the result follows by taking a complete discretely valued subfield
Q\subset K
and then using the identity:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{Q}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{Q})=Q\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}),

together with the fact that
Q\widehat{\otimes}_{K}-
preserves acyclic complexes of Fréchet
K
-vector spaces.
∎

Finally, we can use these lemmas to obtained our fabled resolution:

Proposition 4.3.3
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid space with an étale map
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. There is a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that there is a strict exact complex:

E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}:=\left(0\rightarrow P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0\right),

where each
P^{i}
is a finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module and
P^{0}=\wideparen{E}_{X}
.

Proof.

By the side-switching Theorem for co-admissible bimodules
[
34
, Theorem 6.3.6]
, together with the fact that
\operatorname{S}^{-1}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}
, it is enough to show that there is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-module
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{M}\oplus\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}
admits a resolution by finite-projective
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-modules. By Lemma
4.3.2
, there is a strict exact complex of finite-projective
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}
-modules
P^{\bullet}
of the following form:

P^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}\rightarrow\Delta_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}\rightarrow 0\right).

As étale maps are stable under base-change and composition, the product
f\times f:X^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}
is also an étale map. We claim that the extraordinary inverse image
\left(f\times f\right)^{!}(P^{\bullet})
yields the desired complex. First, notice that as
f\times f:X^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}
is étale, we have a canonical isomorphism
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
. Furthermore, by
[
8
, Theorem 9.11]
for any co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}
-module
\mathcal{N}
we have:

\left(f\times f\right)^{!}\mathcal{N}=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\left(f\times f\right)^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}}\left(f\times f\right)^{-1}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\left(f\times f\right)^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}}\left(f\times f\right)^{-1}\mathcal{N}.

As
P^{\bullet}
is an exact complex of co-admissible modules, it follows that
\left(f\times f\right)^{!}(P^{\bullet})
is also an exact complex of co-admissible modules. Hence, we have:

\left(f\times f\right)^{!}(P^{\bullet})=\left(0\rightarrow\left(f\times f\right)^{!}P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\rightarrow\left(f\times f\right)^{!}\Delta_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}\rightarrow 0\right).

Consider the following fiber product:

Y:=X^{2}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}.

By properties of the pullback, the projection
\alpha:Y\rightarrow X^{2}
is a closed immersion, and the projection
\beta:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
is an étale map. Furthermore, the maps
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
, and
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, induce a map:

X\rightarrow Y,

which is an étale closed immersion. Hence,
X
is a union of connected components of
Y
. We let
Z=Y\setminus X
be the union of the other connected components of
Y
, and set
\mathcal{M}=\alpha_{+}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
.

As above, we have:

\left(f\times f\right)^{!}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\left(f\times f\right)^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}}}\left(f\times f\right)^{-1}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}.

In particular, as
\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}
has support contained in the diagonal of

\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}
, the co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-module
\left(f\times f\right)^{!}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}
has support contained in
Y
. By Kashiwara’s equivalence, we have:

\left(f\times f\right)^{!}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}=\alpha_{+}\alpha^{!}\left(f\times f\right)^{!}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}=\alpha_{+}\beta^{!}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}}=\alpha_{+}\mathcal{O}_{Y}=\alpha_{+}\mathcal{O}_{Z}\oplus\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}.

Thus,
\left(f\times f\right)^{!}(P^{\bullet})
is a resolution of
\mathcal{M}\oplus\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}
by finite-projective
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-modules, as wanted.
∎

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be an affinoid space and
\mathcal{A}\subset A
be an affine formal model of
A
. Assume
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
admits a free
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{L}
satisfying the following two properties:

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\subset\pi\mathcal{A},\quad[\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}]\subset\pi^{2}\mathcal{L}.

In this situation, the following inequality holds for each
n\geq 0
:

\operatorname{gl.dim.}(\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K})\leq 2\operatorname{dim}(X).

Furthermore, if
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K})
satisfies that it is projective as an Ind-Banach space, then
M
admits a projective resolution of
\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K}
-modules of length
2\operatorname{dim}(X)
.

Proof.

For simplicity, let
B_{n}:=\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K}
, and
B:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. In virtue of
[
2
, Theorem 4.1.11]
, our assumptions on
\mathcal{L}
imply that we have a Fréchet-Stein presentation
B=\varprojlim_{n}B_{n}
. Similarly,
[
34
, Corollary 6.2.6]
implies that
B^{e}=\varprojlim_{n}B_{n}^{e}
is also a Fréchet-Stein presentation. In this situation,
B
is a co-admissible
B^{e}
-module, and we have identities:

B_{n}=B_{n}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{B^{e}}B,

for each
n\geq 0
(see
[
34
, Section 7]
for details). Let
E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}
be the complex obtained in Proposition
4.3.3
and fix some
n\geq 0
. As
E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}
is a strict and exact complex of co-admissible
B^{e}
-modules, it remains strict exact after applying the functor
B_{n}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{B^{e}}-
. Thus, the fact that
B_{n}^{e}
is noetherian and
B_{n}
is finite implies that
B_{n}
admits a resolution of length
2\operatorname{dim}(X)
by finite-projective
B_{n}^{e}
-modules.

Let
P^{\bullet}
be such a resolution. Notice that the algebra
B_{n}
is a Banach space which has a dense countably generated vector subspace. Hence, by
[
44
, Proposition 10.4]
, and
[
8
, Lemma 4.16]
it is a projective object in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. In particular, the algebra
B_{n}^{e}:=B_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B_{n}^{\operatorname{op}}
is a projective object viewed as a right
B_{n}
-module. Therefore, every finite-projective
B_{n}^{e}
-module is a fortiori a projective right
B_{n}
-module. It follows that
P^{\bullet}

is a resolution of
B_{n}
by projective
B_{n}
-modules. Thus, for any finitely generated left
B_{n}
-module
M
we have the following identities in the derived category
\operatorname{D}(B_{n})
:

M=B_{n}\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{B_{n}}M=P^{\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{n}}M.

In particular,
P^{\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{n}}M
is a resolution of
B_{n}
-modules of
M
of length
2\operatorname{dim}(X)
. However, using the same arguments as above,
M
is a projective object in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, and therefore the left
B_{n}
-module
B_{n}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{n}}M=B_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}M
is also projective. Hence, if
P
is a finite-projective
B_{n}^{e}
-module, then
P\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{n}}M
is a projective left
B_{n}
-module. We have thus shown that
P^{\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{n}}M
is a projective resolution of
M
of length
2\operatorname{dim}(X)
. As
B_{n}
is noetherian and
M
is finitely generated, this implies that
M
admits a resolution by finite-projective
B_{n}
-modules of length
2\operatorname{dim}(X)
. Hence, we get
\operatorname{gl.dim.}(B_{n})\leq 2\operatorname{dim}(X)
, as we wanted to show. The same arguments as above show the claim at the end of the corollary.
∎

Remark 4.3.5
.

For the rest of the section we assume
K
is discretely valued or algebraically closed.

Even though the resolution we obtained is slightly weaker than the result we had in the previous section, it is still enough to deduce the results we are looking for:

Theorem 4.3.6
.

Let
X
be a Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. For any co-admissible module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\Gamma(X^{2},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}))=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M})).

In particular, we have the following identity:

(18)

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=:\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module. As
X^{2}
is a smooth Stein space with an étale map
X^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2r}_{K}
, it follows by Lemma
4.2.2
that we have an identification of functors:

\Gamma(X^{2},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathcal{N},-))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{N}),\Gamma(X^{2},-)).

Thus, for each
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
we have a natural map in the homotopy category
\operatorname{K}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
:

\psi_{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}}:R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{N}),\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M}))\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{2},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})).

We need to show that this is a quasi-isomorphism for
\mathcal{N}=\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
and every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
.

Until the end of the proof, let
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. By Proposition
4.3.3
, there is a complex of finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules
P^{\bullet}
with a quasi-isomorphism
P^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
. Notice that both complexes are complexes of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules. Hence, this quasi-isomorphism is automatically strict by
[
8
, Corollary 5.15]
. Thus, we have the following chain of identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\displaystyle R\Gamma(X^{2},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{M}))=R\Gamma(X^{2},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(P^{\bullet},\mathcal{M}))=\Gamma(X^{2},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(P^{\bullet},\mathcal{M}))

\displaystyle=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\Gamma(X^{2},P^{\bullet}),\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M}))

\displaystyle=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{T}),\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M})),

where we make extensive use of the fact that co-admissible modules are acyclic for the global sections functor, and finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules are acyclic for the
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(-,\mathcal{M})
-functor. Thus, it follows that
\psi_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{M}}
is a quasi-isomorphism for every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
.

Notice that both the global sections functor and its associated derived functor commute with finite direct sums. Similarly, both
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(-,-)
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(-,-)
and its derived functors commute with finite direct sums on the first variable. As

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, it follows that:

\psi_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{M}}=\psi_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{M}}\oplus\psi_{\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}},

for every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
. This implies that
\psi_{\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}}
is also a quasi-isomorphism, as wanted.

In order to show (
18
), we consider the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}):=R\Gamma(X,R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{e}_{X}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=R\Gamma(X,\Delta^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))

\displaystyle=R\Gamma(X^{2},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))

\displaystyle=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))

\displaystyle=:\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

where the second identity is
[
34
, Proposition 3.5.5]
, the third one follows by
[
34
, Lemma 3.5.3]
, and the fourth one is a special case of the first part of the theorem.
∎

Hence, in the case of Stein spaces with free tangent sheaf, we deduce that the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is completely determined by the Hochschild cohomology of its global sections
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
.

As in the previous section, we can also give a version for the external sheaves of homomorphisms:

Theorem 4.3.7
.

Let
X
be a Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. For any co-admissible module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M})=R\Gamma(X^{2},R\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M})).

Hence, we have the following identities:

(19)

\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}).

Proof.

Lemmas
4.2.5
, and
4.2.8
hold for finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules. Thus, the identities:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M})=R\Gamma(X^{2},R\mathcal{H}om_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M})),

can be deduced using the arguments from the proof of Theorem
4.3.6
. Hence, we only have to show that (
19
) holds. The second identity in (
19
) is a special case of the first part of the theorem. For the first one, consider the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

\displaystyle R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\mathcal{S}_{X}(X^{2})\oplus\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\Gamma(X^{2},E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))

\displaystyle=\left(0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n_{2r}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow 0\right)

\displaystyle=\left(0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(K,\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n_{2r}}K,\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow 0\right)

\displaystyle=\left(0\rightarrow\operatorname{Forget}(JI(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n_{2r}}\operatorname{Forget}(JI(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))\rightarrow 0\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Forget}(J\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\Gamma(X^{2},E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Forget}(JR\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\mathcal{S}_{X}(X^{2})\oplus\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))),

where we have made extensive use of Lemma
4.2.6
. Notice that, by construction,
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
is the image under the dissection functor of a Fréchet algebra. Regard for a moment
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
as an algebra in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. Then we have the following identity of functors
\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}
:

\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})}(I(-),I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))=\operatorname{Forget}(J\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})}(I(-),I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))).

Hence, we can use the fact that
\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
has enough projective objects, and that all functors in the above equation commute with finite direct sums to obtain the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{Forget}(JR\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)))=\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})),

and this is precisely what we wanted to show. Thus, the theorem holds.
∎

Remark 4.3.8
.

We remark that the results thus far also hold if
X
is an affinoid space with free tangent sheaf and
K
is an arbitrary complete non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

In conclusion, we have been able to obtain a direct relation between the Hochschild cohomology groups and the Yoneda extensions of
I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{e}
-modules of
I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))

by
I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. Furthermore, the isomorphism classes of such extensions are in one-to-one correspondence with the extensions classes at the level of sheaves. This relation will be further explored in Chapter
6
, where we will study the deformation theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
.

We finish the section by analyzing the case of rigid-analytifications of smooth algebraic varieties:

Corollary 4.3.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth affine algebraic variety with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. Then
X^{\operatorname{an}}
is a Smooth Stein space with an étale map
X^{\operatorname{an}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, and we have:

\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}))=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X^{\operatorname{an}})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X),\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))=:\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)).

In particular, the Hochschild complex
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}})
has finite-dimensional cohomology.

Proof.

The first identity was shown in Theorem
4.1.2
, the second one is
[
20
, Theorem 3.2]
, and the third one is
[
14
, Proposition 2.3]
. The last identity is just the definition of the Hochschild cohomology of an abstract
K
-algebra (see, for example
[
51
, Remark 1.1.14]
). The fact that the de Rham cohomology groups of
X
are finite dimensional is a classical result. It can be found in
[
33
]
.
∎

Combining this theorem with
[
14
, Proposition 2.1]
, we get the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}})}(\mathcal{O}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}}),\mathcal{O}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}}))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)).

Thus, these
Ext
functors are preserved by the rigid-analytic GAGA functor.

4.4.
Lie-Rinehart cohomology

We finish the chapter by giving a final interpretation of the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Recall the definition of Lie-Rinehart cohomology:

Definition 4.4.1

(
[
39
, Section 4]
)
.

Let
A
be a
K
-algebra. For a
(K,A)
-Lie algebra
L
, and a
L
-representation
M
, we define the Lie-Rinehart cohomology of
L
with coefficients in
M
as:

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{A}(L,M)=R\operatorname{Hom}_{U(L)}(A,M).

For
A=K
, we recover the notion of Lie algebra cohomology (cf.
[
50
, Section 7]
). If
X
is a smooth rigid analytic space, then
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
is canonically a Lie algebroid on
\mathcal{O}_{X}
. In other words,
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
is a finite locally-free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module which is also a sheaf of
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X})
-Lie algebras. Hence, it make sense to consider the following cohomology groups:

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{T}_{X/K},\mathcal{M}):=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X),\mathcal{M}(X))=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X)),

where
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. We may relate this cohomology theory to
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
as follows:

Proposition 4.4.2
.

Let
X
be a Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. For any co-admissible module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X))=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{T}_{X/K},\mathcal{M}).

In particular, we have
\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{dR}(X)=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{T}_{X/K},\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.2.9
and its proof, we have:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\Gamma(X,S^{\bullet}),\mathcal{M}(X)),

where
S^{\bullet}
is the Spencer resolution. By construction,
S^{\bullet}
is a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module generalization of the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex (cf.
[
39
, Section 4]
), which exists for every smooth Lie-Rinehart algebra. In the case of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
, the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex has the following form:

C^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow 0\right),

and is a resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
by finite-free
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
-modules. In this situation, we have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{T}_{X/K},\mathcal{O}_{X}):=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)}(C^{\bullet},\mathcal{M}(X))

\displaystyle=\left(0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{M}(X))\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K},\mathcal{M}(X))\rightarrow 0\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}(\Gamma(X,S^{\bullet}),\mathcal{M}(X)).

Thus, the proposition holds.
∎

We point out that this corollary is similar to the results of
[
41
]
in the context of Arens-Michael envelops of finite dimensional reductive
K
-Lie algebras. In particular, check
[
41
, Corollary 2.2]
.

The previous results show that the complex
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{T}_{X/K},\mathcal{O}_{X})
has a natural structure as a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. Hence, it would be interesting to adapt the results of
[
30
]
to the setting of smooth Stein spaces. We believe that some of the requirements of finite generation on the algebras could be bypassed by analytic methods. We do not pursue this study here.

5.
Duality results

Let
X
be either an affinoid space or a
p
-adic Stein space, and assume
X
has free tangent sheaf. The goal of this section is showing that the Hochschild (co)-homology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-bimodules satisfies a version of Poincaré duality. Namely, we will show that for any complex
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X^{2},\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathscr{M})=\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{L}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Similarly, for any complex
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})
there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}^{L}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}\mathscr{M}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

This type of result is often times called Van den Bergh duality, as it was first obtained in the setting of associative algebras by M. Van den Bergh in
[
48
]
. Notice that one of the most important features of this result is that it does not impose any finiteness conditions on
\mathscr{M}
. In particular, we do not require that
\mathscr{M}
is a
\mathcal{C}
-complex. In latter stages of the section, we will use Van den Bergh duality to translate the results of Section
4.3
to Hochschild homology. In particular, we will show that there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=:\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Thus obtaining an interpretation of
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
in terms of Tor functors, and calculating
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
.

5.1.
Review of operations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules

Let
X
be a smooth rigid analytic space. We will devote this section to reviewing some basic operations on the category of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Most of the constructions in this section are due to A. Bode, and a detailed description of the properties of these operations can be found in his papers
[
8
]
and
[
9
]
. Let us start by introducing the duality functor:

Definition 5.1.1
.

We define the duality functor as the following functor:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})^{\operatorname{op}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{M})=

\displaystyle R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega_{X}^{-1}[\operatorname{dim}(X)],

where
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega_{X}^{-1}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is the side-changing operator from
[
8
, Section 6.3]
.

The duality functor is an extremely important part of the theory of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules, as it is a key ingredient in the
p
-adic analytic version of holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules. Namely, the weakly holonomic
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules defined in
[
4
]
. A co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is called weakly holonomic if its dual complex
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{M})
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module. That is,
\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{M})
is concentrated in degree
0
, and its only non-trivial cohomology group is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module. Recall as well that a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module is called an integrable connection if it is coherent as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. To us, the most important results regarding these two concepts is the following proposition:

Proposition 5.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an integrable connection on
X
. Then
\mathcal{M}
is weakly co-admissible. Furthermore, there is a canonical identification of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules:

\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{M})=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{O}_{X}).

In particular, we have
\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
8
, Proposition 7.3]
.
∎

Let now
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of smooth rigid analytic spaces. So far, we have only used two operations associated to this morphism. Namely, the
extraordinary inverse image functor:

\displaystyle f^{!}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\mapsto f^{!}\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}f^{-1}\mathcal{M}[\operatorname{dim}(X)-\operatorname{dim}(Y)]

and the direct image functor:

\displaystyle f_{+}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}).

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\mapsto Rf_{*}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y\leftarrow X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{M})

Using the duality functor, we can obtain new versions of these morphisms:

Definition 5.1.3
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of smooth rigid analytic spaces. We define the following functors:

(i)

The extraordinary direct image functor:
f_{!}:=\mathbb{D}\circ f_{+}\circ\mathbb{D}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})
.

(ii)

The inverse image functor:
f^{+}:=\mathbb{D}\circ f^{!}\circ\mathbb{D}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
.

For us, the most important feature of these operations is the following proposition:

Proposition 5.1.4
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a closed immersion of smooth rigid analytic spaces, and let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
be weakly holonomic. Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
-modules:

f_{+}\mathcal{M}=f_{!}\mathcal{M}.

In particular, we have
\mathbb{D}f_{+}\mathcal{M}=f_{+}\mathbb{D}\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

As the claim is purely local, we may start by assuming that
X
and
Y
are affinoid. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a weakly holonomic
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module. We claim that the image under
f_{+}
of a weakly holonomic
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module is a weakly holonomic
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
-module. In particular,
f_{!}\mathcal{M}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
-module. Indeed this is
[
4
, Lemma 7.1]
in the case where
K
is discretely valued, and follows by the contents of
[
9
, Section 5.2]
, and in particular
[
9
, Lemma 5.10]
, in the general case.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{X}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})
be the category if co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
-modules supported on
X
, and let
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{C}_{X}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})
. By the version of Kashiwara’s equivalence given in
[
34
, Proposition 8.1.1]
together with
[
9
, Theorem 1.3]
, for every co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
-module
\mathcal{N}
supported on
X
we have:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}}(f_{+}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=f_{*}\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}}(\mathcal{M},f^{!}\mathcal{N})=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}}(f_{!}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}).

Hence, the global sections of these sheaves are isomorphic. As both
f_{+}\mathcal{M}
and
f_{!}\mathcal{M}
are co-admissible, it follows by Lemma
4.2.2
that there is an isomorphism of complete bornological spaces:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}(Y)}(f_{+}\mathcal{M}(Y),\mathcal{N}(Y))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}(Y)}(f_{!}\mathcal{M}(Y),\mathcal{N}(Y)),

Thus, it follows that there is an isomorphism of functors:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}}(f_{+}\mathcal{M},-)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}}(f_{!}\mathcal{M},-):\mathcal{C}_{X}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})\rightarrow\operatorname{Set},

and it follows by Yoneda’s lemma that
f_{+}\mathcal{M}=f_{!}\mathcal{M}
, as we wanted to show.
∎

Corollary 5.1.5
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space. Then the following isomorphism holds in
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}})
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{E}_{X})=\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)]

Proof.

We start by noticing that by the side-switching theorem
[
34
, Proposition 6.1.16]
and the previous proposition we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X^{2},\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{E}_{X})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}(\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})=\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\Omega_{X^{2}}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)],

where the last identity follows by Proposition
5.1.2
. Thus, we have shown that
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{E}_{X})
is a complex in
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}})
concentrated in degree
2\operatorname{dim}(X)
. In order to see that this is isomorphic as a right
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module to
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)]
, we again apply the side-changing isomorphism for right modules, as in
[
34
, pp. 59]
.
∎

Using the results from Section
2.3
, we can show the following variant:

Corollary 5.1.6
.

Assume
X
is an affinoid space or
X
is a Stein space and
K
is discretely valued or algebraically closed. If
X
has free tangent sheaf, then the following isomorphism holds in
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})
:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Proof.

Under our assumption of freeness of the tangent sheaf, the corollary follows by the previous proposition, together with Theorem
4.3.6
.
∎

5.2.
Van den Bergh duality

After the calculations performed in the previous section, we are almost ready to show Van den Bergh duality. Until further notice, we let
X
be a separated rigid space, and assume that it admits an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. As shown in Proposition
4.3.3
, this implies that there is a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that there is a strict exact sequence:

E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}:=\left(0\rightarrow P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0\right),

where each of the
P^{i}
is a finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module and
P^{1}=\wideparen{E}_{X}
. The existence of this complex will be instrumental in our proof of Van den Bergh duality. Let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 5.2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X^{2},\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathscr{M})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{E}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}.

Furthermore, if
X
is affinoid or
X
is Stein and
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, then for any
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})
there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}\mathscr{M}.

Proof.

We only show the first case, the second one is analogous. First, notice that for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
there is always a natural transformation:

\varphi(-):R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(-,\wideparen{E}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}\rightarrow R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(-,\mathscr{M}).

We need to show that this is an isomorphism for
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. As the functors commute with finite direct sums, it suffices to show this for
\mathcal{N}:=\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Consider the following strict exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}\rightarrow 0,

where
\wideparen{E}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is the first morphism in
E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}
. The natural transformation
\varphi
induces a morphism of distinguished triangles:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathcal{N},\wideparen{E}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\wideparen{E}_{X},\wideparen{E}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathcal{K},\wideparen{E}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathcal{N},\mathscr{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\wideparen{E}_{X},\mathscr{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathcal{K},\mathscr{M})}}

and the middle map is always a quasi-isomorphism. Thus, it suffices to show that
\varphi(\mathcal{K})
is a quasi-isomorphism. However, by construction,
\mathcal{K}
admits a strict resolution by finite-projective
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules of length
2\operatorname{dim(X)}-1
. Hence, we can repeat this process inductively to show that
\varphi(\mathcal{K})
is a quasi-isomorphism, and therefore
\varphi(\mathcal{N})
is a quasi-isomorphism, as we wanted to show.
∎

We remark that if
\mathscr{M}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module, then the previous result is a special instance of
[
9
, Proposition 6.19]
. The relevance of this lemma is that it enables us to also handle the case when
\mathscr{M}
is not co-admissible. The importance of this stems from the fact that the deformation theory of a co-admissible module is parameterized by the Hochschild cohomology with values in a bimodule which is far from co-admissible, and so we need homological tools to also deal with this fact. See
[
40
, Section 3.9]
for a discussion of this in the case of associative
K
-algebras. After all this work has been done, the proof of Van den Bergh duality reduces to a simple formal computation:

Theorem 5.2.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. For any
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X^{2},\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathscr{M})=\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Furthermore, if
X
is affinoid or
X
is Stein and
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, then for any
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})
there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{H}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

In particular, for each
n\geq 0
there is an isomorphism in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{HH}_{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})

Proof.

Again, we only deal with the first case, the second is analogous. Let
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
. Then by Corollary
5.1.5
, and Lemma
5.2.1
we have the following isomorphisms in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X^{2},\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathscr{M})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{E}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}=\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}\mathscr{M}[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)],

and this is precisely the identity we wanted to show. The last part follows from the identities:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{H}^{n-2\operatorname{dim}(X)}(\operatorname{H}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M}))=:\operatorname{HH}_{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\mathscr{M}),

where the last identity follows from our conventions regarding the degree of Hochschild homology.
∎

Before moving on, we would like to put the results of this section into perspective. In particular let
X
be either affinoid or Stein, and assume it has free tangent sheaf. The two key ingredients for the proof of Van den Bergh duality of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
are the existence of the strict exact complex:

E^{\bullet}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}:=\left(0\rightarrow P^{2r}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{X}\oplus\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0\right),

and the identity of graded
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
-modules:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)[-2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

In the setting of associative
K
-algebras, these two properties can be encapsulated as follows:

Definition 5.2.3
.

Let
A
be an associative
K
-algebra. We make the following definitions:

(i)

A
is homologically smooth if it admits a finite resolution by finite projective
A^{e}
-modules.

(ii)

A
is a Calabi-Yau algebra of dimension
d
if it is homologically smooth and there is an isomorphism of graded
A^{e}
-modules:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}(A,A^{e})\cong A[-d].

Calabi-Yau algebras were introduced by V. Ginzburg in
[
18
]
in the context of differential graded algebras as a non-commutative generalization of the algebras of regular functions on affine Calabi-Yau varieties. These algebras are a central object in the deformation theory of associative algebras and have multiple applications to non-commutative geometry. See, for example
[
40
]
for an overview of the subject. In the context of Hochschild (co)-homology of associative algebras, one of the most important features of Calabi-Yau algebras is that they always satisfy Van den Bergh duality. In particular, the proofs given above carry over to the setting of bimodules over an associative algebra without significant changes. Notice that the definition of homologically smooth algebra can be reformulated in more categorical terms. Let us start by recalling the following definitions:

Definition 5.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category with arbitrary direct sums. We say an object
X
in
\mathcal{A}
is compact if for every direct sum
\bigoplus_{i\in I}Y_{i}
the canonical map:

\bigoplus_{i\in I}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,V_{i})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i}),

is an isomorphism of sets.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category. Then its derived category
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
is an additive category with direct sums. Hence, we can study the compact objects in
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
. Furthermore, in most situations these objects are completely determined by the compact objects in
\mathcal{A}
:

Lemma 5.2.5

(
[
52
, Tag 0948]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category, and let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of objects of
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

Every object of
\mathcal{A}
is a quotient of a direct sum of elements of
\mathcal{S}
.

(ii)

Every object of
\mathcal{S}
is compact.

Then every compact object of
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
is a direct summand in
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{A})
of a finite complex of finite direct sums of elements of
\mathcal{S}
.

Thus, if
A
is an associative
K
-algebra and
A^{e}

is its enveloping algebra, then the fact that finite-projective
A^{e}
-modules form a system of compact projective generators of
\operatorname{Mod}(A^{e})
implies that
A
is homologically smooth if and only if
A
is a compact object of
D(A^{e})
.

In the bornological setting the situation is much more subtle. Indeed, let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra, and assume for simplicity that
\mathscr{A}
is Fréchet. In this situation, the Grothendieck abelian category
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})^{e})
is no longer generated by finite-projective
I(\mathscr{A}^{e})
-modules. Instead, it admits a system of compact projective generators of the form
I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(V)
, where
V
is a Banach
K
-vector space admitting an orthonormal basis. In this situation, we may again invoke
[
52
, Tag 0948]
, and it follows that
\mathscr{A}
is a compact object in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
if and only if
\mathscr{A}
is a direct summand in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
of a finite complex of finite direct sums of elements of the form

I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(V)
. However, the natural map:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})^{e}}(I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(V),I(\mathscr{A})^{e})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{I(\mathscr{A})^{e}}\mathscr{M}\rightarrow R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})^{e}}(I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(V),\mathscr{M}),

is not a quasi-isomorphism for general
\mathscr{M}
unless
V
is a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space. Hence,
\mathscr{A}
may not satisfy Van den Bergh duality even if
\mathscr{A}
is a compact object in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
, and there is some non-negative integer
d\geq 0
such that:

(20)

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A}^{e})\cong\mathscr{A}[-d].

In other words, one cannot directly translate the notion of a Calabi-Yau algebra to the complete bornological setting and expect to retain the good homological properties. Nevertheless, our results thus far can be rephrased by saying that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is a complete bornological version of a Calabi-Yau algebra. It would perhaps be of interest to study if the techniques from the theory of Calabi-Yau algebras can be applied to the study of the algebras
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
and their deformation theory.

Remark
.

Notice that if
\mathscr{A}^{e}
is noetherian and Banach (or nuclear Fréchet), then the fact that
\mathscr{A}
is a finite
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module shows that
\mathscr{A}
is a compact object in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{e})

if and only if
\mathscr{A}
admits a finite strict resolution by finite-projective
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-modules. In this situation, if (
20
) holds for some
d\geq 0
, then
\mathscr{A}
satisfies Van den Bergh duality.

5.3.
Hochschild homology

We end the section by establishing an analog of Theorem
4.3.6
for Hochschild homology of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules:

Theorem 5.3.1
.

Assume
X
is affinoid or
X
is Stein and
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed. If
X
admits an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, then for any co-admissible module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\Gamma(X^{2},\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{M})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M}).

In particular, we have the following identity:

(21)

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})[2\operatorname{dim}(X)]=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=:\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

This follows by applying the two different forms of Van den Bergh duality in Theorem
5.2.2
, together with Theorem
4.3.6
. Indeed, let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
, we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\Gamma(X^{2},\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{M})=R\Gamma(X^{2},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathscr{M}))[2\operatorname{dim}(X)]\\
=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}))[2\operatorname{dim}(X)]=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma(X^{2},\mathcal{M}),

and this is precisely the identity we wanted to show. The second part follows by the fact that
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-module, together with Theorem
2.3.3
.
∎

Thus, we obtain a new interpretation of
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
in terms of Tor functors. Furthermore, in both the cases we are interested in, the complex
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
is a complex of
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic sheaves. Thus, we can obtain the following consequences for
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
:

Corollary 5.3.2
.

Assume
X
is affinoid or
X
is Stein and
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed. If
X
admits an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, then we have that
\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\neq 0
implies
\operatorname{dim}(X)\leq n\leq 2\operatorname{dim}(X)
. In particular, we have:

\operatorname{HH}_{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{H}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=0.

Furthermore, in the Stein case we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}[2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Proof.

As mentioned above, in both cases
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
is a complex of acyclic sheaves of length
\operatorname{dim}(X)
. In particular,
R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})
can only have non-trivial cohomology in degrees contained between
0
and
\operatorname{dim}(X)
. Thus, the previous theorem shows that
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is also concentrated in degrees between
-2\operatorname{dim}(X)
and
-\operatorname{dim}(X)
. The second part of the corollary follows by Theorem
4.1.2
.
∎

6.
Deformation Theory

The application of Hochschild cohomology to the study of deformation theory of associative algebras was started by M. Gerstenhaber in
[
16
]
. In this chapter, we will translate some of his results to the realm of complete bornological algebras. The main obstacle in doing this is that the classical Hochschild cohomology of associative algebras is based upon explicit calculations with the bar complex, whereas our approach is purely homological in nature. In the setting of associative
K
-algebras, every object appearing in the bar resolution of an algebra is projective, and therefore both notions agree. However, this is no longer the case for complete bornological algebras.

In order to bridge this gap, we will establish some comparison results between our notion of Hochschild cohomology, and the one emerging from the bar resolution. In particular, we will build a first quadrant spectral sequence connecting these two cohomology theories, and use it to obtain some explicit calculations. We point out that a first approach to Hochschild cohomology using explicit complexes was first conveyed by J.L. Taylor in
[
47
]
. In particular, Taylor develops a theory of relative homological algebra for locally convex algebras based upon the use of resolutions which are split-exact as complexes of locally convex spaces. While Taylor’s version of homological algebra lacks the flexibility of the machinery of quasi-abelian categories, it still has proven to be a powerful tool, and has found several applications in
p
-adic representation theory in recent years. For instance, in J. Kohlhaase’s work on the cohomology of locally analytic representations
[
29
]
, and in T. Schmidt’s work on stable flatness of non-archimedean hyperenveloping algebras
[
41
]
. While Taylor’s approach lacks some of the good homological properties
that our definition of Hochschild cohomology enjoys, an advantage of working with the bar resolution is that there are explicit interpretations of the Hochschild cohomology groups in low degrees. Furthermore, these interpretations turn out to be analytic versions of the classical Hochschild cohomology groups of an associative algebra.

Once the basic theory has been established, we will focus on the particular case of the algebra of infinite order differential operators. In particular, if
X
is a smooth Stein space equipped with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
, we will use the Hochschild cohomology spaces
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
to study certain algebraic invariants of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. Namely, we will focus on the center, the space of (bounded) outer derivations, and the space of infinitesimal deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. Furthermore, if
X
has finite-dimensional de Rham cohomology, we will be able to obtain the following explicit isomorphisms:

\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}=\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),\,\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),\,\operatorname{H}^{2}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}=\mathscr{E}xt(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

which are a
p
-adic analytic version of the classical identities for smooth affine
K
-varieties.

Along the way, we will see how the algebras of infinite order twisted differential operators studied in
[
35
]
can be used to construct explicit representatives for each isomorphism class of infinitesimal deformation of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. Furthermore, this construction is analogous to the algebraic case.

6.1.
Taylor’s homological algebra

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra, and

\mathscr{A}^{e}:=\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}},

be its enveloping algebra. Recall from
[
47
, Section 2]
the bar resolution of
\mathscr{A}
as a
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module:

(22)

B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\right).

This is a strict complex of complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-modules with a canonical quasi-isomorphism:

B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}.

The augmented complex
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
is split-exact in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. Furthermore, regarding
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
as a complex of complete bornological
\mathscr{A}
-modules via the morphism of complete bornological algebras
\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}
, it can be shown that
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
is split-exact as a complex of
\mathscr{A}
-modules. Let
\mathscr{M}
be a complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. Applying
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(-,\mathscr{M})
to
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
, and using the extension-restriction adjunction, we obtain the following chain complex:

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\xrightarrow{\delta_{0}^{\mathscr{M}}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\xrightarrow{\delta_{1}^{\mathscr{M}}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{2}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\xrightarrow{\delta_{2}^{\mathscr{M}}}\cdots\right),

where in general we have:

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{n}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{n}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}),

and the differentials are given by the following formula:

(23)

\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}}f(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=a_{0}f(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}f(a_{0}\otimes\cdots a_{i-1}a_{i}\otimes\cdots a_{n})\\
+(-1)^{n+1}f(a_{0}\otimes\cdots a_{n-1})a_{n}.

Whenever there is no confusion possible with the algebra with respect to which we are regarding
\mathscr{M}
as a module, we will omit it from the notation, and simply write:

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}:=\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}.

If
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
, we will write
\delta_{n}:=\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{A}}
for each
n\geq 0
. We can use these complexes to define the following cohomology groups:

Definition 6.1.1

(
[
47
, Definition 2.4]
)
.

The Taylor-Hochschild cohomology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
are the following bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{Coker}\left(J(\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{n-1})\rightarrow J(\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}}))\right).

As before, if the complete bornological algebra
\mathscr{A}
is clear, we will drop it from the notation and write
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{M}):=\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
. For simplicity, we will call these spaces the
TH
cohomology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
. If
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
, we will call the
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
the
TH
cohomology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
.

Before moving on, let us make a few comments on the definition. First, choose some
n\geq 0
. Notice that before calculating the cokernel defining
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{M})
, we apply the functor
J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
. This is done because cokernels in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
do not commute with the forgetful functor:

\operatorname{Forget}(-):\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

In particular, consider the short exact sequence in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{n-1}^{\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}\left(\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{n-1}^{\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}})\right)\rightarrow 0.

If the morphism
\delta_{n-1}^{\mathscr{M}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{n}
fails to be strict, then it could happen that
\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{n-1}^{\mathscr{M}})
is not closed in
\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}})
. In this situation, the vector space underlying
\operatorname{Coker}(\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{n-1}^{\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}}))
is not isomorphic to
\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}})/\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{n-1}^{\mathscr{M}})
, but rather to
\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{M}})/\widehat{\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{n-1}^{\mathscr{M}})}
. As our main reason for introducing the TH cohomology groups is that they allow for explicit calculations, it would not make sense for us to introduce this completion process into the picture. Thus,
we define the cohomology groups as objects of
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
rather than of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
