. We remark that the bornology of
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{M})
can be poorly behaved. For instance,
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{M})
may not be separated. We can, however, show the following:

Proposition 6.1.2
.

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\mathscr{M})
is a closed subspace of
\mathscr{M}
. Thus, it is a complete bornological space.

Proof.

Follows at once from the fact that
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is separated and
\mathscr{M}
is complete.
∎

For the rest of the TH cohomology groups, their bornological properties need to be analyzed on a case-to-case basis. We will carry out this study for
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
in Section
6.3
.

Notice that we have not made any use of quasi-abelian categories in the definition. One reason for this is that working with the underlying
K
-vector spaces allows us to obtain the explicit interpretations of the TH cohomology groups. In fact, these interpretations are bornological versions of the ones for the Hochschild cohomology groups of associative algebras. This is a logical outcome of the fact that the bar resolution (
22
) is an bornological version of the classical bar complex. The second reason is a historical one, as the contents of
[
47
]
predate the notion of quasi-abelian category.

As advertised above, one of the main features of the TH cohomology spaces is that we have explicit interpretations of the spaces in low degrees. Our next goal is describing those interpretations.

Definition 6.1.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra and
\mathscr{M}
be a complete bornological bimodule. A bounded derivation of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is a bounded map
D:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}
satisfying the Leibnitz rule. That is, for
x,y\in\mathscr{A}
we have:

D(xy)=xD(y)+D(x)y.

We let
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
be the
K
-vector space of all bounded derivations of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
, and regard it as a (complete) bornological space as a subspace of
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
. The space of inner derivations of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
is the following
K
-vector space:

\underline{\operatorname{Inn}}(\mathscr{A}):=\{f\in\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\,|\,\textnormal{ there is }m\in\mathscr{M}\textnormal{ such that }f(a)=am-ma,\textnormal{ for all }a\in\mathscr{A}\}.

We regard
\underline{\operatorname{Inn}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
as bornological space with bornology induced by
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
.

With these concepts at hand, we can give a tangible interpretation of the TH cohomology groups:

Proposition 6.1.4

(
[
22
, Remark I.3.3]
)
.

The following hold:

(i)

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}):=\{m\in\mathscr{M}\textnormal{ }|\textnormal{ }am=ma,\textnormal{ for all }a\in\mathscr{A}\}
.

(ii)

\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{1}^{\mathscr{M}})
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Inn}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{0}^{\mathscr{M}})
.

(iii)

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\mathscr{A})=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}):=\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})/\underline{\operatorname{Inn}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward calculation using the formulas in equation (
23
).
∎

The interpretation of
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\mathscr{A})
is more involved, and requires us to introduce some concepts from deformation theory. We borrow the following discussion from
[
22
, Chapter I]
:

Definition 6.1.5

(
[
22
, Chapter I Definition 1.6]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a bornological algebra and
\mathscr{M}
be a
\mathscr{A}
-bimodule. A singular extension of
\mathscr{A}
by
\mathscr{M}
is a triple
(\mathscr{B},i,\sigma)
, where
\mathscr{B}
is a bornological algebra,
i:\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
is the inclusion of a square-zero ideal, and
\sigma:\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
is a morphism of bornological algebras such that there is a strict short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\xrightarrow{i}\mathscr{B}\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow 0,

and such that
\sigma:\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
admits a bounded
K
-linear split
\rho:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
. If
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
, we will call the triple
(\mathscr{B},i,\sigma)
a singular extension of
\mathscr{A}
.

Remark 6.1.6
.

We make the following remarks to the definition:

(i)

These extensions are also called Hochschild extensions or infinitesimal deformations in the literature.

(ii)

Notice that
\mathscr{B}
is complete if and only if
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{M}
are complete.

Until further notice, we let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra and
\mathscr{M}
be a complete bornological
\mathscr{A}
-bimodule. As singular extensions are split in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, we will often identify
\mathscr{M}
with its image
i(\mathscr{M})
. Two singular extensions
(\mathscr{B},i,\sigma)
,
(\mathscr{B}^{\prime},i^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
are said to be equivalent if there is a bounded morphism of
K
-algebras
f:\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
such that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}}

The trivial extension of
\mathscr{A}
by
\mathscr{M}
is the one defined by the product:

(24)

\left(\mathscr{A}\oplus\mathscr{M}\right)\otimes_{K}\left(\mathscr{A}\oplus\mathscr{M}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{A}\oplus\mathscr{M},\,((a_{1},m_{1}),(a_{2},m_{2}))\mapsto(a_{1}a_{2},a_{1}m_{2}+a_{2}m_{1}).

For reasons that will be explained below, we will denote the trivial singular extension of
\mathscr{A}
by
\mathscr{M}
as
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{M}}^{\epsilon}
. If, in addition, we have
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
, then we will write
\mathscr{A}^{\epsilon}:=\mathscr{A}^{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{A}}
.

Definition 6.1.7
.

We define
\mathscr{E}xt(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
as the pointed set of equivalence classes of singular extensions of
\mathscr{A}
by
\mathscr{M}
, with distinguished point given by the trivial extension
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{M}}^{\epsilon}
. As usual, if
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
, we will simply write
\mathscr{E}xt(\mathscr{A})
.

Given a singular extension
(\mathscr{B},i,\sigma)
with splitting
\rho:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
, we define the operator:

\omega(\rho):\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{M},\,(a,b)\mapsto\omega(\rho)(a\otimes b)=\rho(a)\rho(b)-\rho(ab).

It follows by a straightforward calculation that
\delta^{\mathscr{M}}_{2}(\omega(\rho))=0
. In particular,
\omega(\rho)
determines a cohomology class in
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
. Furthermore, it can be shown that this cohomology class only depends on the equivalence class of the singular extension
(\mathscr{B},i,\sigma)
, and not on the choice of splitting. Hence, we get a map:

\omega:\mathscr{E}xt(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}),\,\omega([(\mathscr{B},i,\sigma)])=[\omega(\rho)],

where
\rho
is any bounded
K
-split of
(\mathscr{B},i,\sigma)
. This map satisfies the following property:

Theorem 6.1.8

(
[
22
, Theorem I.1.10]
)
.

The map
\omega:\mathscr{E}xt(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is a bijection sending the distinguished point of
\mathscr{E}xt(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
to zero.

The previous bijection allows us to regard
\mathscr{E}xt(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
as a bornological vector space, and we will do so in the sequel without further mention. Furthermore, we make the following remark:

Remark 6.1.9
.

We remark that the contents of this section can be adapted to non-complete bornological algebras by using the non-complete projective tensor product
\otimes_{K}
in the definition of the Bar complex given in (
22
). Furthermore, Proposition
6.1.4
, and Theorem
6.1.8
also hold in this setting.

The next logical step is establishing a comparison between the TH cohomology groups defined in this section and the Hochschild cohomology groups we have been studying thus far. However, before we do that, we would like to put forward a slightly different perspective of the contents of this section: Let
K^{\epsilon}:=K[t]/t^{2}
be the ring of dual numbers of
K
. This is an affinoid algebra, whose associated affinoid space satisfies the following property: Let
X
be a rigid
K
-variety, then we have:

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Sp}(K^{\epsilon}),X)=\{(x,v),\,\textnormal{ where }x\in X(K),\textnormal{ and }v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K,x}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}K(x)\}.

In other words,
\operatorname{Sp}(K^{\epsilon})
co-represents the functor sending a rigid space
X
to a pair
(x,v)
, where
x
is a
K
-point in
X
, and
v
is a vector in the tangent space of
x
. This space (along with its
higher order versions
, the rings
K[t]/t^{n}
) plays a prominent role in deformation theory and, as we will see below, we can reformulate most of the concepts explained before in terms of
K^{\epsilon}
-algebras.

Notice that there is a canonical complete bornological algebra structure on
\mathscr{A}^{\epsilon}:=\mathscr{A}\otimes_{K}K[t]/t^{2}
given by the tensor product of algebras. Furthermore, this product is given by the formula (
24
) specialized to the case
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
. This is the reason why we denote the trivial singular extension of
\mathscr{A}
by
\mathscr{A}^{\epsilon}
. On the other hand, notice that a singular extension of
\mathscr{A}
by
\mathscr{A}
is by definition a complete bornological algebra structure on
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{A}\otimes_{K}K^{\epsilon}
satisfying that we have an identification of complete bornological algebras:

\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{B}/(t)=\mathscr{B}\otimes_{K^{\epsilon}}K.

This fact justifies the use of the term infinitesimal deformation, and links our theory with the classical deformation theory of associative
K
-algebras, as developed in
[
16
]
. Similarly, given two infinitesimal deformations
\mathscr{B}
, and
\mathscr{C}
, an equivalence between the associated singular extensions is nothing but an isomorphism of complete bornological
K^{\epsilon}
-algebras:

\varphi:\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{C},

which is the identity after reducing modulo
t
. As a consequence of Theorem
6.1.8
, it follows that every such isomorphism is of the form:

\varphi:\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{C},\,x+ty\mapsto x+t(d(x)+y),

where
x,y\in\mathscr{A}
, and
d:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
is an element in
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{1}
. Let us define the following objects:

Definition 6.1.10
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be an infinitesimal deformation of
\mathscr{A}
. An infinitesimal automorphism of
\mathscr{B}
is an equivalence of singular extensions:

\varphi:\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}.

We let
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathscr{B})_{\operatorname{Inf}}
be the group of infinitesimal automorphisms of
\mathscr{B}
.

We may use the above to classify the infinitesimal automorphisms of deformations of
\mathscr{A}
:

Corollary 6.1.11
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be an infinitesimal deformation of
\mathscr{A}
. There is a group isomorphism:

\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathscr{B})_{\operatorname{Inf}},\,d\mapsto(x+ty\mapsto x+t(d(x)+y)).

In particular,
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathscr{B})_{\operatorname{Inf}}
is an abelian group.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the proof of
[
22
, Theorem I.1.10]
.
∎

As in the classical case, it is possible to show that the space
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{3}(\mathscr{A})
is the space of obstructions to extending an infinitesimal deformation
\mathscr{B}
to a second order deformation (
i.e.
a complete bornological algebra structure on
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{K}K[t]/(t^{3})
which restricts to
\mathscr{B}
after quotienting by
t^{2}
). However, as we will see below, this space is harder to relate to our notion of Hochschild cohomology, so we will not give the details here. Let us also mention that, in the setting of Banach algebras,
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{3}(\mathscr{A})
has also been used to study the perturbation theory of
\mathscr{A}
. See
[
22
, I.2.2]
for a complete discussion. Finally, we point out that there is no known interpretation of
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
for
n\geq 4
.

Before moving on, let us introduce a homological variant of the previous discussion. First, notice that there is a canonical isomorphism of complete bornological algebras:

(\mathscr{A}^{e})^{\operatorname{op}}=(\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})^{\operatorname{op}}=\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\cong\mathscr{A}^{e}.

In particular, the quasi-abelian categories of left and right complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-modules are canonically isomorphic. In particular, we can regard the bar complex
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
as a complex of right
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-modules. Thus, for any complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module
\mathscr{M}
, we can apply the functor
-\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}\mathscr{M}
to the Bar complex to obtain the following chain complex:

\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\xrightarrow{\delta^{-2}_{\mathscr{M}}}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{2}\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{M}\xrightarrow{\delta^{-1}_{\mathscr{M}}}\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{M}\xrightarrow{\delta^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}\mathscr{M}\right),

where, in general, we have:

\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{-n}=\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{n}\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{M},

and the differentials are given by the following formula:

(25)

\delta^{-n}_{\mathscr{M}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes m)=a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes ma_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes m\\
+(-1)^{n}a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n-1}\otimes a_{n}m.

As before, whenever there is no confusion with our choice of
\mathscr{A}
, we will simply write:

\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}:=\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet},

and if
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
, we will write
\delta^{-n}:=\delta^{-n}_{\mathscr{A}}
for
n\leq 0
. We can now define the following homology groups:

Definition 6.1.12

(
[
47
, Definition 2.4]
)
.

The Taylor-Hochschild homology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
are the following bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{\operatorname{T}}_{n}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{Coker}\left(J(\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{-n-1})\rightarrow J(\operatorname{Ker}(\delta^{-n}_{\mathscr{M}}))\right).

As before, if the complete bornological algebra
\mathscr{A}
is clear, we will drop it from the notation and write
\operatorname{HH}^{\operatorname{T}}_{n}(\mathscr{M}):=\operatorname{HH}^{\operatorname{T}}_{n}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
. For simplicity, we will call these spaces the
TH
homology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
. If
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{A}
, we will call the
\operatorname{HH}^{\operatorname{T}}_{n}(\mathscr{A})
the
TH
homology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
.

Unfortunately, the TH homology spaces of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
do not admit a description as explicit as the TH cohomology groups. However, as we will see, they present better behavior with respect to homological algebra, and their relation with the Hochschild homology groups
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is much less convoluted than in the cohomological setting.

6.2.
Comparison with Hochschild (co)-homology

In order to compare the TH cohomology groups with the Hochschild cohomology spaces studied in previous sections, we need to translate these complexes into
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Until the end of the section, we let
\mathscr{A}
be a Fréchet
K
-algebra.

First, notice that every object in
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
is a finite complete tensor product of copies of
\mathscr{A}
. As
\mathscr{A}
is Fréchet, it follows by
[
8
, Proposition 4.25]
that:

I(\widehat{\otimes}^{n}_{K}\mathscr{A})=\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{n}I(\mathscr{A}).

By the same reasoning, it also follows that we have:

I(\mathscr{A}^{e})=I(\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})=I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}=I(\mathscr{A})^{e}.

Furthermore,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
has enough flat projectives stable under
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
(
cf.

[
8
, Corollary 4.17]
). Hence, it follows by
[
46
, Proposition 1.5.3]
that we have an identification of functors:

I(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(-,-))=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(-),I(-)).

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. We may use these facts to obtain the following identity of chain complexes in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\mathcal{L}_{I}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}:=I(\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet})=\left(0\rightarrow I(\mathscr{M})\xrightarrow{I(\delta_{0}^{\mathscr{M}})}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{B}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}),I(\mathscr{M}))\xrightarrow{I(\delta_{1}^{\mathscr{M}})}\cdots\right),

where in general we have
\mathcal{L}_{I}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{n}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{B}c_{K})}(\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{n}I(\mathscr{A}),I(\mathscr{M}))
for every
n\geq 0
.

We can now start introducing Hochschild cohomology into the picture. In particular, by definition of Hochschild cohomology of a complete bornological algebra, we have:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}):=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}).

As shown above, the bar resolution
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}

is a strict exact resolution of
\mathscr{A}
as a
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. Hence, we get the following identity:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet},\mathscr{M}).

We may use the fact that
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is a Grothendieck abelian category to give an explicit calculation of this complex. Namely, choose an injective resolution
I(\mathscr{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
. Then we have the following:

(26)

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\operatorname{Tot}_{\pi}\left(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{I(\mathscr{A})^{e}}(I(B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\right).

As a consequence of this discussion, we arrive at the following theorem:

Theorem 6.2.1
.

There is a first quadrant spectral sequence in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

E_{1}^{p,q}=\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{q}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{p}I(\mathscr{A}),I(\mathscr{M}))\Rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{p+q}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}),

which we call the Taylor-Hochschild spectral sequence. Furthermore, we have an identification:

E_{1}^{\bullet,0}=\mathcal{L}_{I}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet},

and we have
E_{1}^{0,q}=0
for all
q\geq 1
.

Proof.

The existence of the spectral sequence follows from equation (
26
). For the rest, notice that:

E_{1}^{0,\bullet}=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(K,\mathscr{M})=\mathscr{M},

and that
E_{1}^{\bullet,0}=\mathcal{L}_{I}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}
by definition of the sequence.
∎

Corollary 6.2.2
.

Let
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is an object in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}

(ii)

The map
\mathscr{M}\xrightarrow{\delta_{0}^{\mathscr{M}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is strict.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
. By construction of the Taylor-Hochschild spectral sequence, we have
E_{1}^{1,0}=0
. Hence, there is an isomorphism
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\rightarrow\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet})
. By the properties of the canonical morphism
I:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{L}\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
, it follows that the map
\delta_{0}^{\mathscr{M}}
is strict if and only if
\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet})
is a complete bornological space. Hence, the result is clear.
∎

In the general case, the complex
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is not necessarily strict. Hence, it is difficult to use the Taylor-Hochschild spectral sequence to deduce properties of the TH cohomology groups of
\mathscr{M}
. However, if
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is strict, then the situation is not as dire. The sequence also behaves exceedingly well in the case of Banach algebras:

Corollary 6.2.3
.

Assume
\mathscr{A}
is a Banach algebra and one of the two following conditions hold:

(i)

K
is discretely valued.

(ii)

\mathscr{A}
admits a dense subspace which is countably generated.

Then we have the following identification in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\mathcal{L}_{I}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}.

Proof.

If any of the two conditions above hold, then it follows by
[
8
, Lemma 4.16]
and
[
44
, Proposition 10.4]
that
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{p}I(\mathscr{A})
is a projective object in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
for each
p\geq 0
. In this situation, the spaces
\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{q}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{p}I(\mathscr{A}),I(\mathscr{M}))
are trivial for all
q\geq 1
, and the sequence degenerates.
∎

Let us now study the TH homology groups. Recall the dissection functor
\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
studied in Section
2.1
. By assumption, the algebra
\mathscr{A}
is (the bornologification of) a Fréchet space. Thus, it follows by Proposition
2.1.9
that for every
n\geq 0
we have:

(27)

\operatorname{diss}(B(\mathscr{A})^{n})=\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{n}\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})^{e}.

Thus, the complex
\operatorname{diss}(B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet})
is a strict exact resolution of
\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})
as a
\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})^{e}
-module. Furthermore, as
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
is exact in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, it follows by (
27
) that
\operatorname{diss}(B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet})
is in fact a strict exact flat resolution of
\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})
as a
\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})^{e}
-module. Hence, we have the following proposition:

Proposition 6.2.4
.

Consider the following complex in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

B^{\bullet}(I(\mathscr{A})):=I(B^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}))=\left(\cdots\rightarrow I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\right).

Then
B^{\bullet}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a flat resolution of
I(\mathscr{A})
. Thus, for each
\mathscr{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
we have:

I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{I(\mathscr{A})^{e}}\mathscr{N}=\left(\cdots\rightarrow\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{2}I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{N}\rightarrow I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{N}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}\right).

Furthermore, if
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
is the bornologification of a metrizable locally convex space, then we have the following identification in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=I(\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}).

Proof.

By Proposition
2.1.7
, the functor
I:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is strong symmetric monoidal, and by
[
8
, Proposition 4.21]
every object in the essential image of
I
is flat with respect to
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
. Hence, it follows by the previous discussion that
B^{\bullet}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a flat resolution of
I(\mathscr{A})
as a
I(\mathscr{A})^{e}
-module. On the other hand, for every object
V\in LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
and every
\mathscr{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
we have:

\left(V\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\right)\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})^{e}}\mathscr{N}=V\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{N}.

Thus, the first part of the proposition holds. Let
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
be the bornologification of a metrizable locally convex space. In this situation, we have the following identities:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}):=\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}\mathscr{M}=I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{I(\mathscr{A})^{e}}I(\mathscr{M})\\
=\left(\cdots\rightarrow\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{2}I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{M})\rightarrow I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{M})\rightarrow I(\mathscr{M})\right)=I(\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{\bullet}),

where the last identity follows by Propositions
2.1.7
and
2.1.9
.
∎

In most of the cases we are interested in, both
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{A}^{e}
are Fréchet-Stein algebras, and
\mathscr{M}
is a co-admissible
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. Hence,
\mathscr{M}
is a Fréchet space. In particular, it is metrizable, and therefore the conditions of the previous proposition are satisfied.

6.3.
The TH cohomology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)

Assume
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, and let
X
be a smooth Stein space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. In this section we will apply the previous theory to study the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. As shown above,
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is canonically a nuclear Fréchet algebra, so we may calculate its TH cohomology groups
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. Furthermore, as shown in Theorems
4.1.2
, and
4.3.6
, we have the following identification in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b},

which shows that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is (the bornologification of) a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. Hence, we are in the best possible situation, and we may start using the Taylor-Hochschild spectral sequence to obtain calculations of the TH cohomology groups:

Proposition 6.3.1
.

We have the following identities in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{0}(X)^{b}=\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

Notice that by the formulation of the Taylor-Hochschild spectral sequence, we have:

I(\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}\right)=\operatorname{Ker}(I(\delta_{0})).

As
I
is a right adjoint, it preserves kernels. Hence, we have:

\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}\right)=\operatorname{Ker}(I(\delta_{0}))=I(\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{0}))=I(\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))).

Thus, we have
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
, as wanted. The third identity is Proposition
6.1.4
.
∎

Working a bit more, we can get a similar result for
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. Let us start with the following:

Proposition 6.3.2
.

The following hold:

(i)

The map
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\xrightarrow{\delta_{0}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is strict with closed image in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. Furthermore, there is a decomposition of complete bornological spaces:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\oplus\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{0}).

(ii)

\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{1}\left(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}\right)=\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

By our previous calculations we have
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}
. As the latter is a complete bornological space, it follows by Corollary
6.2.2
that
\delta_{0}
is strict. In particular,
\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{0})
is a closed subspace of

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. Thus, we
have identifications:

\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{1}\left(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}\right)=\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

and statement
(ii)
holds. For the last part of claim
(i)
, choose a decomposition
X=\cup_{i\in I}X_{i}
, where each
X_{i}
is a connected component of
X
. Notice that
I
is always countable by definition of Stein space. Furthermore, by statement
(iii)
in Lemma
3.1.5
, each
X_{i}
is a Stein space with free tangent sheaf. As
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is a sheaf on
X
, we have the following identity of complete bornological algebras:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\prod_{i\in I}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{i}).

By definition of the center of an algebra, we have the following identity of bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\prod_{i\in I}\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{0}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{i}))=\prod_{i\in I}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{0}(X_{i})^{b}=\prod_{i\in I}K,

where the last identity follows because each
X_{i}
is connected. As each
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{i})
is a Fréchet space, and
K
is finite-dimensional, we can use
[
44
, Proposition 10.5]
to conclude that each of the inclusions:

0\rightarrow K\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X_{i}),

has a bounded split. Hence, we obtain the following decomposition in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\oplus\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{0}),

which is the identity we wanted to show.
∎

We can draw a plethora of conclusions from this proposition:

Corollary 6.3.3
.

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
as complete bornological spaces.

Proof.

Follows from the previous proposition, together with Proposition
6.1.4
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.4
.

If
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}
is finite dimensional then
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is a nuclear Fréchet space.

Proof.

If
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is finite dimensional, then we have a decomposition:

\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{0})\oplus\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

By the proof of Proposition
6.3.2
, the space
\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{0})
is isomorphic to a closed subspace of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, and therefore is a nuclear Fréchet space. Hence,
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is also a nuclear Fréchet space.
∎

Even though the isomorphism obtained in Corollary
6.3.3
is obtained via purely homological methods, it is possible to define an explicit
K
-linear map:

c_{1}:\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

which is an isomorphism in some situations. This construction involves showing that any closed
1
-form
\lambda\in\Omega_{X/K}^{1,cl}(X)
induces a bounded derivation on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. In order to simplify the calculations, we will make use of Corollary
6.1.11
, and instead show that
\lambda
induces an infinitesimal automorphism of the trivial deformation
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
. Hence, before defining the aforementioned map, we will study the structure of infinitesimal deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. Let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 6.3.5
.

We have the following short exact sequence in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

(28)

0\rightarrow E_{3}^{1,1}\rightarrow I(\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))\rightarrow\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet})\rightarrow 0.

In particular
E_{3}^{1,1}
is in the image of
I:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Thus, it follows that the morphism:

\delta_{1}:\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{2}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

is strict if and only if
E_{3}^{1,1}\rightarrow I(\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
is the image under
I
of a strict monomorphism. Furthermore, if this holds, then the following identity also holds:

\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet})=\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

The Hochschild-Taylor spectral sequence is a first quadrant spectral sequence with
E_{1}^{0,q}=0
for all
q\geq 1
. Hence, the existence of the short exact sequence
(\ref{equation Lemma comparison map second cohomology group})
follows from standard facts on spectral sequences (
cf
.
[
50
, Chapter 5]
). By
[
46
, Proposition 1.2.29]
, the essential image of
I:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is closed under sub-objects. Hence,

E^{1,1}_{3}
is in the essential image of
I
. Choose some object
F\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
such that
I(F)=E_{3}^{1,1}
. As
I
is fully faithful, there is a unique map
f:F\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
such that its image under
I
is the boundary map
E_{3}^{1,1}\rightarrow I(\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))
. Assume this map is strict. As
I
preserves strict short exact sequences, this implies that
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet})

is also in the image of
I
. By definition, we
have:

\mathcal{L}_{I}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}=I(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}).

Hence, the map
\delta_{1}
must also be strict. The converse is analogous. For the last part of the lemma, recall that the functor
J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
preserves strict short exact sequences. Thus, the fact that
\delta_{1}
is strict implies that we have:

\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)):=\operatorname{Coker}\left(J(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{1})\rightarrow J(\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{2}))\right)=J\left(\operatorname{Coker}\left(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{1}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{2})\right)\right)\\
=J(\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet})).

Thus,
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is a complete bornological space, and we have
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet})
.
∎

Ideally, we would like to show that the map
I(\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))\rightarrow\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet})
is an isomorphism. Equivalently, we would like to show that the term
E_{3}^{1,1}
is trivial. This is hard to do directly, as the explicit expression of
E_{3}^{1,1}
is rather complicated, and we have almost no understanding of the higher Ext groups
\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{n}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. However, there are some situations in which we can show that this holds. Namely, when the second de Rham cohomology group
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)
is finite-dimensional.

Indeed, the strategy is constructing a
K
-linear monomorphism:

\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\xrightarrow{c_{2}}\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

As before, we have the following identifications of complete bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)^{b}=\operatorname{H}^{2}\left(\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet})\right).

Thus, every cohomology class
\beta\in\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is represented by a
closed
2
-form
\omega_{\beta}\in\Omega^{2,cl}_{X/K}(X)
.

By definition of a
2
-form,
\omega_{\beta}

induces a
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-bilinear map:

\omega_{\beta}:\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)\oplus\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X).

As
\omega_{\beta}
is closed, it follows by the contents of
[
35
, Section 2.2]
that this induces a
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
-Lie algebra structure on the following
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-module:

\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X):=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\bigoplus\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X),

where the bracket of two elements
(f,v),(g,w)\in\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X)
is given by the following formula:

(29)

[(f,v),(g,w)]=(v(g)-w(f)+\omega_{\beta}(v,w),[v,w]),

and the anchor map is the projection onto the second factor. A Lie algebra such as the one described above is called an Atiyah algebra or Picard algebroid on
X
.

By
[
35
, Definition 2.3.1]
, we can use the Atiyah algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X)
to obtain an algebra of twisted differential operators
\mathcal{D}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X)
on
X
. These algebras are generalizations of
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
which carry a canonical filtration called the filtration by order of differential operators. Furthermore, it can be shown that their isomorphism class as filtered
K
-algebras is completely determined by the cohomology class of
\omega_{\beta}
in
\operatorname{H}^{2}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
(
cf
.
[
35
, Corollary 2.2.14]
). We will make extensive use of this property in our construction of the map
c_{2}:\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

By construction, the algebra of twisted differential operators
\mathcal{D}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X)
arises as a quotient of the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X)
. In particular, we can give a presentation of
\mathcal{D}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X)
in terms of generators and relations. To simplify notation, for every
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
we let
\mathbb{L}_{v}
be its image in
\operatorname{Sym}_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X))
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X)
is the quotient of
\operatorname{Sym}_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X))
by the following relations:

(30)

\begin{gathered}f\cdot\mathbb{L}_{v}=\mathbb{L}_{fv},\quad f\cdot g=fg,\quad 1_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}=1_{\operatorname{Sym}_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{\omega}(X))},\\
\quad[\mathbb{L}_{v},\mathbb{L}_{w}]=\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]}+\omega_{\beta}(\mathbb{L}_{v},\mathbb{L}_{w}),\quad[\mathbb{L}_{v},f]=v(f),\end{gathered}

where
f,g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
,
v,w\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
, and the symbol
\cdot
represents the product in
\operatorname{Sym}_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{\beta}}(X))
. Notice that if
\omega_{\beta}=0
we recover the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
.

The strategy now is defining a slight modification of this algebra, and use it to construct an infinitesimal deformation of
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
. Indeed, let
K^{\epsilon}=K[t]/(t^{2})
be the ring of dual numbers, and consider the following Fréchet-Stein algebra:

\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}:=K^{\epsilon}\otimes_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)=\mathcal{O}_{X}[t]/(t^{2}),

which is the algebra of rigid functions on the non-smooth Stein space:

X^{\epsilon}:=X\times_{\operatorname{Sp}(K)}\operatorname{Sp}(K^{\epsilon}).

Similarly, we can consider the following
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
-module:

\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)^{\epsilon}:=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)=K^{\epsilon}\otimes_{K}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X).

As
X^{\epsilon}
is not smooth, its tangent sheaf is not free. Thus,
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)^{\epsilon}
is not isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}_{X^{\epsilon}/K}(X^{\epsilon})
.

Definition 6.3.6
.

We define the following objects:

(i)

We define the following
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
-module:
\mathcal{A}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}\bigoplus\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)^{\epsilon}
.

(ii)

We define
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
as the quotient of
\operatorname{Sym}_{K^{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{A}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X))
by the following relations:

(31)

\begin{gathered}f\mathbb{L}_{v}=\mathbb{L}_{fv},\quad f\cdot g=fg,\quad 1_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}=1_{\operatorname{Sym}_{K^{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{A}_{t\omega}(X))},\\
[\mathbb{L}_{v},\mathbb{L}_{w}]=\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]}+t\omega_{\beta}(v,w),\quad[\mathbb{L}_{v},f]=v(f),\end{gathered}

where we have
f,g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, and
v,w\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. Notice that
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega}(X)
is canonically a
K^{\epsilon}
-algebra.

As mentioned above, the fact that
X^{\epsilon}
is not smooth implies that
\mathcal{A}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
is not an Atiyah algebra on
X^{\epsilon}
. Thus, technically speaking,
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
is not an algebra of twisted differential operators on
X^{\epsilon}
. However, our explicit construction allows us to show that it satisfies similar properties:

Lemma 6.3.7
.

We have the following canonical decomposition of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-modules:

\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)=\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\oplus t\mathcal{D}_{X}(X).

Proof.

Notice that, by definition, we have the following canonical decompositions of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-modules:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}:=K^{\epsilon}\otimes_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\oplus t\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)^{\epsilon}:=K^{\epsilon}\otimes_{K}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)=\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)\oplus t\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)

Hence, our decomposition follows by the fact that
t
commutes with every element in
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
.
∎

Recall that the map
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
endows
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
with a canonical bornology. Thus, by the previous lemma, we obtain a canonical bornology on
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
. Furthermore, it is easy to check that the product in
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
is bounded with respect to this bornology, and thus
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
is canonically a bornological algebra. Our interest in
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
stems from the following proposition:

Proposition 6.3.8
.

There is a map
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\omega_{\beta}}}\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
such that there is a singular extension:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\omega_{\beta}}}\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

Notice that reducing the relations in (
31
) modulo
t
we obtain the relations in (
30
). Thus, by Lemma
6.3.7
, reducing
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
modulo
t
yields the following short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow t\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\omega_{\beta}}}\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow 0,

where
\sigma_{\omega_{\beta}}
is a bounded morphism of bornological
K
-algebras. The next step is showing that
\sigma_{\omega_{\beta}}
has a bounded
K
-linear split. We do this by defining one explicitly. By assumption, there is an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. Thus, by the PBW Theorem
[
35
, Proposition 2.3.2]
, there are tangent fields
v_{1},\cdots,v_{r}\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
such that we have an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-modules:

\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)=\bigoplus_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0,r}}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\mathbb{L}_{v_{1}}^{\underline{n}(1)}\cdots\mathbb{L}_{v_{r}}^{\underline{n}(r)}.

This identity, together with Lemma
6.3.7
, induces the following decomposition of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-modules:

(32)

\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)=\left(\bigoplus_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0,r}}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\mathbb{L}_{v_{1}}^{\underline{n}(1)}\cdots\mathbb{L}_{v_{r}}^{\underline{n}(r)}\right)\bigoplus t\left(\bigoplus_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0,r}}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\mathbb{L}_{v_{1}}^{\underline{n}(1)}\cdots\mathbb{L}_{v_{r}}^{\underline{n}(r)}\right).

Hence, we can define a
K
-linear split to
\sigma_{\omega_{\beta}}
by the following formula:

\rho_{\omega_{\beta}}:\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X),\,a_{\underline{n}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{1}}^{\underline{n}(1)}\cdots\mathbb{L}_{v_{r}}^{\underline{n}(r)}\mapsto a_{\underline{n}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{1}}^{\underline{n}(1)}\cdots\mathbb{L}_{v_{r}}^{\underline{n}(r)}\oplus 0.

Next, notice that as
t^{2}=0
in
K^{\epsilon}
, it follows that
t\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
is a square-zero ideal in
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
. Thus, it has a canonical structure as a bornological
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
-bimodule. All we need to do is showing that
t\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
as a bornological bimodule. It suffices to show that for each
1\leq i,j\leq r
we have the following identity:

\rho_{\omega_{\beta}}(\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}})\cdot t\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}=t\rho_{\omega_{\beta}}(\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}).

If
i\leq j
, then the explicit basis of
\mathcal{D}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)
as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-module showcased in (
32
) makes this automatic. Thus, we may freely assume that
i>j
. In this situation, our choice of vector fields
v_{1},\cdots,v_{r}
implies that
[\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}},\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}]=0
in
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
. Hence, we have:

t\rho_{\omega_{\beta}}(\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}})=t\rho_{\omega_{\beta}}(\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}})=t\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}}.

On the other hand, using the relations in (
31
), we have:

\rho_{\omega_{\beta}}(\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}})\cdot t\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}=t(\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}})=t(\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}}+t\omega_{\beta}(v_{i},v_{j}))=t\mathbb{L}_{v_{j}}\mathbb{L}_{v_{i}}.

Thus, both expressions agree, and the proposition holds.
∎

Applying the bornological completion functor, we obtain the following Hochschild extension:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega_{\beta}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\sigma}_{\omega_{\beta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\rho}_{\omega_{\beta}}}

Furthermore, the proof of Proposition
6.3.8
shows that our choice of an ordered
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-basis
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{r}\}
of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
induces a
K
-linear map:

c_{2}:\Omega_{X/K}^{2,cl}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{2}),

defined by sending a closed
2
-form
\omega
to the bounded
K
-linear morphism:

c_{2}(\omega):\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\,(a,b)\mapsto c_{2}(\omega)(a\otimes b)=\wideparen{\rho}_{\omega}(a)\wideparen{\rho}_{\omega}(b)-\wideparen{\rho}_{\omega}(ab),

where
\rho_{\omega}
is constructed as in Proposition
6.3.8
. This map is rather hard to describe in general. However, as an upshot of the proof of Proposition
6.3.8
, we get the following formula:

(33)

c_{2}(\omega)(v_{i}\otimes v_{j})=\begin{cases}0&\textnormal{ if $i\leq j$}\\
\omega(v_{i},v_{j})&\textnormal{ if $i>j$}\end{cases}

Composing this map with the projection
\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
, we obtain a
K
-linear map:

c_{2}:\Omega_{X/K}^{2,cl}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Notice that, a priory,
c_{2}:\Omega_{X/K}^{2,cl}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
depends on the choice of an ordered basis for
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. However, this map is in fact independent of such a choice. Indeed, let
\omega\in\Omega_{X/K}^{2,cl}(X)
be a closed differential
2
-form. Notice that the construction of the algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
from Definition
6.3.6
is completely independent of the choice of a basis of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. In particular, a different choice of ordered basis of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
does not alter the product of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
, it only induces a different bounded split of the canonical projection:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X).

Furthermore, as shown in Proposition
6.3.8
, this map is obtained by reducing
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
modulo
t
. Hence, it is also independent of the basis of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. However, in this situation, Theorem
6.1.8
shows that isomorphism class of the infinitesimal deformation
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
in
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is independent of the choice of the split. Therefore, the map:

c_{2}:\Omega_{X/K}^{2,cl}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

is canonical. That is, it does not depend on the choice of an ordered basis of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
.

Next, we want to show that
c_{2}
descends to a
K
-linear monomorphism:

\displaystyle c_{2}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}

\displaystyle(X)^{b}\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

This is equivalent to showing that given a closed
2
-form
\omega\in\Omega_{X/K}^{2,cl}(X)
, its class
[\omega]\in\operatorname{H}^{2}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}
is trivial if and only if
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
is a trivial Hochschild extension. Let us now show that this holds:

Proposition 6.3.9
.

The Hochschild extension
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
is trivial if and only if
[\omega]=0
in
\operatorname{H}^{2}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}
.

Proof.

By definition, the extension
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
is trivial if and only if there is a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

where
\varphi:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
is an isomorphism of complete bornological algebras. Using the split:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\bigoplus t\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),

commutativity of this diagram means that for
x,y\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, we have the following identity:

\varphi(x+ty)=x+t(\eta(x)+y),

where
\eta:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is
K
-linear and bounded. Our goal is showing that
\omega
is exact. Namely, we will show that there is a form
\beta:\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
such that for all
v,w\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
we have:

\omega(v,w)=v(\beta(w))-w(\beta(v))-\beta([v,w]).

Next, recall that
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
has a canonical
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module structure. As the deformation
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
is trivial, it follows that
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
has a canonical structure as a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
-module. Namely, for a pair of elements
x+ty\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
, and
f+tg\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
, we have:

(x+ty)(f+tg)=x(f)+t(x(g)+y(f)).

We can use the morphism
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
to obtain a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
-module structure on
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
. Explicitly, for
x+ty\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)^{\epsilon}
, and
f+tg\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
, we have:

(x+ty)(f+tg)=x(f)+t(x(g)+y(f)+\eta(x)(f)).

In particular, the above formula induces a bounded
K
-linear map:

\eta(-):\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)).

In order to simplify the notation, given
x\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
, we will denote the bounded
K
-linear endomorphism
x(-):\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
also by
x
. Choose
v,w\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. Then we have the following chain of identities in
\operatorname{End}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]}+t\left(\eta(\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]}))+\omega(v,w)\right)=\varphi([\mathbb{L}_{v},\mathbb{L}_{w}])=[\varphi(\mathbb{L}_{v}),\varphi(\mathbb{L}_{w})]=[\mathbb{L}_{v}+t\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v}),\mathbb{L}_{w}+t\eta(\mathbb{L}_{w})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]}+t\left([\mathbb{L}_{v},\eta(\mathbb{L}_{w})]-[\mathbb{L}_{w},\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v})]\right)

.

Simplifying this expression, we arrive at the following equation in
\operatorname{End}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
:

(34)

\omega(v,w)=[\mathbb{L}_{v},\eta(\mathbb{L}_{w})]-[\mathbb{L}_{w},\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v})]-\eta(\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]}).

Thus, it suffices to show that the restriction of
\eta(-)
to
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
:

\eta(-):\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)),

is a
1
-form. Equivalently, we need to show that it is
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-linear and takes values in the subalgebra
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\subset\operatorname{End}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
.

Assume that
\eta(f):\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
is zero for all
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. In this situation, for every
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{End}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))
:

\eta(f\mathbb{L}_{v})=f\circ\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v})+\eta(f)\circ\mathbb{L}_{v}=f\circ\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v}),

so the restriction of
\eta
to
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
is
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-linear. On the other hand, fix some
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. Then, using the canonical identification:

\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)=\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)),

it follows that
\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v})\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
if and only if
[\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v}),f]=0
for all
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. We may repeat the calculations leading up to equation (
34
) to obtain the following:

0=\eta(v(f))=\eta([\mathbb{L}_{v},f])=[\mathbb{L}_{v},\eta(f)]-[f,\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v})]=[\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v}),f].

Thus,
\eta(\mathbb{L}_{v})\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, as we wanted to show. Unfortunately, in the general case it is not always true that
\eta(f)=0
for all
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. However, we will see that we can replace our representation in a way that the previous developments hold. In order to do this, we will use some techniques from classical deformation theory of associative algebras. In particular, until the end of the proof, the symbol
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(-)
will denote the Hochschild cohomology of associative algebras, and the
Ext
functors are calculated in the corresponding categories of modules.

First, choose an ordered
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-basis of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. As shown above, this induces a split
\rho_{\omega}:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
. Notice that by construction
\rho_{\omega}
is an algebra homomorphism when restricted to
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. In particular, the trivial deformation
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}

is a subalgebra of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
. Hence, restriction along the inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)

endows
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
with a new
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
-module structure. Furthermore, using the formulas above, and the fact that this structure is
K^{\epsilon}
-linear, it follows that it is completely determined by the following formula for varying
f,g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
:

f(g):=\varphi(f)(g)=(f+t\eta(f))(g)=fg+t\eta(f)(g).

Denote this new module structure by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\varphi}
. Notice that, reducing modulo
t
, we recover the usual action of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
on itself. Thus, it follows that
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\varphi}
is an infinitesimal deformation of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
as a module over itself. As shown in
[
40
, Proposition 3.9.3.]
, the isomorphism classes of infinitesimal deformations of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
as a module over itself are parameterized by the following space:

\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\operatorname{End}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)))=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))=0.

In particular,
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\varphi}
is induced by the following
2
-cocycle:

\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{End}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)),\,f\mapsto\eta(f):\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(X).

The fact that its cohomology class in
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\operatorname{End}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)))
is trivial implies that this 2-cocycle is contained in the image of the zeroth boundary map of the Hochschild complex:

\operatorname{End}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),\operatorname{End}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X))),\,\psi\mapsto(f\mapsto\psi(f-)-f\psi(-)).

Thus, there is some
K
-linear endomorphism
\psi:\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
satisfying that:

\Psi:\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\varphi}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon},\,x+ty\mapsto x+t(\psi(x)+y),

is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
-linear isomorphism. Thus, we can use the isomorphism of
K
-algebras:

\operatorname{End}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\varphi})\rightarrow\operatorname{End}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}),\,f\mapsto\Psi\circ f\circ\Psi^{-1},

to obtain a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)
-module structure on
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
. As
\Psi
is
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
-linear, the action of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
on
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
obtained in this way is the canonical one. In particular,
\eta(f)=0
for all
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.10
.

There is a
K
-linear monomorphism:

c_{2}:\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{2}_{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),\,[\omega]\mapsto[\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{t\omega}(X)].

We remark that the previous result was first obtained in the setting of complex affine varieties by P. Etingof in
[
14
, Lemma 2.5]
, and that our proof closely follows his. We regard the fact that the algebraic proof can be adapted to the bornological setting as a hint at the idea that, once the proper homological tools have been established, the deformation theory of complete bornological algebras should behave like the classical deformation theory of associative
K
-algebras.

Finally, we can use these results to show the following theorem:

Theorem 6.3.11
.

Assume
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)
is finite dimensional. The following isomorphisms hold:

\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)^{b}=\mathscr{E}xt(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Furthermore,
c_{2}:\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{2}_{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

If the conditions of the theorem hold, then
\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space. Hence, by Lemma
6.3.5

the space
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is also finite dimensional, and we have:

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))\geq\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

However, as
c_{2}:\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{2}_{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is injective, it follows that
c_{2}
must be an isomorphism.
∎

As a consequence, we get the following two Corollaries:

Corollary 6.3.12
.

Assume
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)
is finite dimensional. Then there is an isomorphism:

\mathscr{E}xt(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\mathscr{E}xt(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Corollary 6.3.13
.

Let
X
be a smooth affine
K
-variety with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
.

The following identities hold:

(i)

\operatorname{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}}))
.

(ii)

\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}}))
.

(iii)

\mathscr{E}xt(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))=\mathscr{E}xt(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}}))
.

Furthermore, the space of bounded derivations
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{\operatorname{an}}}(X^{\operatorname{an}}))
is a nuclear Fréchet space.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Corollary
4.3.9
and the results of this section.
∎

Thus, we have completely clarified the structure of infinitesimal deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, at least in the case where
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)
is finite dimensional. For the general case, the arguments of Theorem
6.3.11
do not hold, and a complete treatment is out of the scope of this paper. However, let us mention a possible approach. First, notice that by the proof of
[
20
, Corollary 3.2]
, every smooth Stein space
X
admits an admissible cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
satisfying that
U_{i}\subset U_{i+1}
for all
i\geq 0
, and such that each
U_{i}
is a smooth Stein space with finite-dimensional de Rham cohomology. Second, the construction of the comparison map
c_{2}
from Corollary
6.3.10
is functorial, and does not require that
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2}(X)
is finite-dimensional. Hence, a possible approach to the general case could arise from establishing a theory of infinitesimal deformations at the level of sheaves, and using that de Rham cohomology parametrizes such deformations in a cover. This type of study has been carried out in the setting of smooth algebraic
K
-varieties by A. Vitanov in
[
49
]
.

As we advertised at the beginning of the section, we will now use our understanding of the infinitesimal deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
to construct a
K
-linear comparison map:

c_{1}:\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Let us start with the following proposition:

Proposition 6.3.14
.

Every
\lambda\in\Omega_{X/K}^{1,cl}(X)
induces a unique bounded
K
-linear derivation:

c_{1}(\lambda):\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),

defined for all
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, and all
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
by the following formulas:

c_{1}(\lambda)(f)=0,\,c_{1}(\lambda)(v)=\lambda(v).

Proof.

Assume that the formulas given above make
c_{1}(\lambda)
a
K
-linear derivation. Then
c_{1}(\lambda)
is bounded. As
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
is dense in
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
,
it follows that
c_{1}(\lambda)
is completely determined by its values on
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
. However,
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
is generated as an algebra by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
and
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
, so the formulas above uniquely determine
c_{1}(\lambda)
. Further, by definition of a derivation, for
x,y\in\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
we have:

c_{1}(\lambda)(xy)=xc_{1}(\lambda)(y)+c_{1}(\lambda)(x)y.

Thus,
c_{1}(\lambda)
restricts to a bounded
K
-linear derivation:

c_{1}(\lambda):\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X).

Thus, we only need to show that the formulas above make
c_{1}(\lambda)
a
K
-linear derivation on
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
. By Corollary
6.1.11
, it suffices to construct an infinitesimal automorphism:

\varphi:\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon},

satisfying that for
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, and
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
we have:

\varphi(f)=f,\,\varphi(\mathbb{L}_{v})=\mathbb{L}_{v}+t\lambda(v).

The advantage of this formulation is that we have a presentation of
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
in terms of generators and relations, which makes it easier to define algebra homomorphisms. Indeed, as discussed above,

\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
is the quotient of
\operatorname{Sym}_{K^{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X))

by the relations in equation (
31
).

Consider the following
K^{\epsilon}
-linear automorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X)
:

\varphi:\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X),

given by
\varphi(f)=f
, and
\varphi(\mathbb{L}_{v})=\mathbb{L}_{v}+t\lambda(v)
for
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, and
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. By the properties of
\operatorname{Sym}_{K^{\epsilon}}(-)
, this extends to an automorphism of
K^{\epsilon}
-algebras
\varphi:\operatorname{Sym}_{K^{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{Sym}_{K^{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X))
, so we may consider the composition:

\varphi:(\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{Sym}_{K^{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{A}_{t0}(X))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}.

Hence,
\varphi
induces an infinitesimal automorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
if and only if
\varphi
preserves the relations in equation (
31
). This follows by straightforward calculations, so we only show this for the more complicated one. Indeed, we have the following identities in
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
:

\varphi(\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]})-\varphi([\mathbb{L}_{v},\mathbb{L}_{w}])=\mathbb{L}_{[v,w]}+t\lambda([v,w])-[\mathbb{L}_{v}+t\lambda(v),\mathbb{L}_{w}+t\lambda(w)]\\
=t\left(\lambda([v,w])-v(\lambda(w))+w(\lambda(v))\right)=0,

where the last identity follows because
\lambda
is closed. Hence,
\varphi:\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)^{\epsilon}
is an infinitesimal automorphism. Again, in virtue of Corollary
6.1.11
, it follows that for
x,y\in\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
we have
\varphi(x+ty)=x+t(c_{1}(\lambda)(x)+y)
, where
c_{1}(\lambda):\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
is a bounded
K
-linear derivation, and by construction we have
c_{1}(\lambda)(f)=0
for
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, and
c_{1}(\lambda)(v)=\lambda(v)
for
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
.
∎

Hence, we obtain a
K
-linear monomorphism:

\Omega_{X/K}^{1,cl}(X)\xrightarrow{c_{1}}\underline{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}^{b}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Furthermore, if
\lambda=df
for some
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, then we have the following identity :

c_{1}(\lambda)(v)=\lambda(v)=df(v):=[v,f],

where
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. Thus,
c_{1}(\lambda)
agrees with the inner derivation induced by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. Therefore, we have shown that we have the following commutative diagram of strict short exact sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{X/K}^{1,ext}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{X/K}^{1,cl}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\operatorname{Inn}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\underline{\operatorname{Der}}}_{K}^{b}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{1}_{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

where the first two vertical maps are monomorphisms. We may use this to show the following:

Proposition 6.3.15
.

There is a
K
-linear monomorphism:

c_{1}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{1}_{\operatorname{T}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),\,[\lambda]\mapsto[c_{1}(\lambda)].

Proof.

In virtue of the previous commutative diagram, if suffices to show that given a closed
1
-form
\lambda
that is not exact, the associated derivation
c_{1}(\lambda)
is not an inner derivation. Assume that
c_{1}(\lambda)
is inner. In this situation, there is an operator
x\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
satisfying the following identity:

c_{1}(\lambda)=[x,-].

However, notice that, by construction, the derivation
c_{1}(\lambda)
is
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-linear. Hence, the operator
x
must commute with every element in
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. However, as shown in
[
34
, Proposition 7.5.2]
, this implies that
x\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. This, in turn, implies that
\lambda
is an exact form, a contradiction.
∎

As in the case of infinitesimal deformations, this result is most potent when applied to the case where
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}
is a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space. In this situation, we can show the following:

Corollary 6.3.16
.

Assume
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}
is a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space. Then the map:

c_{1}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{1}_{\operatorname{T}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

is a
K
-linear isomorphism.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.3.3
, and Proposition
6.3.15
,
c_{1}
is and injective
K
-linear map between two finite-dimensional
K
-vector spaces of the same dimension. Hence, it must be an isomorphism.
∎

Along these lines, we can also show the following:

Corollary 6.3.17
.

Assume
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}
is finite-dimensional. Then there is an isomorphism:

\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Proof.

By the construction above, the map
c_{1}:\Omega_{X/K}^{1,cl}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\underline{\operatorname{Der}}}_{K}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
factors as:

\Omega_{X/K}^{1,cl}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\underline{\operatorname{Der}}}_{K}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\underline{\operatorname{Der}}}_{K}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Thus, we get an induced map
\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
, which must be a surjection because
c_{1}:\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is an isomorphism. We just need to show that this map is injective. As a consequence of the snake Lemma, it follows that
\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathcal{D}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is injective if every
f\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
such that
fx-xf\in\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
satisfies
f\in\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
.

By assumption, there is an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{K}
. Thus, there are functions
x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
with associated vector fields
\partial_{x_{1}},\cdots,\partial_{x_{r}}\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
such that:

\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\partial_{x_{i}},\quad[\partial_{x_{i}},\partial_{x_{j}}]=0,\textnormal{ and }\partial_{x_{i}}(x_{j})=\delta_{i,j}.

Notice that, under these conditions, we have the following identity for each
j\geq 0
, and each
1\leq i\leq n
:

(35)

x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}^{j+1}-\partial_{x_{i}}^{j+1}x_{i}=(j+1)\partial_{x_{i}}^{j}.

Choose
f\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
satisfying the conditions above. Then
f
has the following form:

f=\sum_{I\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}a_{I}\partial_{x_{1}}^{I(1)}\cdots\partial_{x_{r}}^{I(r)}.

Choose some
1\leq i\leq r
. By assumption, we have:

\partial_{x_{i}}f-f\partial_{x_{i}}=\sum_{I\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\left(\partial_{x_{i}}a_{I}\partial_{x_{1}}^{I(1)}\cdots\partial_{x_{r}}^{I(r)}-a_{I}\partial_{x_{1}}^{I(1)}\cdots\partial_{x_{r}}^{I(r)}\partial_{x_{i}}\right)=\sum_{I\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\partial_{x_{i}}(a_{I})\partial_{x_{1}}^{I(1)}\cdots\partial_{x_{r}}^{I(r)}\partial_{x_{i}}.

As this must be contained in
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
, it follows that
\partial_{x_{i}}(a_{I})=0
for sufficiently big
I\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
. As we chose
i
to be arbitrary, and
\partial_{x_{1}},\cdots,\partial_{x_{r}}
are a basis of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
, it follows that
a_{I}\in K
for sufficiently big
I\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
. Again, choose some
1\leq i\leq r
. We may rewrite
f
as follows:

(36)

f=\sum_{n\geq 0}f_{n}\partial_{x_{i}}^{n},

where each of the
f_{n}
does not contain any
\partial_{x_{i}}^{n}
. Furthermore, this decomposition is unique. Notice that by equation (
35
), we have
f_{n}x_{i}-x_{i}f_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 0
. Hence, we have:

x_{i}f-fx_{i}=\sum_{n\geq 0}(n+1)f_{n+1}\partial_{x_{i}}^{n}.

Again, as this must be contained in
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
, it follows that
f_{n}=0
for big enough
n\geq 0
. The fact that we choose an arbitrary
1\leq i\leq r
, and that the decomposition in (
36
) is unique implies that
a_{I}=0
for sufficiently high
I
, as we wanted to show.
∎

Finally, let us mention that for
n\geq 3
the situation is much more convoluted. For instance, the edge morphisms:

I(\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))\rightarrow\operatorname{H}^{n}(\mathcal{L}_{I}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}),

are not necessarily epimorphisms. Thus, even if all the de Rham cohomology groups are finite-dimensional, it is not clear if the maps
\delta_{n}
are strict for
n\geq 3
.

6.4.
The TH homology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)

Keeping the assumptions of the previous section, the next step will be carrying out the previous discussion for the TH homology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. First, recall that, in virtue of Proposition
6.2.4
, the fact that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is a Fréchet space implies that we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}.

Therefore, we may use the results of Section
5
to show the following proposition:

Proposition 6.4.1
.

The Taylor-Hochschild homology complex:

\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\xrightarrow{\delta^{-2}}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{2}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\xrightarrow{\delta^{-1}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\xrightarrow{\delta^{0}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\right),

is a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. In particular, for each
n\geq 0
there is an isomorphism:

\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n}(X)^{b}=\operatorname{HH}_{n}^{\operatorname{T}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

and all the spaces are also nuclear Fréchet spaces.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
is a complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces follows by the fact that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is a nuclear Fréchet space, together with the fact that being nuclear and Fréchet is preserved by
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
(
cf.

[
44
, Proposition 19.11]
). As mentioned above, we have an identification:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}

Thus, we may use this, together with Theorem
5.3.1
to obtain the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}=\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{b}[2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

As the rightmost complex is a strict complex, it follows that
\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
is also strict.
∎

Thus, as advertised above, the relation between the TH homology groups and the Hochschild homology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is much simpler than in the cohomological case. Namely, they always agree. Even if the spaces
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
do not have a direct interpretation in terms of deformation theory, the fact that they can be calculated using the complex
\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
makes them easier to manipulate. In particular, many of the classical operations on Hochschild (co)-homology of associative algebras are defined in terms of explicit calculations using the complexes
\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{T}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
. For instance,
it is possible to define a cup product and a bilinear bracket on

\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
endowing
\operatorname{HH}_{\operatorname{T}}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
with the structure of a Gerstenhaber algebra (see the monograph
[
51
, Chapter 1]
for the definitions). The fact that the complex
\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
does not calculate
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
