implies that many of these constructions are not directly available for Hochschild cohomology. However, they are available in the homological setting and, in some situations, it is possible to use Van den Bergh duality to bypass the shortcomings of the TH cohomology complex
\mathcal{L}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{\bullet}
.

6.5.
Some operations on Hochschild cohomology

As an instance of this, we remark that it is possible to define a bornological version of the Connes differential on Hochschild Homology:

\mathcal{B}:\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))[-1].

Indeed, let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra, and let
\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
be its TH homology complex. The Connes differential will ultimately arise from a morphism of chain complexes of bornological spaces:

\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}[-1].

The operator
\mathcal{B}
is defined via a composition of several different maps. First, for each
n\geq 0
we define the following morphism of bornological spaces:

s_{n}:\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n-1},\quad s_{n}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})\mapsto 1\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n},

where
a_{0},\cdots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
. Next, notice that the group
\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)
acts on
\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}
by signed cyclic permutation of the tensor factors. Namely, the action is induced by the following map:

t_{n}:\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n},\quad t_{n}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})\mapsto(-1)^{n}a_{n}\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n-1},

which is often called the cyclic operator on
\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}
. We may use these actions to define a map:

N_{n}:=1+t_{n}+\cdots+t_{n}^{n}:\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n},

which is called the norm operator on
\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}
. Finally, we can condense all these maps into the definition of the Connes differential:

Lemma 6.5.1
.

For each
n\geq 0
consider the following bounded map:

\mathcal{B}_{n}:=(1-t_{n+1})s_{n}N_{n}:\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n-1}.

This map is given on simple tensors
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{-n}
by the following formula:

\mathcal{B}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{ni}1\otimes a_{i}\otimes\cdots a_{n}\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i-1}\\
-(-1)^{ni}1\otimes a_{i}\otimes 1\otimes a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i-1}.

Furthermore, these maps induce a morphism of chain complexes of complete bornological spaces:

\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}[-1],

which we call the Connes differential.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
31
, Section 2.1.7]
.
∎

As
\mathcal{B}
is a morphism of chain complexes, it induces a map on the Taylor-Hochschild homology:

Definition 6.5.2
.

We define the Connes differential on the
TH
homology to be the map:

\mathcal{B}:\operatorname{HH}^{\operatorname{T}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\operatorname{T}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A})[-1].

Back to our setting, we again let
X
be a Stein space with free tangent sheaf, and let
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
be its algebra of
p
-adic differential operators. In this situation, it follows by Proposition
6.4.1
that there is an isomorphism of complexes of nuclear Fréchet spaces
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\cong\operatorname{HH}^{\operatorname{T}}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. Hence, we obtain a version of the Connes differential for Hochschild homology:

\mathcal{B}:\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))[-1].

Remark 6.5.3
.

In fact, in virtue of Proposition
6.2.4
the Connes differential on Hochschild homology exists whenever
\mathscr{A}
is a Fréchet algebra. However, for arbitrary
\mathscr{A}
, the
TH
homology groups of
\mathscr{A}
do not agree with its Hochschild homology groups.

Using the fact that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
satisfies Van den Bergh duality, we have a quasi-isomorphism:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\cong\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))[2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Thus, we obtain an operator on Hochschild cohomology:

\Delta:\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))[-1],

defined as the unique morphism map making the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))[2\operatorname{dim}(X)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))[2\operatorname{dim}(X)-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}

In particular, for each
n\geq 0
we obtain a commutative diagram of complete bornological spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n-1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}_{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}_{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n+1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}

In the setting of associative Calabi-Yau
K
-algebras (
cf.
Definition
5.2.3
), the operator
\Delta
is called the Batalin-Vilkovisky operator on Hochschild cohomology, and it is studied in
[
18
, Section 9.3]
. We remark that, if
A
is a Calabi-Yau
K
-algebra, then the
the Gerstenhaber bracket, the cup product, and the Batalin-Vilkovisky operator on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A)
are
related via the following formula:

(37)

[\alpha,\beta]=\Delta(\alpha\smile\beta)-\Delta(\alpha)\smile\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\smile\Delta(\beta),

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are homogeneous elements in
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A)
. The Gerstenhaber bracket on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A)
is defined via an explicit formula at the level of chain complexes, so it is not possible to define it directly for complete bornological algebras. However, for any associative
K
-algebra, the cup product on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A):=R\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}(A,A)
is the Yoneda product, and this can be defined at the bornological level.

Indeed, the Yoneda product can be extended naturally to any closed symmetric monoidal category (see
[
8
, Section 3.3]
for the definitions). Let us now give a description of this construction:

Proposition 6.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category with tensor
\otimes
and inner homomorphisms functor
H
. Let
\mathscr{A}
be a monoid in
\mathcal{C}
, and let
H_{\mathscr{A}}
be the inner homomorphism functor relative to
\mathscr{A}
. Then for
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{A})
there is a canonical natural map:

H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1},M_{2})\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{2},M_{3})\rightarrow H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1},M_{3}),

which we call the composition map. Furthermore, for any
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{A})
, the object
H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)
is also a monoid in
\mathcal{C}
, which we call the endomorphism algebra of
M
(relative to
\mathscr{A}
).

Proof.

By the usual tensor-Hom adjunction, it is equivalent to define a map in
\mathcal{C}
:

H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1},M_{2})\rightarrow H(H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{2},M_{3}),H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1},M_{3}))\cong H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1}\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{2},M_{3}),M_{3}).

Applying the same adjunction again, this is equivalent to giving an
\mathscr{A}
-linear map:

M_{1}\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1},M_{2})\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{2},M_{3})\rightarrow M_{3}.

The counit of the tensor-Hom adjunction is the evaluation map, which yields two
\mathscr{A}
-linear maps:

M_{1}\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1},M_{2})\rightarrow M_{2},\quad M_{2}\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{2},M_{3})\rightarrow M_{3}.

Combining these two maps, we obtain a composition:

M_{1}\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{1},M_{2})\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{2},M_{3})\rightarrow M_{2}\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M_{2},M_{3})\rightarrow M_{3},

which is the map we wanted. Fix now some object
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{A})
. We need to show that
H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)
is a monoid in
\mathcal{C}
. First, we need to show that the obvious diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)}}

is commutative. This is a straightforward calculation using the same arguments as above. Next, let
1_{\mathcal{C}}
be the unit of the closed symmetric monoidal structure in
\mathcal{C}
. We need to show that there is an identity map
1_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)
satisfying that the composition:

(38)

1_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\rightarrow H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\rightarrow H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M),

is the identity map. We define the unit morphism as the unique map

1_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)
induced by the identity map
\operatorname{Id}:M\rightarrow M
and the following isomorphisms:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{A})}(M\otimes 1_{\mathcal{C}},M)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{A})}(M,M).

With this definition, the fact that the composition in (
38
) is the identity in
H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)
follows by the arguments above, together with the fact that:

M\otimes 1_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow M\otimes H_{\mathscr{A}}(M,M)\rightarrow M,

is the identity map of
M
.
∎

We may now specialize this construction to our setting to obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 6.5.5
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a Fréchet algebra. There is a well defined cup product:

\smile:\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A})\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}),

making
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A})
a monoid in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Furthermore, if
X
is a Stein space with free tangent sheaf, the cup product on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
descends to a cup product of graded complete bornological spaces:

\smile:\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

In particular
\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is a graded complete bornological algebra.

Proof.

First, recall that by
[
26
, Theorem 14.4.8]
and
[
8
, Corollary 4.17]
the closed symmetric monoidal structure in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
given by
-\widehat{\otimes}_{K}-
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(-,-)
extends to a closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
given by
-\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}-
and
R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(-,-)
. Next, as
\mathscr{A}
is a Fréchet algebra, it follows that
\mathscr{A}^{e}
is also a Fréchet algebra. In particular,
I(\mathscr{A}^{e})=I(\mathscr{A})^{e}
is a monoid in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
(
cf.
Proposition
2.1.9
). Furthermore,
I(\mathscr{A})^{e}
is flat with respect to
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
. Hence, we have:

I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}I(\mathscr{A})^{e}=I(\mathscr{A})^{e}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{A})^{e},

and therefore
\mathscr{A}^{e}
is a monoid in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Similarly,
\mathscr{A}
is a module over
\mathscr{A}^{e}
in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Thus, it follows by Proposition
6.5.4
that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}):=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A})
is a monoid in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
.

Let now
X
be a Stein space with free tangent sheaf. Then
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. In particular, it is a complex of flat modules with flat cohomology groups. Hence, it follows that for each
n\geq 0
we have:

\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\right)=\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\operatorname{HH}^{r}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{HH}^{s}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Hence, the product on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
descends to a morphism of graded complete bornological spaces:

\smile:\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

The fact that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is a monoid and the unit of the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is
K

imply that
\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is in fact a graded complete bornological algebra.
∎

Hence, we have shown that for any
n,m\geq 0
there is a bounded cup product:

\smile:\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{HH}^{m}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{n+m}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),

and a bounded Batalin-Vilkovisky operator:

\Delta:\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{n-1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)).

Using these two operators, together with equation (
37
), we can define a bounded Gerstenhaber bracket on the graded complete bornological space
\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
via the formula:

[\alpha,\beta]=\Delta(\alpha\smile\beta)-\Delta(\alpha)\smile\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\smile\Delta(\beta),

for
\alpha
, and
\beta
homogeneous elements in
\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
. We will postpone studying the properties of this bracket to a later paper. However, the previous discussion should convince the reader that most of the operations from the classical theory of Hochschild cohomology of associative algebras can also be developed in the complete bornological setting.

6.6.
Non-commutative differential forms I

In order to generalize some of our previous results to the case where the de Rham cohomology is not finite, we will need to use a bornological version of the space of non-commutative differential forms. Our exposition is based on the constructions of
[
12
]
, by J. Cuntz and D. Quillen. Until the end of the section, we let
K
be a complete non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra, and
\mathscr{A}^{e}
be its enveloping algebra.

Definition 6.6.1
.

The module of non-commutative differential forms of
\mathscr{A}
is the following complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module:

\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}):=\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathscr{A}^{e}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}\right),

where
\mathscr{A}^{e}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
is the unique
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-linear bounded map defined on simple tensors by
x\otimes y\mapsto xy
.

The idea is using
\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})
to simplify some of the calculations of Hochschild cohomology. Our strategy is based upon the following lemma:

Lemma 6.6.2
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra. Then the functor:

\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}^{e})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K},

is co-representable by
\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})
. In other words, there is a natural isomorphism of functors:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}),-)\cong\operatorname{Der}(\mathscr{A},-).

Proof.

The proof is follows the lines of
[
12
, Proposition 3.1]
. Let
\mathscr{M}
be a complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module, and recall from Section
6.1
the bar resolution of
\mathscr{A}
:

B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\right).

By Proposition
6.1.4
the space
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})
is the kernel of the following map:

\delta_{1}^{\mathscr{M}}:\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M}).

Recall that the bar resolution
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
is split as a complex of complete bornological
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-modules. In particular, it is strict exact. As
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(-,\mathscr{M})
is left exact, we get a chain of isomorphisms

\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\operatorname{Coker}\left(\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\right),\mathscr{M})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}),\mathscr{M}),

and this is precisely the identity we wanted to show.
∎

Thus,
\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})
enables us to pass from derivations to homomorphisms, and the latter are much easier to study. Let us mention that for each
n\geq 2
there are non-commutative analogs of the module of
n
-forms on a commutative algebra. These are defined via the following construction:

\Omega_{\operatorname{nc}}^{n}(\mathscr{A}):=\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}^{n}\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}),

where the
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module structure on
\Omega_{\operatorname{nc}}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
is given by the action of
\mathscr{A}
on the leftmost factor, and the action of
\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
on the rightmost factor. As shown in
[
12
]
, in the case of associative
K
-algebras these modules are intimately related to the Hochschild cohomology of
\mathscr{A}
. In particular, it can be shown that
\Omega_{\operatorname{nc}}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
co-represents
the functor of
n
-reduced Hochschild cocycles, and it is possible to define a DGA structure on the direct sum:

\Omega_{\operatorname{nc}}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}):=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\Omega_{\operatorname{nc}}^{n}(\mathscr{A}).

Although these constructions also make sense in the bornological setting, we will not pursue this study here. The reason behind this is that for
n\geq 2
the module
\Omega_{\operatorname{nc}}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
is too big, and thus the functor
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\Omega_{\operatorname{nc}}^{n}(\mathscr{A}),-)
does not simplify matters.

6.7.
Non-commutative differential forms II

As mentioned above, the goal is using the module of non-commutative differential forms to obtain more information on the Hochschild cohomology of the ring of complete
p
-adic differential operators on a smooth Stein space with free tangent sheaf. For future use, we will actually work in a slightly more general setting. Namely, until the end of the section, we fix a (two-sided) Fréchet-Stein algebra
\mathscr{A}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{A}_{n}
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

Every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathscr{A})
satisfies that the Fréchet-Stein presentation
\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}
makes
\mathcal{M}
a nuclear Fréchet space.

(ii)

Every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
satisfies that the Fréchet-Stein presentation
\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}
makes
\mathcal{M}
a nuclear Fréchet space.

(iii)

The isomorphism of Fréchet algebras
\mathscr{A}^{e}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{A}^{e}_{n}
is a Fréchet-Stein presentation, and
\mathscr{A}^{e}
also satisfies conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
.

Let us start the section with the following lemma:

Lemma 6.7.1
.

The module of non-commutative differential forms
\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})
is a co-admissible
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. In particular, there is a Fréchet-Stein presentation:

\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})=\varprojlim\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}_{n}),

and for each co-admissible
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module
\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}
there is a canonical isomorphism:

\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}).

In particular, the space of bounded derivations
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
is a Fréchet space.

Proof.

As shown in
[
34
, Section 7]
, our assumptions on
\mathscr{A}
imply that
\mathscr{A}
is a co-admissible
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module, and that for every
n\geq 0
we have a canonical identification:

\mathscr{A}_{n}=\mathscr{A}^{e}_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}\mathscr{A}.

As the category of co-admissible modules of a Fréchet-Stein algebra is abelian, and the map
\mathscr{A}^{e}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
is
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-linear, this implies that its kernel
\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})
is a co-admissible module. Furthermore, for every
n\geq 0
we have a short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{n}\rightarrow 0,

from which it follows that
\mathscr{A}^{e}_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})=\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}_{n})
. Thus, we have
\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A})=\varprojlim_{n}\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}_{n})
, as we wanted to show. Let now
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
be a co-admissible module. Then by Lemma
6.6.2
we have the following chain of identities of complete bornological modules:

\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}),\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim_{n}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}_{n}^{e}}(\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{nc}}(\mathscr{A}_{n}),\mathcal{M}_{n})=\varprojlim_{n}\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}),

where the third identity follows by
[
3
, Lemma 7.2]
. Thus, the lemma holds.
∎

With this lemma at hand, we can show the following proposition:

Proposition 6.7.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
be a co-admissible module. The following hold:

(i)

For each
n\geq 0
, we have
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})
, and this is a Banach space.

(ii)

There is an isomorphism of complete bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})=\varprojlim\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}).

Furthermore,
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
is a nuclear Fréchet space.

(iii)

Assume that we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\varprojlim\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})=\varprojlim\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}),

then there is an isomorphism of complete bornological spaces:

\mathcal{M}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\right),

and this inverse limit makes
\mathcal{M}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
a nuclear Fréchet space.

Assume in addition that
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
is an object of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. Then the following hold:

(iv)

For each
n\geq 0
, we have
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})
, and this is a Banach space.

(v)

There is an isomorphism of complete bornological spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})=\varprojlim\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}),

and this inverse limit makes
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
a Fréchet space.

Proof.

By definition, the Hochschild cohomology of
\mathcal{M}
is given by the expression:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M}):=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M}).

In particular, we have the following chain of identities:

\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}_{n}^{e}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})=\varprojlim\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}),

where, again, the second identity is a consequence of
[
3
, Lemma 7.2]
. Furthermore, by
Proposition
6.1.4
we have
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},-)=\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},-)
. In particular,
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is a closed immersion. By assumption,
\mathcal{M}
is a nuclear Fréchet space. Hence, it follows by
[
44
, Proposition 19.4]
that
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
is a nuclear Fréchet space as well. Thus, statements
(i)
and
(ii)
hold. For statement
(iii)
, notice that for any
n\geq 0
we have the following strict short exact sequence of Banach spaces:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{n}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\rightarrow 0.

By assumption, we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

R\varprojlim\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})=\varprojlim\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}).

In particular, there is a strict short exact sequence of nuclear Fréchet spaces:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow 0,

and we have
\mathcal{M}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim_{n}\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\right)
. Hence, claim
(iii)
also holds.

Assume now that
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
is an object in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. As shown in Corollary
6.2.2
, this holds if and only if the sequence:

(39)

0\rightarrow\mathcal{M}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})\rightarrow 0,

is a strict short exact sequence in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, in which case we have
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})\cong\underline{\operatorname{Out}}^{b}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})
. By Lemma
6.7.1
we have an isomorphism of Fréchet spaces
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim_{n}\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})
. Hence, it follows that the sequence in (
39
) arises as the inverse limit of the following short exact sequences of Banach spaces for each
n\geq 0
:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{M}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\rightarrow 0.

Hence, claim
(iv)
holds. By assumption, the presentation
\mathcal{M}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}
makes
\mathcal{M}
a nuclear Fréchet space. Hence, we may again apply the contents of
[
8
, Section 5.3]

to deduce that:

R\varprojlim\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\right)=\varprojlim\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})\right).

Thus, it follows that
\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M})=\varprojlim\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\mathscr{A}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n})
, and this shows
(v)
.
∎

Corollary 6.7.3
.

Assume
K
is either discretely valued or algebraically closed, and let
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
be a smooth Stein space with free tangent sheaf. Consider a Fréchet-Stein presentation:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{n}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)},

as in Theorem
3.2.3
. Then for each
n\geq 0
we have
\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})=\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{n})
. In particular,
\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})
is a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space. Thus, we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=R\varprojlim_{n}\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}).

In this situation, the maps
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}
induce a natural isomorphism of Fréchet spaces:

\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}),

and this implies that the map:

c_{1}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)),\,[\lambda]\mapsto[c_{1}(\lambda)]

is an isomorphism of nuclear Fréchet spaces.

Proof.

Using Kashiwara’s equivalence an the side-changing for bimodules developed in
[
34
]
, it follows that for each
n\geq 0
we have the following chain of identities:

\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}^{e}}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)},\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{n}))=\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{n}),

and this is a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space. As
\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=R\varprojlim_{n}\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})
is an inverse limit of finite-dimensional
K
-vector spaces, it follows by
[
43
, Section 2 Corollary 5]
that the sequence:

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\prod_{n}\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})\rightarrow\prod_{n}\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})\rightarrow 0,

is a short exact sequence of
K
-vector spaces, and that all the maps are bounded. As
\prod_{n}\operatorname{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)})
is a nuclear Fréchet space, it follows by
[
8
, Proposition 5.12]
that the sequence is strict. Hence, by Proposition
6.7.2
the maps
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}
induce a natural isomorphism of Fréchet spaces:

\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n,m(n)}).

Thus, the first part of the corollary holds. For the second part, we notice that by the proof of
[
20
, Corollary 3.2]
, we can consider an admissible cover
X=\cup_{m\geq 0}Y_{m}
such that
Y_{m}\subset Y_{m+1}
and each
Y_{m}
is a smooth Stein space with finite-dimensional de Rham cohomology. In this situation, it follows by the discussion above and
loc. cit.
that we have isomorphisms of nuclear Fréchet spaces:

\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\varprojlim_{m}\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(Y_{m})),\textnormal{ and }\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}=\varprojlim_{m}\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(Y_{m})^{b}.

Furthermore, the identity
\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))=\varprojlim_{m}\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(Y_{m}))
is induced by the restriction maps
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(Y_{m})
.
By construction of
c_{1}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(X)^{b}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
, it follows that
this map arises as the inverse limit of the maps:

c_{1}:\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(Y_{m})^{b}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(Y_{m})),

for each
m\geq 0
. However, by assumption,
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{1}(Y_{m})^{b}
is finite-dimensional, so by Corollary
6.3.16
this is an isomorphism for each
m\geq 0
. Thus, the result holds.
∎

As an upshot, we obtain a generalization of Corollary
6.3.4
:

Definition 6.7.4

(
[
44
, pp. 71 ]
)
.

Let
V
be a locally convex
K
-vector space. A
\mathcal{R}
-submodule
A\subset V
is called compactoid (in
V
) if for any open lattice
U\subset V
there are vectors
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\in V
such that:

A\subset U+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{R}v_{i}.

Corollary 6.7.5
.

Assume
K
is discretely valued or algebraically closed, and let
X
be a Stein space with free tangent sheaf. The space of bounded derivations
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is a nuclear Fréchet space.

Proof.

As the tangent space of
X
is free, we have a short exact sequence of Fréchet spaces:

0\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)/\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow 0.

By
[
8
, Lemma 5.4]
and
[
44
, Proposition 19.5]
, it suffices to show that for every Banach space
V
every bounded map
f:\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))\rightarrow V
is compact. That is, that there is an open lattice
U\subset\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
such that the closure
\overline{f(U)}
is compactoid. As
W:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)/\operatorname{Z}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))

is a nuclear Fréchet space, it follows by
loc. cit.
that every bounded map from
W
to a Banach space is compact. As the map
W\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is strict, this implies that there is an open lattice
U\subset\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
such that the space
\overline{f(U\cap W)}
is bounded and
c
-compact in
V
. Completing with respect to the norm induced by this lattice, we obtain a short exact sequence of Banach spaces:

0\rightarrow V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}\rightarrow V_{3}\rightarrow 0,

satisfying that the map
f
extends uniquely to a map
f^{\prime}:V_{2}\rightarrow V
, and such that the restriction of
f^{\prime}
to
V_{1}
is compact. By the previous corollary,
\underline{\operatorname{Out}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is an inverse limit of finite-dimensional spaces. Thus, it follows that
V_{3}
is finite-dimensional. Hence, the map
f^{\prime}:V_{2}\rightarrow V
is also compact, and therefore
\underline{\operatorname{Der}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is a nuclear Fréchet space, as we wanted to show.
∎
